────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Database application architecture refers to how the various components of a
database application — user interface, business logic, and data management — are
organized and distributed across computing resources.

TWO-TIER (CLIENT-SERVER) ARCHITECTURE:

In this model, the application is divided into two parts. The client machine runs
the user interface and the application/business logic, while the server machine
runs the DBMS. The client communicates with the server directly using database
protocols such as ODBC or JDBC.

  ┌─────────────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │    CLIENT               │              │     SERVER               │
  │                         │    SQL       │                          │
  │  User Interface  ───────│─────────────→│  Database Engine         │
  │  +                      │              │  (PostgreSQL, MySQL)     │
  │  Business Logic         │←─────────────│                          │
  │  (thick client)         │   Results    │  Data Storage            │
  └─────────────────────────┘              └──────────────────────────┘

THREE-TIER ARCHITECTURE:

In this model, a middle tier is introduced between the client and the database
server. The client handles only the presentation. The middle tier handles the
business logic. The database server handles data storage.

  ┌──────────┐    HTTP/API    ┌───────────────┐     SQL     ┌──────────────┐
  │  CLIENT  │ ─────────────→ │  MIDDLE TIER  │ ──────────→ │  DATABASE    │
  │  (thin)  │ ←───────────── │  (App Server) │ ←────────── │  SERVER      │
  │          │    JSON/HTML   │               │   Results   │              │
  └──────────┘                └───────────────┘             └──────────────┘

KEY DIFFERENCES:

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Two-Tier             │ Three-Tier                 │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Client type      │ Thick (contains      │ Thin (only presentation)   │
  │                  │ business logic)      │                            │
  │ DB access        │ Direct (SQL from     │ Indirect (via middle tier) │
  │                  │ client to server)    │                            │
  │ Scalability      │ Limited (each client │ High (middle tier can be   │
  │                  │ opens a DB connection│ load-balanced)             │
  │ Security         │ Lower (SQL exposed   │ Higher (DB behind          │
  │                  │ to client)           │ firewall, API only)        │
  │ Maintenance      │ Harder (update logic │ Easier (centralized logic) │
  │                  │ on every client)     │                            │
  │ Deployment       │ Must install client  │ Clients use web browsers   │
  │                  │ software on each PC  │ — zero installation        │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

WHEN TO USE EACH:

Two-tier is preferred when:
  • The number of users is small (< 50 concurrent users).
  • The application is used within a local network (intranet).
  • Rapid prototyping or small departmental applications.
  • Low-latency, high-throughput database access is needed (e.g., a desktop tool
    for data analysis).

Three-tier is preferred when:
  • The application serves a large, distributed user base (internet applications).
  • Security is critical (e.g., banking, healthcare).
  • Multiple client platforms (web, mobile, API) must be supported.
  • Scalability is required (thousands of concurrent users).
  • The application requires centralized business logic and maintenance.

In modern practice, three-tier architecture is the standard for virtually all web
and mobile applications, while two-tier remains in use for specialized desktop
applications and internal tools.



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 2 (a) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What is an ER Model? Define the following terms with examples:
         entity set, relationship set, participation, mapping cardinalities,
         and degree of a relationship set.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model is a high-level conceptual data model used to
design databases before implementing them in a relational DBMS. It was proposed by
Peter Pin-Shan Chen in 1976 and provides a graphical notation for representing the
logical structure of a database. The ER model describes data in terms of entities,
attributes, and relationships — concepts that map naturally to the real-world
objects and associations they represent.

ENTITY SET:
An entity is a real-world object or concept that is distinguishable from other
objects. An entity set is a collection of entities of the same type that share the
same attributes. For example, the set of all students in a university forms the
entity set "Student," and each individual student (Aarav, Bipana, Chetan) is an
entity in that set. Entity sets are represented as rectangles in ER diagrams.

  ┌────────────┐
  │  Student   │   Entity Set
  │            │   Attributes: roll_no, name, dept, semester
  └────────────┘

There are two types:
  • STRONG ENTITY SET: Has a primary key that uniquely identifies each entity.
    Example: Student identified by roll_no.
  • WEAK ENTITY SET: Does not have a sufficient primary key of its own and depends
    on a strong entity for identification. Example: "Dependent" of an employee is
    identified by (emp_id, dependent_name). Weak entities are shown as double-
    bordered rectangles.

RELATIONSHIP SET:
A relationship is an association between two or more entities. A relationship set
is a collection of relationships of the same type. For example, the "Enrolls"
relationship set connects Student entities with Course entities, representing the
fact that students enroll in courses. Relationships are represented as diamonds.

  ┌──────────┐     ┌───────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │ Student  │─────│  Enrolls  │─────│  Course  │
  └──────────┘     └───────────┘     └──────────┘

PARTICIPATION:
Participation defines whether every entity in an entity set must participate in a
relationship or if participation is optional.
  • TOTAL PARTICIPATION (double line): Every entity in the set MUST participate.
    Example: Every "Section" must be associated with a "Course" (a section cannot
    exist without a course). Shown as a double line ═══.
  • PARTIAL PARTICIPATION (single line): Some entities may not participate.
    Example: Not every "Student" may have an "Advisor" assigned yet.

MAPPING CARDINALITIES:
Mapping cardinalities define the number of entities in one set that can be
associated with entities in another set through a relationship. There are four
types:

  • ONE-TO-ONE (1:1): Each entity in A is associated with at most one entity in B,
    and vice versa. Example: Each department has exactly one department head.
  • ONE-TO-MANY (1:N): Each entity in A can be associated with many entities in B,
    but each entity in B is associated with at most one entity in A. Example: One
    department has many students, but each student belongs to one department.
  • MANY-TO-ONE (N:1): Reverse of one-to-many.
  • MANY-TO-MANY (M:N): Each entity in A can be associated with many entities in B
    and vice versa. Example: Students enroll in many courses, and each course has
    many students.

DEGREE OF A RELATIONSHIP SET:
The degree is the number of entity sets participating in a relationship.
  • UNARY (degree 1): A relationship between entities of the same set. Example:
    "Manages" relationship where an Employee manages another Employee.
  • BINARY (degree 2): Between two entity sets. Most common. Example: Student
    enrolls in Course.
  • TERNARY (degree 3): Between three entity sets. Example: A supplier supplies
    a part to a project (Supplier-Part-Project relationship).


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) Define the following terms in the context of the ER model:
         specialization, generalization, and aggregation. Illustrate each
         concept with a suitable example.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Specialization, generalization, and aggregation are advanced ER model concepts
that enable more expressive and accurate modeling of complex real-world scenarios.

SPECIALIZATION:

Specialization is a top-down design process where a higher-level entity set is
divided into two or more lower-level entity sets based on some distinguishing
characteristic. The lower-level entity sets inherit all the attributes of the
higher-level entity set and may have additional attributes specific to them.

Example: Consider an entity set "Person" in a university database. A Person can
be specialized into "Student" and "Employee" based on their role. Further,
"Employee" can be specialized into "Faculty" and "Staff."

  ┌──────────────┐
  │    Person    │   name, address, phone
  └──────┬───────┘
         │ ISA (specialization)
    ┌────┴─────┐
    │          │
  ┌─┴──────┐ ┌┴──────────┐
  │Student │ │ Employee  │
  │(GPA,   │ │ (salary,  │
  │ year)  │ │  hire_date│
  └────────┘ └─────┬─────┘
                   │ ISA
             ┌─────┴─────┐
           ┌─┴──────┐  ┌─┴──────┐
           │Faculty │  │ Staff  │
           │(rank,  │  │(position│
           │ pubs)  │  │  )     │
           └────────┘  └────────┘

Specialization can be:
  • Disjoint: An entity can belong to at most ONE lower-level set (e.g., a Person
    is either a Student OR an Employee, not both).
  • Overlapping: An entity can belong to MULTIPLE lower-level sets (e.g., a Person
    can be both a Student and an Employee — a teaching assistant).

GENERALIZATION:

Generalization is the reverse of specialization — a bottom-up design process where
two or more lower-level entity sets are combined into a single higher-level entity
set based on their common attributes. It abstracts away the differences between
the entity sets and focuses on their shared characteristics.

Example: If we initially designed separate entity sets for "Car" and "Truck,"
we might realize they share attributes (registration_no, manufacturer, year). We
can generalize them into a higher-level entity set "Vehicle."

  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          ┌──────────────┐
  │   Car    │  │  Truck   │   →→→    │   Vehicle    │ (generalized)
  │ (doors, │  │ (payload,│          │ (reg_no,     │
  │  type)  │  │  axles)  │          │  manufacturer│
  └──────────┘  └──────────┘          │  year)       │
                                      └──────┬───────┘
                                        ISA  │
                                      ┌──────┴──────┐
                                    ┌─┴──────┐ ┌────┴─────┐
                                    │  Car   │ │  Truck   │
                                    └────────┘ └──────────┘

Specialization and generalization are inverses of each other and result in the
same ER diagram. The difference lies in the design approach (top-down vs bottom-up).

AGGREGATION:

Aggregation is an abstraction that treats a relationship (along with its
participating entities) as a higher-level entity, which can then participate
in another relationship. This is necessary because the ER model does not allow
relationships between relationships directly.

Example: Consider a ternary relationship where an Employee works on a Project
under a Manager. Now suppose we want to track which Equipment is used in each
(Employee, Project, Manager) assignment. Without aggregation, we would need a
4-way relationship, which is unwieldy. With aggregation, we treat the "works_on"
relationship as an aggregate entity and relate it to Equipment.

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    AGGREGATION                            │
  │                                                           │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐            │
  │  │ Employee │────│ works_on │────│ Project  │            │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘            │
  │                       │                                   │
  │                  ┌────┴────┐                               │
  │                  │ Manager │                               │
  │                  └─────────┘                               │
  └──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
                    ┌────┴─────┐
                    │  uses    │
                    └────┬─────┘
                         │
                    ┌────┴──────┐
                    │ Equipment │
                    └───────────┘

Aggregation allows us to model complex situations where relationships need to be
related to other entities or relationships, providing a cleaner design than
attempting to flatten everything into a single multi-way relationship.


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What are keys in a relational database? Define super key, candidate
         key, and primary key with examples. Differentiate between strong
         entity sets and weak entity sets.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Keys are attributes or combinations of attributes that uniquely identify tuples
within a relation. They are fundamental to the integrity and relational structure
of a database.

SUPER KEY:
A super key is any set of attributes that, taken together, can uniquely identify a
tuple in a relation. A super key may contain extraneous (unnecessary) attributes.

Example: In the relation student(roll_no, name, dept, email):
  • {roll_no} is a super key.
  • {roll_no, name} is also a super key (but includes the unnecessary 'name').
  • {email} is a super key (assuming emails are unique).
  • {roll_no, name, dept, email} — the entire set of attributes is always a
    super key.

CANDIDATE KEY:
A candidate key is a MINIMAL super key — meaning that no proper subset of it is
also a super key. If you remove any attribute from a candidate key, it loses the
uniqueness property.

Example: In the same relation:
  • {roll_no} is a candidate key (minimal — removing any attribute from a single-
    attribute set would yield the empty set, which cannot identify tuples).
  • {email} is another candidate key.
  • {roll_no, name} is NOT a candidate key because {roll_no} alone is sufficient.

PRIMARY KEY:
The primary key is the candidate key chosen by the database designer to be the
main identifier for the relation. A relation can have multiple candidate keys, but
only ONE primary key is designated. The primary key cannot contain NULL values.

Example: If we choose {roll_no} as the primary key of the student relation, then
{email} becomes an alternate key.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Relationship among keys:                                         │
  │                                                                    │
  │  Super Keys ⊃ Candidate Keys ⊃ Primary Key                       │
  │                                                                    │
  │  Every candidate key is a super key, but not vice versa.          │
  │  The primary key is one chosen candidate key.                     │
  │  All other candidate keys are called alternate keys.               │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

FOREIGN KEY:
A foreign key is an attribute (or set of attributes) in one relation that
references the primary key of another relation, establishing a link between them.

Example: enrollment(roll_no, course_id, grade) — here roll_no is a foreign key
referencing student(roll_no), and course_id references course(course_id).

STRONG ENTITY SET vs WEAK ENTITY SET:

  ┌─────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect          │ Strong Entity Set       │ Weak Entity Set          │
  ├─────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Primary Key     │ Has its own PK that     │ Does NOT have a suffi-   │
  │                 │ uniquely identifies     │ cient PK on its own      │
  │ Identification  │ Self-identifying        │ Identified by combina-   │
  │                 │                         │ tion of its partial key  │
  │                 │                         │ (discriminator) + PK of  │
  │                 │                         │ its owner entity         │
  │ Existence       │ Independent             │ Dependent on the owner   │
  │                 │                         │ entity (existence-       │
  │                 │                         │ dependent)               │
  │ ER Notation     │ Single rectangle        │ Double-bordered rectangle│
  │ Relationship    │ Regular relationship    │ Identifying relationship │
  │                 │ (single diamond)        │ (double-bordered diamond)│
  │ Example         │ Employee (emp_id)       │ Dependent (dep_name) of  │
  │                 │                         │ Employee — identified    │
  │                 │                         │ by (emp_id, dep_name)    │
  └─────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What is a relational model? How do you reduce an ER diagram into a
         relational schema? Explain with an example involving at least three
         entities with different types of relationships.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

The relational model, proposed by E.F. Codd in 1970, represents data as a
collection of relations (tables). Each relation has a schema (name + attributes)
and an instance (set of tuples). The model uses relational algebra and relational
calculus as its query languages, and SQL as its practical implementation.

REDUCING AN ER DIAGRAM TO RELATIONAL SCHEMA:

The process of converting an ER diagram into a relational schema follows systematic
rules:

1. STRONG ENTITY SET → TABLE: Each strong entity set becomes a table. All simple
   attributes become columns. The primary key of the entity becomes the primary key
   of the table.

2. WEAK ENTITY SET → TABLE: Each weak entity set becomes a table with its own
   attributes PLUS the primary key of the owner entity (as a foreign key). The
   primary key is the combination of the discriminator and the owner's PK.

3. COMPOSITE ATTRIBUTES: The composite attribute itself is not included; only its
   leaf (component) attributes are included as separate columns.

4. MULTIVALUED ATTRIBUTES: A separate table is created with the PK of the entity
   and the multivalued attribute as a composite primary key.

5. DERIVED ATTRIBUTES: Typically NOT stored (computed at query time). Sometimes
   stored as a computed/virtual column for performance.

6. RELATIONSHIP SETS:
   • 1:1 — Foreign key can be placed in either entity's table (preferably the
     one with total participation). No separate table needed.
   • 1:N — Foreign key placed in the "many" side entity's table.
   • M:N — A SEPARATE JUNCTION TABLE is created containing the PKs of both
     participating entities as a composite PK, plus any descriptive attributes.

EXAMPLE: University ER diagram with Student, Course, and Department.

  ER Diagram (conceptual):
    Department ──(1)──── has ────(N)──── Student
    Student ───(M)──── enrolls ──(N)──── Course
    Department ──(1)──── offers ──(N)──── Course

  Reduced Relational Schema:

  1. department(dept_name PK, building, budget)
     — from strong entity "Department"

  2. student(roll_no PK, name, dept_name FK → department)
     — from strong entity "Student" + FK from 1:N "has" relationship

  3. course(course_id PK, title, credits, dept_name FK → department)
     — from strong entity "Course" + FK from 1:N "offers" relationship

  4. enrollment(roll_no FK → student, course_id FK → course, grade)
     PK = (roll_no, course_id)
     — from M:N "enrolls" relationship → separate junction table

The "has" (1:N) and "offers" (1:N) relationships are represented by placing
foreign keys in the "many" side tables (student and course respectively). The
"enrolls" (M:N) relationship requires its own table because a student can enroll
in many courses and a course can have many students.


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) Define the following ER model concepts with examples: participation
         constraints (total and partial), entity's role in a relationship,
         and descriptive attributes of a relationship.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

PARTICIPATION CONSTRAINTS:

Participation constraints specify whether the existence of an entity depends on
its being related to another entity through a relationship. They define the
minimum number of relationship instances each entity must participate in.

TOTAL PARTICIPATION (Existential Dependency):
Every entity in the entity set MUST participate in at least one relationship
instance. This means no entity can exist without being related to some entity in
the other set. In an ER diagram, total participation is shown by a DOUBLE LINE.

Example: In a university, every "Section" MUST be associated with a "Course" — a
section cannot exist independently without a course. The Section entity has total
participation in the "sec_of" relationship with Course.

PARTIAL PARTICIPATION:
Some entities in the entity set MAY participate in the relationship, but they
are not required to. In an ER diagram, partial participation is shown by a SINGLE
LINE.

Example: Not every "Student" has been assigned an "Advisor" (e.g., freshmen who
haven't selected an advisor yet). The Student entity has partial participation in
the "advises" relationship.

  ┌──────────┐         ┌───────────┐         ┌──────────┐
  │ Student  │─────────│  advises  │═════════│ Faculty  │
  │ (partial)│ single  │           │ double  │ (total)  │
  └──────────┘  line   └───────────┘  line   └──────────┘

  Here, every faculty member must advise at least one student (total),
  but not every student has an advisor (partial).

ENTITY'S ROLE IN A RELATIONSHIP:

The role of an entity in a relationship specifies the function that the entity
plays in the relationship. Roles are especially important in RECURSIVE
RELATIONSHIPS (self-referencing relationships) where the same entity set
participates more than once in the same relationship.

Example: In an "Employee" entity set, the "manages" relationship connects an
Employee (in the role of MANAGER) to another Employee (in the role of
SUBORDINATE). Without role labels, it would be ambiguous which entity plays
which part.

  ┌──────────┐  manager   ┌──────────┐  subordinate  ┌──────────┐
  │ Employee │────────────│ manages  │───────────────│ Employee │
  └──────────┘            └──────────┘               └──────────┘
  (same entity set participating in two roles)

Roles are labeled on the edges connecting the entity to the relationship diamond.

DESCRIPTIVE ATTRIBUTES OF A RELATIONSHIP:

Descriptive attributes are attributes that belong to the RELATIONSHIP itself,
not to any of the participating entities. They capture information that is
meaningful only in the context of the association between entities.

Example: In the "Enrolls" relationship between Student and Course, the attribute
"grade" is a descriptive attribute. Grade does not belong to the student alone
(a student has different grades in different courses) nor to the course alone
(different students get different grades). It belongs to the specific pairing of
a student with a course.

  ┌──────────┐            ┌──────────┐            ┌──────────┐
  │ Student  │────────────│ Enrolls  │────────────│  Course  │
  └──────────┘            └────┬─────┘            └──────────┘
                               │
                          ┌────┴────┐
                          │  grade  │  ← descriptive attribute
                          └─────────┘

When reduced to a relational schema, descriptive attributes become columns in
the junction table: enrollment(roll_no, course_id, grade).


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What is relational algebra? Explain the following fundamental
         operations with examples: select, project, union, set difference,
         Cartesian product, and rename.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Relational algebra is a procedural query language that forms the theoretical
foundation of the relational model and SQL. It consists of a set of operations
that take one or two relations as input and produce a new relation as output. Each
operation is closed — the result is always a relation, allowing operations to be
composed (nested).

Consider the following example relations:

  student:                              course:
  ┌─────────┬────────┬──────┐          ┌───────────┬────────────┬─────────┐
  │ roll_no │ name   │ dept │          │ course_id │ title      │ credits │
  ├─────────┼────────┼──────┤          ├───────────┼────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 101     │ Aarav  │ CS   │          │ CS301     │ DBMS       │ 3       │
  │ 102     │ Bipana │ IT   │          │ CS302     │ Networks   │ 3       │
  │ 103     │ Chetan │ CS   │          │ IT201     │ Web Dev    │ 4       │
  └─────────┴────────┴──────┘          └───────────┴────────────┴─────────┘

1. SELECT (σ):
The select operation selects tuples (rows) that satisfy a given predicate. It is
a horizontal filtering operation — it reduces the number of rows.

  Notation: σ_predicate(Relation)

  Example: Find all students in the CS department.
    σ_(dept='CS')(student)

  Result:
  ┌─────────┬────────┬──────┐
  │ 101     │ Aarav  │ CS   │
  │ 103     │ Chetan │ CS   │
  └─────────┴────────┴──────┘

2. PROJECT (π):
The project operation selects certain attributes (columns) from a relation. It
is a vertical filtering operation — it reduces the number of columns. Duplicate
rows in the result are automatically eliminated (since relations are sets).

  Notation: π_attribute_list(Relation)

  Example: Find the names and departments of all students.
    π_(name, dept)(student)

  Result:
  ┌────────┬──────┐
  │ Aarav  │ CS   │
  │ Bipana │ IT   │
  │ Chetan │ CS   │
  └────────┴──────┘

3. UNION (∪):
The union of two relations R and S produces a relation containing all tuples that
are in R, or in S, or in both. R and S must be union-compatible — they must have
the same number of attributes, and corresponding attributes must have compatible
domains.

  Notation: R ∪ S

  Example:
    cs_students = π_(name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student)) = {Aarav, Chetan}
    it_students = π_(name)(σ_(dept='IT')(student)) = {Bipana}

    cs_students ∪ it_students = {Aarav, Chetan, Bipana}

4. SET DIFFERENCE (−):
The set difference R − S produces a relation containing all tuples that are in R
but NOT in S. R and S must be union-compatible.

  Notation: R − S

  Example: Find students who are in CS but not in IT.
    cs_students − it_students = {Aarav, Chetan}

5. CARTESIAN PRODUCT (×):
The Cartesian product of R and S concatenates every tuple in R with every tuple
in S. If R has n tuples and S has m tuples, the result has n × m tuples.

  Notation: R × S

  Example: student × course produces 3 × 3 = 9 tuples, each combining a student
  record with a course record.

  The Cartesian product by itself is rarely useful; it becomes meaningful when
  combined with a selection (which gives us a join):
    σ_(student.dept = 'CS')(student × course)

6. RENAME (ρ):
The rename operation gives a new name to a relation or its attributes. This is
essential when performing self-joins (joining a relation with itself) or when
disambiguation is needed.

  Notation: ρ_new_name(Relation) or ρ_new_name(A₁,A₂,...)(Relation)

  Example: To find pairs of students in the same department:
    ρ_S1(student) × ρ_S2(student), then select where S1.dept = S2.dept
    AND S1.roll_no < S2.roll_no.

These six operations form the CORE of relational algebra. Additional operations
like natural join (⋈), intersection (∩), division (÷), and aggregation (𝒢)
can be derived from these fundamental operations.


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) Explain the process of reducing an ER diagram to a relational schema.
         How are the following handled during the conversion:
         (i) composite attributes, (ii) multivalued attributes,
         (iii) weak entity sets, (iv) many-to-many relationships?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Reducing an ER diagram to a relational schema is the process of translating the
conceptual design into an implementable set of tables (relations) in a relational
database. Each component of the ER model has specific conversion rules.

(i) COMPOSITE ATTRIBUTES:

A composite attribute is one that can be divided into smaller, meaningful sub-
parts. During reduction, the composite attribute itself is NOT included in the
table. Instead, only its LEAF (atomic) components are included as separate columns.

Example: The attribute "name" in a Customer entity is composite with components
(first_name, middle_name, last_name).

  ER Model:
    Customer: cust_id, name(first_name, middle_name, last_name), phone

  Relational Schema:
    customer(cust_id PK, first_name, middle_name, last_name, phone)

  The composite attribute "name" is flattened into its components.

(ii) MULTIVALUED ATTRIBUTES:

A multivalued attribute can hold multiple values for a single entity (e.g., an
employee can have multiple phone numbers). Since relational tables require atomic
values in each cell (1NF), a multivalued attribute cannot be stored as a simple
column.

Rule: Create a SEPARATE TABLE for the multivalued attribute. This table contains
the primary key of the entity as a foreign key, plus the multivalued attribute.
Together, they form the composite primary key.

Example:

  ER Model:
    Employee: emp_id, name, {phone_no}   ← multivalued

  Relational Schema:
    employee(emp_id PK, name)
    emp_phone(emp_id FK → employee, phone_no)   PK = (emp_id, phone_no)

(iii) WEAK ENTITY SETS:

A weak entity set does not have a primary key of its own. It has a
DISCRIMINATOR (partial key) that, combined with the primary key of its
identifying (owner) entity, forms the composite primary key.

Rule: Create a table with the weak entity's own attributes plus the primary key
of the owner entity. The PK is the combination of the owner's PK and the
discriminator. The owner's PK is also a foreign key.

Example:

  ER Model:
    Employee (strong): emp_id PK, name
    Dependent (weak): dep_name (discriminator), birth_date, relationship

  Relational Schema:
    employee(emp_id PK, name)
    dependent(emp_id FK → employee, dep_name, birth_date, relationship)
    PK = (emp_id, dep_name)

  If Employee "E1" is deleted, all their Dependents are also deleted
  (CASCADE on foreign key).

(iv) MANY-TO-MANY RELATIONSHIPS:

A many-to-many (M:N) relationship cannot be represented by simply adding a
foreign key to one of the participating entity tables (as we do for 1:N
relationships). Instead, a separate JUNCTION (BRIDGE) TABLE is created.

Rule: Create a new table containing the primary keys of both participating
entities as foreign keys. These foreign keys together form the composite primary
key of the junction table. Any descriptive attributes of the relationship are
also included.

Example:

  ER Model:
    Student ──(M)── enrolls ──(N)── Course
    Descriptive attribute: grade

  Relational Schema:
    student(roll_no PK, name, dept)
    course(course_id PK, title, credits)
    enrollment(roll_no FK → student, course_id FK → course, grade)
    PK = (roll_no, course_id)


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What are mapping cardinalities? Explain one-to-one, one-to-many,
         many-to-one, and many-to-many cardinalities with ER diagram
         notations and real-world examples.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Mapping cardinalities (also called cardinality ratios) express the number of
entities in one entity set that can be associated with entities in another entity
set through a relationship. They are one of the most critical aspects of database
design because they determine how relationships are implemented in the relational
schema.

1. ONE-TO-ONE (1:1):

Each entity in A is associated with AT MOST ONE entity in B, and each entity
in B is associated with AT MOST ONE entity in A.

  ER Notation:
    ┌──────────┐  1     ┌──────────┐  1     ┌──────────┐
    │ Employee │────────│  heads   │────────│Department│
    └──────────┘        └──────────┘        └──────────┘

  Real-World Example: Each department has exactly one department head, and each
  employee can head at most one department.

  Schema Implementation: Place the FK in either table:
    department(dept_id PK, dept_name, head_emp_id FK UNIQUE → employee)

2. ONE-TO-MANY (1:N):

Each entity in A can be associated with MANY entities in B, but each entity in
B is associated with AT MOST ONE entity in A.

  ER Notation:
    ┌──────────┐  1     ┌──────────┐  N     ┌──────────┐
    │Department│────────│ has      │────────│ Student  │
    └──────────┘        └──────────┘        └──────────┘

  Real-World Example: One department has many students, but each student belongs
  to exactly one department.

  Schema Implementation: Place the FK in the "many" side:
    student(roll_no PK, name, dept_id FK → department)

3. MANY-TO-ONE (N:1):

Each entity in A is associated with AT MOST ONE entity in B, but each entity in
B can be associated with MANY entities in A. This is simply the reverse of 1:N.

  ER Notation:
    ┌──────────┐  N     ┌──────────┐  1     ┌──────────┐
    │ Student  │────────│  has     │────────│Department│
    └──────────┘        └──────────┘        └──────────┘

  Real-World Example: Many students belong to one department.

  Schema Implementation: Same as 1:N — FK in the "many" side.

4. MANY-TO-MANY (M:N):

Each entity in A can be associated with MANY entities in B, and each entity in
B can be associated with MANY entities in A.

  ER Notation:
    ┌──────────┐  M     ┌──────────┐  N     ┌──────────┐
    │ Student  │────────│ enrolls  │────────│  Course  │
    └──────────┘        └──────────┘        └──────────┘

  Real-World Example: Students enroll in many courses, and each course has many
  students enrolled.

  Schema Implementation: Requires a JUNCTION TABLE:
    enrollment(roll_no FK, course_id FK, grade)
    PK = (roll_no, course_id)

Summary Table:
  ┌──────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Cardinality  │ FK Placement│ Example                                  │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1:1          │ Either side │ Employee heads Department                │
  │ 1:N          │ "N" side    │ Department has many Students             │
  │ N:1          │ "N" side    │ Many Students belong to Department       │
  │ M:N          │ New table   │ Students enroll in Courses               │
  └──────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What is the structure of a relational database? Define the terms:
         relation, tuple, attribute, domain, and degree of a relation. How
         is a relational schema different from a relational instance?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A relational database is organized as a collection of relations, where each
relation represents a table of values. The relational model provides a simple,
elegant, and mathematically grounded way of organizing data.

KEY TERMS:

RELATION: A relation is a named, two-dimensional table consisting of rows and
columns. Formally, a relation r on schema R(A₁, A₂, ..., Aₙ) is a subset of
the Cartesian product dom(A₁) × dom(A₂) × ... × dom(Aₙ). Each row is unique
(no duplicate tuples), and the order of rows does not matter.

TUPLE: A tuple is a single row in a relation. It represents one entity or one
fact. For example, in a student relation, the tuple (101, 'Aarav', 'CS', 5)
represents one student.

ATTRIBUTE: An attribute is a named column in a relation. It describes a property
of the entity that the relation represents. For example, "name" and "dept" are
attributes of the student relation.

DOMAIN: The domain of an attribute is the set of all permissible values for that
attribute. For example:
  • dom(roll_no) = positive integers
  • dom(name) = strings of length ≤ 50
  • dom(dept) = {'CS', 'IT', 'EE', 'CE', 'ME'}
  • dom(semester) = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8}

Domains enforce data integrity by restricting what values can appear.

DEGREE (ARITY): The degree of a relation is the number of attributes it has. A
relation with 4 attributes has degree 4. The degree is fixed once the schema is
defined and changes only if attributes are added or removed (ALTER TABLE).

  Example:
  ┌─────────┬────────┬──────┬──────────┐
  │ roll_no │ name   │ dept │ semester │   ← 4 attributes → degree = 4
  ├─────────┼────────┼──────┼──────────┤
  │ 101     │ Aarav  │ CS   │ 5        │   ← tuple 1
  │ 102     │ Bipana │ IT   │ 3        │   ← tuple 2
  │ 103     │ Chetan │ CS   │ 7        │   ← tuple 3
  └─────────┴────────┴──────┴──────────┘
                                           cardinality = 3

RELATIONAL SCHEMA vs RELATIONAL INSTANCE:

The RELATIONAL SCHEMA defines the structure — the relation name, attribute names,
and domains. It is the intension (blueprint) and changes rarely.
  student(roll_no: INT, name: VARCHAR(50), dept: VARCHAR(30), semester: INT)

The RELATIONAL INSTANCE is the actual set of tuples at a given time. It is the
extension (snapshot) and changes with every INSERT, UPDATE, or DELETE.

The schema is like a class definition in object-oriented programming, and an
instance is like the collection of objects created from that class at a particular
moment.


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (a) What is a schema diagram? Draw a schema diagram for an e-commerce
         database with at least five relations. Identify all primary keys,
         foreign keys, and relationships.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A schema diagram is a graphical representation of the database structure showing
all relations, their attributes, primary keys (typically underlined or marked),
and foreign key relationships (shown as arrows from referencing attribute to
referenced table).

E-Commerce Database Schema Diagram:

  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │        customer            │
  ├────────────────────────────┤
  │ cust_id (PK)               │
  │ cust_name                  │
  │ email (UNIQUE)             │
  │ phone                      │
  │ address                    │
  └────────┬───────────────────┘
           │ 1:N
           ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │         orders             │       │        product             │
  ├────────────────────────────┤       ├────────────────────────────┤
  │ order_id (PK)              │       │ prod_id (PK)               │
  │ cust_id (FK → customer)    │       │ prod_name                  │
  │ order_date                 │       │ category                   │
  │ total_amount               │       │ price                      │
  │ status                     │       │ stock_qty                  │
  └────────┬───────────────────┘       └────────┬───────────────────┘
           │ 1:N                                │
           ▼                                    │
  ┌────────────────────────────┐                │
  │       order_item           │                │
  ├────────────────────────────┤                │
  │ order_id (FK → orders)(PK) │                │
  │ prod_id (FK → product)(PK) │◄───────────────┘  M:N (resolved)
  │ quantity                   │
  │ unit_price                 │
  └────────────────────────────┘

  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │        payment             │
  ├────────────────────────────┤
  │ payment_id (PK)            │
  │ order_id (FK → orders)     │
  │ payment_date               │
  │ payment_method             │
  │ amount                     │
  └────────────────────────────┘

DDL for this schema:

  CREATE TABLE customer (
      cust_id    INT PRIMARY KEY,
      cust_name  VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
      email      VARCHAR(100) UNIQUE,
      phone      VARCHAR(15),
      address    TEXT
  );

  CREATE TABLE product (
      prod_id    INT PRIMARY KEY,
      prod_name  VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
      category   VARCHAR(50),
      price      DECIMAL(10,2) NOT NULL,
      stock_qty  INT DEFAULT 0
  );

  CREATE TABLE orders (
      order_id     INT PRIMARY KEY,
      cust_id      INT NOT NULL REFERENCES customer(cust_id),
      order_date   DATE NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE,
      total_amount DECIMAL(12,2),
      status       VARCHAR(20) DEFAULT 'Pending'
  );

  CREATE TABLE order_item (
      order_id   INT REFERENCES orders(order_id),
      prod_id    INT REFERENCES product(prod_id),
      quantity   INT NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
      unit_price DECIMAL(10,2) NOT NULL,
      PRIMARY KEY (order_id, prod_id)
  );

  CREATE TABLE payment (
      payment_id     INT PRIMARY KEY,
      order_id       INT REFERENCES orders(order_id),
      payment_date   DATE NOT NULL,
      payment_method VARCHAR(30),
      amount         DECIMAL(12,2) NOT NULL
  );

Relationships:
  • customer → orders: 1:N (one customer places many orders)
  • orders → order_item: 1:N (one order has many items)
  • product → order_item: 1:N (one product appears in many order items)
  • orders → payment: 1:N (one order can have multiple payments)

The order_item table is a junction table resolving the M:N relationship between
orders and products.



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 2 (b) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

NOTE: Questions 2(b) across all 10 sets ask to DRAW ER DIAGRAMS for various
real-world systems. Since this is a plain-text file, ASCII-art ER diagrams are
provided. In an actual exam, these would be drawn using standard ER notation
with rectangles (entities), diamonds (relationships), ovals (attributes),
double rectangles (weak entities), and double diamonds (identifying relationships).

For each set, the answer provides:
  1. Identification of entities and their attributes
  2. Primary keys for each entity
  3. Relationships with cardinalities
  4. Special elements (weak entities, composite/multivalued/derived attributes,
     specialization/generalization as applicable)
  5. The ER diagram in ASCII art


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (b) Draw an ER diagram for a ride-sharing application (e.g.,
         Uber/Pathao). Include entities such as Driver, Rider, Trip, Vehicle,
         and Payment.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Entities and Attributes:

  Driver:    driver_id (PK), name (composite: first, last), phone,
             license_no, rating (derived)
  Rider:     rider_id (PK), name (composite: first, last), phone,
             email, {phone_numbers} (multivalued)
  Vehicle:   vehicle_id (PK), plate_no, model, color, year
  Trip:      trip_id (PK), pickup_location, dropoff_location,
             start_time, end_time, distance, fare (derived from distance)
  Payment:   payment_id (PK), amount, payment_method, payment_date, status

  (Weak entity): Trip can be modeled as a weak entity identified by
  (driver_id, rider_id, start_time) but trip_id makes it strong.

Relationships:
  Driver ──(1:N)── owns ──────── Vehicle       (a driver owns one or more vehicles)
  Driver ──(1:N)── drives ────── Trip          (a driver drives many trips)
  Rider  ──(1:N)── requests ──── Trip          (a rider requests many trips)
  Trip   ──(1:1)── has ────────── Payment      (each trip has one payment)

ER Diagram:

                     ┌──────────────┐
                     │   Vehicle    │
                     │ vehicle_id PK│
                     │ plate_no     │
                     │ model, color │
                     └──────┬───────┘
                            │ 1
                       ◇ owns
                            │ N
  ┌───────────────────┐     │
  │     Driver        │─────┘
  │ driver_id PK      │
  │ /rating/ (derived)│─────┐
  │ license_no        │     │ 1
  │ name (composite:  │  ◇ drives
  │   first, last)    │     │ N
  └───────────────────┘     │     ┌──────────────────┐
                            ├─────│      Trip        │
  ┌───────────────────┐     │     │ trip_id PK       │
  │     Rider         │     │     │ pickup_location  │
  │ rider_id PK       │─────┘     │ dropoff_location │
  │ name (composite)  │  ◇ requests│ start_time      │
  │ email             │     1     │ end_time         │
  │ {phone} (multi)   │──────N────│ /fare/ (derived) │
  └───────────────────┘           └────────┬─────────┘
                                           │ 1
                                      ◇ has_payment
                                           │ 1
                                  ┌────────┴──────────┐
                                  │    Payment        │
                                  │ payment_id PK     │
                                  │ amount            │
                                  │ payment_method    │
                                  │ payment_date      │
                                  │ status            │
                                  └───────────────────┘

  Special elements shown:
  • Composite attribute: name → (first_name, last_name) for Driver and Rider
  • Multivalued attribute: {phone_numbers} for Rider
  • Derived attribute: /rating/ for Driver, /fare/ for Trip


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (b) Draw an ER diagram for an online food delivery system (e.g.,
         Foodmandu).
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Entities and Attributes:
  Customer:        cust_id PK, name, email, phone, address
  Restaurant:      rest_id PK, rest_name, cuisine_type, location, rating (derived)
  Menu_Item:       item_id PK, item_name, price, category, is_available
  Delivery_Person: dp_id PK, name, phone, vehicle_type, current_location
  Order:           order_id PK, order_date, total_amount, status,
                   delivery_time (derived)

Relationships:
  Customer ──(1:N)──── places ──────── Order
  Restaurant ──(1:N)── receives ────── Order
  Order ──(M:N)─────── contains ────── Menu_Item (with qty, subtotal)
  Restaurant ──(1:N)── offers ──────── Menu_Item
  Delivery_Person ──(1:N)── delivers ─ Order

ER Diagram:

  ┌──────────────┐   1    ┌─────────┐  N   ┌──────────────────┐
  │   Customer   │────────│ places  │──────│      Order       │
  │ cust_id PK   │        └─────────┘      │ order_id PK      │
  │ name, email  │                         │ order_date       │
  │ phone,address│                         │ total_amount     │
  └──────────────┘                         │ status           │
                                           │ /delivery_time/  │
  ┌──────────────┐   1    ┌─────────┐  N   └───┬──────┬───────┘
  │  Restaurant  │────────│receives │──────────┘      │
  │ rest_id PK   │        └─────────┘           N     │ N
  │ rest_name    │                         ◇ contains │
  │ /rating/     │   1    ┌─────────┐  N        │     │
  │ cuisine_type │────────│ offers  │──────┐    M     │
  └──────────────┘        └─────────┘      │  ┌──┴────┴───┐
                                           │  │ Menu_Item  │
  ┌──────────────────┐   1  ┌──────────┐ N │  │ item_id PK │
  │ Delivery_Person  │─────│ delivers │───┘  │ item_name  │
  │ dp_id PK         │     └──────────┘      │ price      │
  │ name, phone      │                       └────────────┘
  │ vehicle_type     │
  └──────────────────┘

  Descriptive attribute on "contains": quantity, subtotal


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 2 (b) Draw an ER diagram for a smart city traffic management system.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Entities and Attributes:
  Vehicle:         vehicle_id PK, plate_no, type, owner_name
  Traffic_Signal:  signal_id PK, location, status (red/green/yellow), timer
  Intersection:    intersection_id PK, name, coordinates, road_count
  Violation:       violation_id PK, violation_type, date_time, speed
                   (Weak entity — identified with vehicle)
  Fine:            fine_id PK, amount, due_date, paid_status

Relationships:
  Vehicle ──(1:N)──── commits ───── Violation
  Intersection ──(1:N)── has ────── Traffic_Signal
  Violation ──(N:1)──── at ──────── Intersection
  Violation ──(1:1)──── incurs ──── Fine

  Vehicle plays the role of "offender" in the commits relationship.
  Traffic_Signal plays the role of "controller" at an Intersection.

ER Diagram:

  ┌──────────────────┐   1     ┌────────┐   N   ╔══════════════╗
  │    Vehicle       │─────────│commits │───────║  Violation   ║
  │ vehicle_id PK    │(offender)└────────┘       ║ violation_id ║
  │ plate_no         │                           ║ type, speed  ║
  │ type, owner_name │                           ║ date_time    ║
  └──────────────────┘                           ╚══════╤═══════╝
                                                        │  1
  ┌──────────────────┐   1     ┌────────┐   N    ◇ incurs
  │  Intersection    │─────────│  has   │──────┐      │  1
  │ intersection_id  │         └────────┘      │ ┌────┴───────┐
  │ name, coordinates│                         │ │   Fine     │
  │ road_count       │   N     ┌────────┐  1   │ │ fine_id PK │
  └──────┬───────────┘◄────────│   at   │──────┘ │ amount     │
         │                     └────────┘        │ due_date   │
         │                                       │ paid_status│
  ┌──────┴───────────┐                           └────────────┘
  │ Traffic_Signal   │
  │ signal_id PK     │    ╔═══╗ = weak entity (double border)
  │ status, timer    │
  └──────────────────┘


SETS 4–10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
(Sets 4 through 10 follow the same pattern. For each system — hospital,
bank, gym, real estate, event management, online exam, vehicle rental —
the answer identifies entities with typed attributes, marks PK/FK/composite/
multivalued/derived attributes, defines relationships with cardinalities,
and draws the ER diagram. The structure and methodology are identical to
Sets 1–3 above. Key elements for each:)

SET 4 — Hospital Management System:
  Entities: Patient, Doctor, Department, Appointment, Prescription, Medicine
  Special: Doctor ISA Person (specialization with Faculty/Consultant subtypes)
  Key relationships: Patient ──(M:N)── consults ── Doctor (via Appointment)
                     Doctor ──(N:1)── belongs_to ── Department
                     Appointment ──(1:N)── generates ── Prescription
                     Prescription ──(M:N)── includes ── Medicine (with dosage)

SET 5 — Bank Management System:
  Entities: Customer, Account, Branch, Loan, Transaction
  Special: name (composite: first, middle, last), {phone_numbers} (multivalued),
           /account_age/ (derived from opening_date)
  Key relationships: Customer ──(M:N)── holds ── Account
                     Branch ──(1:N)── maintains ── Account
                     Customer ──(M:N)── borrows ── Loan
                     Account ──(1:N)── has ── Transaction

SET 6 — Gym/Fitness Center:
  Entities: Member, Trainer, Workout_Plan, Equipment, Membership
  Key relationships: Member ──(N:1)── has ── Membership
                     Member ──(M:N)── follows ── Workout_Plan
                     Trainer ──(1:N)── designs ── Workout_Plan
                     Workout_Plan ──(M:N)── uses ── Equipment

SET 7 — Real Estate Property Management:
  Entities: Property, Owner, Tenant, Lease_Agreement, Maintenance_Request
  Special: Aggregation — the (Tenant-Lease_Agreement-Property) relationship
           is aggregated and then related to Maintenance_Request.
  Key relationships: Owner ──(1:N)── owns ── Property
                     Tenant ──(M:N)── leases ── Property (via Lease_Agreement)
                     [Lease] ──(1:N)── generates ── Maintenance_Request

SET 8 — Event Management System:
  Entities: Event, Venue, Organizer, Attendee, Sponsor, Ticket
  Special: Ticket is a weak entity (identified by event_id + ticket_no)
  Key relationships: Organizer ──(1:N)── organizes ── Event
                     Event ──(N:1)── held_at ── Venue
                     Event ──(M:N)── attended_by ── Attendee (via Ticket)
                     Event ──(M:N)── sponsored_by ── Sponsor (with amount)

SET 9 — Online Examination System:
  Entities: Student, Exam, Question, Answer, Course, Result
  Special: Total participation of Student in takes_exam, partial for advises.
  Key relationships: Course ──(1:N)── has ── Exam
                     Exam ──(1:N)── contains ── Question
                     Student ──(M:N)── takes ── Exam (via Result with score)
                     Student ──(M:N)── answers ── Question (via Answer)

SET 10 — Vehicle Rental System:
  Entities: Customer, Vehicle, Rental, Insurance, Payment
  Special: Rental as weak entity (rental_no discriminator + vehicle_id),
           /rental_duration/ derived from pickup_date and return_date,
           customer name as composite (first, last).
  Key relationships: Customer ──(1:N)── makes ── Rental
                     Vehicle ──(1:N)── rented_in ── Rental
                     Rental ──(1:1)── covered_by ── Insurance
                     Rental ──(1:1)── paid_via ── Payment



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 3 (a) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) E-commerce schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: customers(cust_id, cust_name, email, city)
                 products(prod_id, prod_name, category, price)
                 orders(order_id, cust_id, prod_id, quantity, order_date)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Names of all customers who live in 'Kathmandu':
   π_(cust_name)(σ_(city='Kathmandu')(customers))

ii. Product names and prices of all products in the 'Electronics' category:
   π_(prod_name, price)(σ_(category='Electronics')(products))

iii. Names of customers who have ordered a product with price > Rs. 10,000:
   π_(cust_name)(customers ⋈ orders ⋈ σ_(price > 10000)(products))

   Step-by-step:
   1. expensive ← σ_(price > 10000)(products)
   2. expensive_orders ← orders ⋈ expensive       (join on prod_id)
   3. result ← π_(cust_name)(customers ⋈ expensive_orders)  (join on cust_id)

iv. Total quantity ordered for each product:
   𝒢_(prod_id, SUM(quantity))(orders)

   Or equivalently with a join to get names:
   π_(prod_name, total_qty)(products ⋈ (prod_id 𝒢 SUM(quantity) AS total_qty(orders)))


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) University schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: student(roll_no, name, dept, semester)
                 course(course_id, course_name, credits)
                 enrollment(roll_no, course_id, grade)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Names of students enrolled in 'Database Systems':
   π_(name)(student ⋈ enrollment ⋈ σ_(course_name='Database Systems')(course))

ii. Names of students who scored 'A' grade in any course:
   π_(name)(student ⋈ σ_(grade='A')(enrollment))

iii. Course names with number of students enrolled:
   π_(course_name, count)(course ⋈ (course_id 𝒢 COUNT(roll_no) AS count(enrollment)))

iv. Names of students NOT enrolled in any course:
   π_(name)(student) − π_(name)(student ⋈ enrollment)

   Or using set difference on roll_no first:
   not_enrolled ← π_(roll_no)(student) − π_(roll_no)(enrollment)
   result ← π_(name)(student ⋈ not_enrolled)


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Logistics schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: warehouse(w_id, w_name, location, capacity)
                 shipment(ship_id, w_id, destination, ship_date, status)
                 driver(driver_id, driver_name, license_no, phone)
                 assignment(ship_id, driver_id, truck_no)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Names of drivers assigned to shipments from warehouse in 'Birgunj':
   birgunj_wh ← σ_(location='Birgunj')(warehouse)
   birgunj_ships ← shipment ⋈ birgunj_wh
   result ← π_(driver_name)(driver ⋈ assignment ⋈ birgunj_ships)

ii. Shipment IDs and destinations with status 'In Transit':
   π_(ship_id, destination)(σ_(status='In Transit')(shipment))

iii. Drivers not assigned to any shipment:
   all_drivers ← π_(driver_id)(driver)
   assigned_drivers ← π_(driver_id)(assignment)
   unassigned ← all_drivers − assigned_drivers
   result ← π_(driver_name)(driver ⋈ unassigned)

iv. Warehouse names with total shipments:
   π_(w_name, ship_count)(warehouse ⋈ (w_id 𝒢 COUNT(ship_id) AS ship_count(shipment)))


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Streaming service schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: users(user_id, username, email, subscription_type, join_date)
                 content(content_id, title, genre, release_year, rating)
                 watch_history(user_id, content_id, watch_date, duration_watched)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Titles of content in 'Action' genre released after 2020:
   π_(title)(σ_(genre='Action' ∧ release_year > 2020)(content))

ii. Usernames of users who watched content with rating above 8.0:
   high_rated ← σ_(rating > 8.0)(content)
   result ← π_(username)(users ⋈ watch_history ⋈ high_rated)

iii. Titles of content that no user has watched:
   all_content ← π_(content_id)(content)
   watched_content ← π_(content_id)(watch_history)
   unwatched ← all_content − watched_content
   result ← π_(title)(content ⋈ unwatched)

iv. Update is a DML operation; relational algebra is a query language
   and does not support updates directly. In SQL:
   UPDATE users SET subscription_type = 'Premium' WHERE join_date < '2023-01-01';


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Airline schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: flight(flight_id, airline, source, destination, departure, arrival)
                 passenger(pass_id, name, passport_no, nationality)
                 booking(booking_id, pass_id, flight_id, seat_no, class, booking_date)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Names of passengers who booked a flight from 'Kathmandu' to 'Delhi':
   ktm_del ← σ_(source='Kathmandu' ∧ destination='Delhi')(flight)
   result ← π_(name)(passenger ⋈ booking ⋈ ktm_del)

ii. Flight IDs and airlines of flights departing after '2025-06-01':
   π_(flight_id, airline)(σ_(departure > '2025-06-01')(flight))

iii. Names of passengers who booked 'Business' class:
   π_(name)(passenger ⋈ σ_(class='Business')(booking))

iv. Names of passengers with bookings on more than one flight:
   multi_flights ← σ_(count > 1)(pass_id 𝒢 COUNT(DISTINCT flight_id) AS count(booking))
   result ← π_(name)(passenger ⋈ multi_flights)


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Social media schema — Write SQL statements.
         Schema: users(user_id, username, full_name, email, join_date)
                 posts(post_id, user_id, content, post_date, likes_count)
                 comments(comment_id, post_id, user_id, comment_text, comment_date)
                 follows(follower_id, following_id, follow_date)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Create the follows table ensuring a user cannot follow themselves:

   CREATE TABLE follows (
       follower_id  VARCHAR(10) REFERENCES users(user_id),
       following_id VARCHAR(10) REFERENCES users(user_id),
       follow_date  DATE NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE,
       PRIMARY KEY (follower_id, following_id),
       CHECK (follower_id <> following_id)
   );

ii. Users with more than 100 followers:

   SELECT u.username
   FROM users u
   JOIN follows f ON u.user_id = f.following_id
   GROUP BY u.user_id, u.username
   HAVING COUNT(f.follower_id) > 100;

iii. Top 10 posts with highest number of comments:

   SELECT p.post_id, p.content, COUNT(c.comment_id) AS comment_count
   FROM posts p
   LEFT JOIN comments c ON p.post_id = c.post_id
   GROUP BY p.post_id, p.content
   ORDER BY comment_count DESC
   LIMIT 10;

iv. Delete all comments by user 'U105':

   DELETE FROM comments WHERE user_id = 'U105';

v. Increment likes_count for post 'P200':

   UPDATE posts SET likes_count = likes_count + 1 WHERE post_id = 'P200';


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Pharmacy schema — Write SQL statements.
         Schema: medicine(med_id, med_name, manufacturer, expiry_date,
                          unit_price, stock_qty)
                 customer(cust_id, cust_name, phone, address)
                 purchase(purchase_id, cust_id, med_id, quantity, purchase_date,
                          total_amount)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Medicines by 'Sun Pharma' that are not expired:

   SELECT med_name, unit_price
   FROM medicine
   WHERE manufacturer = 'Sun Pharma' AND expiry_date > CURRENT_DATE;

ii. Customer with the highest total purchase amount:

   SELECT c.cust_name, SUM(p.total_amount) AS total_spent
   FROM customer c
   JOIN purchase p ON c.cust_id = p.cust_id
   GROUP BY c.cust_id, c.cust_name
   ORDER BY total_spent DESC
   LIMIT 1;

iii. Low stock alert (stock < 10):

   SELECT med_name, stock_qty
   FROM medicine
   WHERE stock_qty < 10;

iv. View: sales_summary

   CREATE VIEW sales_summary AS
   SELECT m.med_name,
          SUM(p.quantity) AS total_qty_sold,
          SUM(p.total_amount) AS total_revenue
   FROM medicine m
   JOIN purchase p ON m.med_id = p.med_id
   GROUP BY m.med_id, m.med_name;

v. Stored procedure:

   CREATE OR REPLACE PROCEDURE get_medicine_info(
       IN p_med_id VARCHAR(10),
       OUT p_stock INT,
       OUT p_expiry DATE
   )
   LANGUAGE SQL
   AS $$
       SELECT stock_qty, expiry_date
       INTO p_stock, p_expiry
       FROM medicine
       WHERE med_id = p_med_id;
   $$;


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Hotel booking schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: hotels(hotel_id, hotel_name, city, star_rating, total_rooms)
                 guests(guest_id, guest_name, phone, email, nationality)
                 reservations(res_id, guest_id, hotel_id, check_in, check_out,
                              room_type, amount)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Hotels in 'Pokhara' with star rating ≥ 4:
   π_(hotel_name)(σ_(city='Pokhara' ∧ star_rating ≥ 4)(hotels))

ii. Guest names with reservations at hotels with > 100 rooms:
   big_hotels ← σ_(total_rooms > 100)(hotels)
   result ← π_(guest_name)(guests ⋈ reservations ⋈ big_hotels)

iii. Hotel names with no reservations:
   all_hotels ← π_(hotel_id)(hotels)
   reserved_hotels ← π_(hotel_id)(reservations)
   no_res ← all_hotels − reserved_hotels
   result ← π_(hotel_name)(hotels ⋈ no_res)

iv. Guests who reserved in both 'Kathmandu' AND 'Pokhara':
   ktm_guests ← π_(guest_id)(reservations ⋈ σ_(city='Kathmandu')(hotels))
   pkr_guests ← π_(guest_id)(reservations ⋈ σ_(city='Pokhara')(hotels))
   both ← ktm_guests ∩ pkr_guests
   result ← π_(guest_name)(guests ⋈ both)


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Inventory schema — Write relational algebra expressions.
         Schema: supplier(supplier_id, supplier_name, city, phone)
                 parts(part_id, part_name, color, weight, unit_cost)
                 supply(supplier_id, part_id, quantity, supply_date)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Names of suppliers who supply 'Red' parts:
   red_parts ← σ_(color='Red')(parts)
   result ← π_(supplier_name)(supplier ⋈ supply ⋈ red_parts)

ii. Part names and weights supplied by 'Nepal Hardware Pvt. Ltd.':
   nh ← σ_(supplier_name='Nepal Hardware Pvt. Ltd.')(supplier)
   result ← π_(part_name, weight)(parts ⋈ supply ⋈ nh)

iii. Suppliers who supply ALL parts (division):
   all_parts ← π_(part_id)(parts)
   supplier_parts ← π_(supplier_id, part_id)(supply)
   supplies_all ← supplier_parts ÷ all_parts
   result ← π_(supplier_name)(supplier ⋈ supplies_all)

iv. Update (DML operation — relational algebra is query-only):
   In SQL: UPDATE parts SET unit_cost = unit_cost * 1.05 WHERE weight > 50;


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (a) Music streaming schema — Write SQL statements.
         Schema: artists(artist_id, artist_name, genre, country)
                 albums(album_id, album_name, artist_id, release_year)
                 songs(song_id, song_name, album_id, duration, play_count)
                 playlists(playlist_id, user_id, playlist_name, created_date)
                 playlist_songs(playlist_id, song_id, added_date)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Top 10 most played songs with artist and album:

   SELECT s.song_name, a.artist_name, al.album_name, s.play_count
   FROM songs s
   JOIN albums al ON s.album_id = al.album_id
   JOIN artists a ON al.artist_id = a.artist_id
   ORDER BY s.play_count DESC
   LIMIT 10;

ii. Artists with more than 3 albums:

   SELECT a.artist_name, COUNT(al.album_id) AS album_count
   FROM artists a
   JOIN albums al ON a.artist_id = al.artist_id
   GROUP BY a.artist_id, a.artist_name
   HAVING COUNT(al.album_id) > 3;

iii. Total play count per genre:

   SELECT a.genre, SUM(s.play_count) AS total_plays
   FROM artists a
   JOIN albums al ON a.artist_id = al.artist_id
   JOIN songs s ON al.album_id = s.album_id
   GROUP BY a.genre
   ORDER BY total_plays DESC;

iv. Delete playlist songs from albums released before 2010:

   DELETE FROM playlist_songs
   WHERE song_id IN (
       SELECT s.song_id
       FROM songs s
       JOIN albums al ON s.album_id = al.album_id
       WHERE al.release_year < 2010
   );

v. View: playlist summary

   CREATE VIEW playlist_summary AS
   SELECT p.playlist_name,
          COUNT(ps.song_id) AS total_songs,
          COALESCE(SUM(s.duration), 0) AS total_duration
   FROM playlists p
   LEFT JOIN playlist_songs ps ON p.playlist_id = ps.playlist_id
   LEFT JOIN songs s ON ps.song_id = s.song_id
   GROUP BY p.playlist_id, p.playlist_name;

SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) E-commerce schema — Write SQL statements.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Create the orders table:

   CREATE TABLE orders (
       order_id   INT PRIMARY KEY,
       cust_id    INT NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(cust_id),
       prod_id    INT NOT NULL REFERENCES products(prod_id),
       quantity   INT NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
       order_date DATE NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE
   );

ii. Insert a new order:

   INSERT INTO orders VALUES (1001, 101, 201, 2, '2025-03-15');

iii. Customers with more than 3 orders:

   SELECT c.cust_name
   FROM customers c
   JOIN orders o ON c.cust_id = o.cust_id
   GROUP BY c.cust_id, c.cust_name
   HAVING COUNT(o.order_id) > 3;

iv. Delete orders before '2024-01-01':

   DELETE FROM orders WHERE order_date < '2024-01-01';

v. Increase price by 15% for 'Clothing':

   UPDATE products SET price = price * 1.15 WHERE category = 'Clothing';


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Nested queries. University schema — SQL statements.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A nested query (subquery) is a SELECT statement embedded within another SQL
statement. The inner query executes first and its result is used by the outer
query. Subqueries can appear in WHERE, FROM, SELECT, or HAVING clauses.

Types:
  • Scalar subquery — returns a single value.
  • Row subquery — returns a single row.
  • Table subquery — returns a full result set.
  • Correlated subquery — references outer query columns; re-executes for
    each outer row.

Example of a correlated subquery:
  SELECT name FROM student s
  WHERE EXISTS (
      SELECT 1 FROM enrollment e WHERE e.roll_no = s.roll_no AND e.grade = 'A'
  );

i. Students enrolled in courses with more than 3 credits:

   SELECT s.name
   FROM student s
   WHERE s.roll_no IN (
       SELECT e.roll_no
       FROM enrollment e
       JOIN course c ON e.course_id = c.course_id
       WHERE c.credits > 3
   );

ii. Average grade point per department (assuming grade → point mapping):

   SELECT s.dept, AVG(
       CASE e.grade
           WHEN 'A' THEN 4.0 WHEN 'B' THEN 3.0
           WHEN 'C' THEN 2.0 WHEN 'D' THEN 1.0 ELSE 0.0
       END
   ) AS avg_gpa
   FROM student s
   JOIN enrollment e ON s.roll_no = e.roll_no
   GROUP BY s.dept;

iii. View showing student name, course name, and grade:

   CREATE VIEW student_grades AS
   SELECT s.name AS student_name,
          c.course_name,
          e.grade
   FROM student s
   JOIN enrollment e ON s.roll_no = e.roll_no
   JOIN course c ON e.course_id = c.course_id;


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Join types — Explain and illustrate with logistics schema.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Joins combine rows from two or more tables based on a related column. SQL supports
several types:

1. NATURAL JOIN: Automatically joins on ALL columns with the same name. No join
   condition needs to be specified. Columns with matching names must have compatible
   types.

   SELECT * FROM shipment NATURAL JOIN warehouse;
   -- Joins on w_id (the common column)

2. INNER JOIN: Returns only rows where the join condition is satisfied in BOTH
   tables. Rows that do not have a matching partner are excluded.

   SELECT d.driver_name, a.truck_no
   FROM driver d
   INNER JOIN assignment a ON d.driver_id = a.driver_id;
   -- Only drivers who have been assigned appear in the result

3. LEFT OUTER JOIN: Returns ALL rows from the LEFT table, and matching rows from
   the right table. If no match exists, NULL values are filled in for the right
   table's columns.

   SELECT d.driver_name, a.truck_no
   FROM driver d
   LEFT OUTER JOIN assignment a ON d.driver_id = a.driver_id;
   -- ALL drivers appear, even unassigned ones (truck_no = NULL for them)

4. RIGHT OUTER JOIN: Returns ALL rows from the RIGHT table, and matching rows
   from the left table. The mirror image of LEFT JOIN.

   SELECT d.driver_name, s.destination
   FROM driver d
   RIGHT OUTER JOIN (assignment a JOIN shipment s ON a.ship_id = s.ship_id)
   ON d.driver_id = a.driver_id;
   -- ALL shipments appear, even those without a driver assignment

5. FULL OUTER JOIN: Returns ALL rows from BOTH tables. Where a match exists,
   columns from both tables are filled. Where no match exists, NULLs are used.

   SELECT w.w_name, s.ship_id, s.destination
   FROM warehouse w
   FULL OUTER JOIN shipment s ON w.w_id = s.w_id;
   -- Warehouses with no shipments AND shipments with invalid w_id both appear

  Comparison:
  ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Join Type        │ Rows Returned                                     │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ INNER JOIN       │ Only matching rows from both tables               │
  │ LEFT OUTER JOIN  │ All left + matching right (NULL if no match)     │
  │ RIGHT OUTER JOIN │ All right + matching left (NULL if no match)     │
  │ FULL OUTER JOIN  │ All from both (NULL where no match)              │
  │ NATURAL JOIN     │ Inner join on all common column names             │
  │ CROSS JOIN       │ Cartesian product (every row × every row)        │
  └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Streaming service schema — SQL statements.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Create watch_history table:

   CREATE TABLE watch_history (
       user_id          VARCHAR(10) REFERENCES users(user_id),
       content_id       VARCHAR(10) REFERENCES content(content_id),
       watch_date       DATE NOT NULL,
       duration_watched INT,  -- in minutes
       PRIMARY KEY (user_id, content_id, watch_date)
   );

ii. Top 5 most-watched content:

   SELECT c.title, COUNT(*) AS watch_count
   FROM content c
   JOIN watch_history wh ON c.content_id = wh.content_id
   GROUP BY c.content_id, c.title
   ORDER BY watch_count DESC
   LIMIT 5;

iii. Users with total watch duration > 100 hours (6000 minutes):

   SELECT u.username, SUM(wh.duration_watched) AS total_minutes
   FROM users u
   JOIN watch_history wh ON u.user_id = wh.user_id
   GROUP BY u.user_id, u.username
   HAVING SUM(wh.duration_watched) > 6000;

iv. Delete watch history older than 2 years:

   DELETE FROM watch_history
   WHERE watch_date < CURRENT_DATE - INTERVAL '2 years';

v. Stored procedure to add a new user:

   CREATE OR REPLACE PROCEDURE add_user(
       p_user_id VARCHAR(10),
       p_username VARCHAR(50),
       p_email VARCHAR(100),
       p_sub_type VARCHAR(20),
       p_join_date DATE
   )
   LANGUAGE SQL
   AS $$
       INSERT INTO users VALUES (p_user_id, p_username, p_email, p_sub_type, p_join_date);
   $$;


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Set operations in SQL with airline schema.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

SET OPERATIONS in SQL allow combining results of two SELECT statements:

UNION: Returns all distinct rows from both queries.
INTERSECT: Returns only rows that appear in both queries.
EXCEPT (MINUS): Returns rows from the first query that do NOT appear in the second.

All set operations require the queries to be UNION-COMPATIBLE (same number of
columns, compatible types).

i. Passengers who booked to both 'Delhi' AND 'Dubai':

   SELECT p.name
   FROM passenger p
   JOIN booking b ON p.pass_id = b.pass_id
   JOIN flight f ON b.flight_id = f.flight_id
   WHERE f.destination = 'Delhi'

   INTERSECT

   SELECT p.name
   FROM passenger p
   JOIN booking b ON p.pass_id = b.pass_id
   JOIN flight f ON b.flight_id = f.flight_id
   WHERE f.destination = 'Dubai';

ii. All flights with booking count:

   SELECT f.flight_id, f.airline, f.source, f.destination,
          COUNT(b.booking_id) AS booking_count
   FROM flight f
   LEFT JOIN booking b ON f.flight_id = b.flight_id
   GROUP BY f.flight_id, f.airline, f.source, f.destination;

iii. Passenger name, flight details, seat for all bookings (sorted):

   SELECT p.name, f.airline, f.source, f.destination,
          b.seat_no, b.booking_date
   FROM passenger p
   JOIN booking b ON p.pass_id = b.pass_id
   JOIN flight f ON b.flight_id = f.flight_id
   ORDER BY b.booking_date DESC;


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Aggregate functions, GROUP BY, and HAVING.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Aggregate functions perform calculations on a set of values and return a single
result. They are the foundation of data summarization in SQL.

  ┌───────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Function  │ Description                                               │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ COUNT(*)  │ Counts the number of rows (including NULLs)              │
  │ COUNT(col)│ Counts non-NULL values in the column                      │
  │ SUM(col)  │ Sums all non-NULL values                                  │
  │ AVG(col)  │ Calculates the average of non-NULL values                 │
  │ MAX(col)  │ Returns the maximum value                                 │
  │ MIN(col)  │ Returns the minimum value                                 │
  └───────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Examples:
  SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders;                     -- Total orders
  SELECT SUM(price * quantity) FROM order_items;    -- Total revenue
  SELECT AVG(salary) FROM employee;                -- Average salary
  SELECT MAX(price) FROM products;                 -- Most expensive product
  SELECT MIN(join_date) FROM users;                -- Earliest user

GROUP BY: Groups rows that have the same values in the specified columns, then
applies aggregate functions to each group independently.

  -- Revenue per category
  SELECT category, SUM(price * quantity) AS revenue
  FROM products p
  JOIN order_items oi ON p.prod_id = oi.prod_id
  GROUP BY category;

HAVING: Filters GROUPS after aggregation (analogous to WHERE for individual rows).
WHERE filters before grouping; HAVING filters after.

  -- Categories with revenue > 100,000
  SELECT category, SUM(price * quantity) AS revenue
  FROM products p
  JOIN order_items oi ON p.prod_id = oi.prod_id
  GROUP BY category
  HAVING SUM(price * quantity) > 100000;

Order of SQL clause execution:
  FROM → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → ORDER BY → LIMIT


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Null values in SQL.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

NULL in SQL represents a MISSING or UNKNOWN value. It is NOT the same as zero,
an empty string, or a blank space. NULL means the value is either not applicable,
not yet known, or does not exist.

HOW NULL AFFECTS COMPARISONS:

SQL uses THREE-VALUED LOGIC: TRUE, FALSE, and UNKNOWN. Any comparison involving
