NULL yields UNKNOWN, not TRUE or FALSE.

  5 = NULL     →  UNKNOWN
  NULL = NULL  →  UNKNOWN
  NULL <> NULL →  UNKNOWN
  NULL > 3     →  UNKNOWN

This means WHERE clauses that compare with NULL using = or <> will NOT return
those rows. To test for NULL, use IS NULL or IS NOT NULL:

  SELECT * FROM student WHERE email IS NULL;     -- Correct
  SELECT * FROM student WHERE email = NULL;      -- WRONG — returns no rows

BOOLEAN LOGIC WITH UNKNOWN:
  TRUE  AND UNKNOWN = UNKNOWN
  FALSE AND UNKNOWN = FALSE
  TRUE  OR  UNKNOWN = TRUE
  FALSE OR  UNKNOWN = UNKNOWN
  NOT UNKNOWN        = UNKNOWN

HOW NULL AFFECTS AGGREGATES:

Aggregate functions IGNORE NULL values (except COUNT(*)):

  Values: {10, NULL, 20, NULL, 30}
  COUNT(*) = 5           (counts all rows including NULLs)
  COUNT(value) = 3       (counts only non-NULL values)
  SUM(value) = 60        (ignores NULLs)
  AVG(value) = 20        (60/3, not 60/5)
  MAX(value) = 30
  MIN(value) = 10

If ALL values are NULL: SUM, AVG, MAX, MIN return NULL. COUNT returns 0.

HANDLING NULLs:
  COALESCE(x, default): Returns x if non-NULL, otherwise returns default.
  NULLIF(a, b): Returns NULL if a = b, otherwise returns a.
  CASE WHEN x IS NULL THEN ... ELSE x END


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Hotel booking schema — SQL statements.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Create reservations with check constraint:

   CREATE TABLE reservations (
       res_id     INT PRIMARY KEY,
       guest_id   INT NOT NULL REFERENCES guests(guest_id),
       hotel_id   INT NOT NULL REFERENCES hotels(hotel_id),
       check_in   DATE NOT NULL,
       check_out  DATE NOT NULL,
       room_type  VARCHAR(20),
       amount     DECIMAL(10,2),
       CHECK (check_out > check_in)
   );

ii. Total revenue per hotel (descending):

   SELECT h.hotel_name, SUM(r.amount) AS total_revenue
   FROM hotels h
   JOIN reservations r ON h.hotel_id = r.hotel_id
   GROUP BY h.hotel_id, h.hotel_name
   ORDER BY total_revenue DESC;

iii. Guests who stayed more than 5 times at a single hotel:

   SELECT g.guest_name
   FROM guests g
   JOIN reservations r ON g.guest_id = r.guest_id
   GROUP BY g.guest_id, g.guest_name, r.hotel_id
   HAVING COUNT(r.res_id) > 5;

iv. Hotels with average reservation above overall average (nested):

   SELECT h.hotel_name, AVG(r.amount) AS avg_amount
   FROM hotels h
   JOIN reservations r ON h.hotel_id = r.hotel_id
   GROUP BY h.hotel_id, h.hotel_name
   HAVING AVG(r.amount) > (SELECT AVG(amount) FROM reservations);


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) Inventory schema — SQL statements.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Supplier with total quantity > 500:

   SELECT s.supplier_name, SUM(su.quantity) AS total_qty
   FROM supplier s
   JOIN supply su ON s.supplier_id = su.supplier_id
   GROUP BY s.supplier_id, s.supplier_name
   HAVING SUM(su.quantity) > 500;

ii. Part with maximum unit cost:

   SELECT part_name, unit_cost
   FROM parts
   WHERE unit_cost = (SELECT MAX(unit_cost) FROM parts);

iii. Suppliers who do NOT supply any 'Blue' part:

   SELECT supplier_name
   FROM supplier
   WHERE supplier_id NOT IN (
       SELECT su.supplier_id
       FROM supply su
       JOIN parts p ON su.part_id = p.part_id
       WHERE p.color = 'Blue'
   );

iv. Stored procedure:

   CREATE OR REPLACE PROCEDURE get_supplier_parts(
       IN p_supplier_id VARCHAR(10)
   )
   LANGUAGE SQL
   AS $$
       SELECT p.part_id, p.part_name, p.color, p.weight, su.quantity
       FROM parts p
       JOIN supply su ON p.part_id = su.part_id
       WHERE su.supplier_id = p_supplier_id;
   $$;


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 3 (b) DDL vs DML. Explain CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

DDL (Data Definition Language) defines and modifies the STRUCTURE of the database.
DDL statements affect the schema and are auto-committed (cannot be rolled back in
most RDBMS). DML (Data Manipulation Language) retrieves and modifies the DATA
within tables. DML statements operate within transactions and can be rolled back.

  ┌─────────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect          │ DDL                    │ DML                      │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Operates on     │ Schema (metadata)      │ Data (tuples/rows)       │
  │ Transaction     │ Auto-commit            │ Explicit COMMIT/ROLLBACK │
  │ Key commands    │ CREATE, ALTER, DROP,   │ SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE,  │
  │                 │ TRUNCATE               │ DELETE                   │
  │ Rollback        │ Usually NOT possible   │ Possible before COMMIT   │
  └─────────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

1. CREATE TABLE — creates a new table:

   CREATE TABLE employee (
       emp_id   INT PRIMARY KEY,
       name     VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
       salary   DECIMAL(10,2) CHECK (salary > 0),
       dept_id  INT REFERENCES department(dept_id)
   );

2. ALTER TABLE — modifies an existing table:

   -- Add a new column
   ALTER TABLE employee ADD COLUMN email VARCHAR(100);

   -- Modify column type
   ALTER TABLE employee ALTER COLUMN name TYPE VARCHAR(200);

   -- Drop a column
   ALTER TABLE employee DROP COLUMN email;

   -- Add a constraint
   ALTER TABLE employee ADD CONSTRAINT chk_sal CHECK (salary >= 10000);

3. DROP TABLE — permanently removes a table and ALL its data:

   DROP TABLE employee;                  -- Error if dependencies exist
   DROP TABLE employee CASCADE;          -- Also drops dependent objects
   DROP TABLE IF EXISTS employee;        -- No error if table doesn't exist

4. TRUNCATE — removes ALL rows but KEEPS the table structure:

   TRUNCATE TABLE employee;

   TRUNCATE vs DELETE:
   ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
   │ Aspect       │ TRUNCATE                 │ DELETE                   │
   ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
   │ Rows removed │ ALL                      │ Can use WHERE clause     │
   │ Speed        │ Very fast (deallocates   │ Slower (row-by-row)      │
   │              │ pages)                   │                          │
   │ Logging      │ Minimal                  │ Full (each row logged)   │
   │ Rollback     │ Usually not possible     │ Possible before COMMIT   │
   │ Triggers     │ Does NOT fire triggers   │ Fires DELETE triggers    │
   │ Identity     │ Resets auto-increment    │ Does not reset           │
   └──────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 4 (a) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Integrity constraints: domain, entity integrity, referential
         integrity.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Integrity constraints are rules defined on a database that restrict the data
values that can be stored, ensuring the accuracy, validity, and consistency of
the data. The DBMS enforces these constraints automatically — any operation that
would violate a constraint is rejected.

1. DOMAIN CONSTRAINTS:

Domain constraints restrict the VALUES that an attribute can take. A domain is the
set of permissible values for an attribute, defined by the data type and any
additional CHECK conditions.

  Examples:
  • age INT CHECK (age >= 0 AND age <= 150) — age must be a non-negative integer
    within a realistic range.
  • gender CHAR(1) CHECK (gender IN ('M', 'F', 'O')) — only three valid values.
  • salary DECIMAL(10,2) NOT NULL — salary must be a decimal and cannot be NULL.
  • email VARCHAR(100) — restricts to strings of at most 100 characters.

Domain constraints are the most basic form of integrity enforcement and are
checked on every INSERT and UPDATE.

2. ENTITY INTEGRITY CONSTRAINTS:

Entity integrity ensures that every tuple in a relation is uniquely identifiable.
It consists of two rules:
  a. Every relation MUST have a PRIMARY KEY.
  b. No attribute that is part of the primary key can have a NULL value.

The rationale is that if a primary key could be NULL, we would not be able to
distinguish between entities, which would violate the fundamental requirement
that every entity must be uniquely identifiable.

  Example:
  CREATE TABLE student (
      roll_no  INT PRIMARY KEY,   -- Cannot be NULL, must be unique
      name     VARCHAR(50)
  );

  INSERT INTO student VALUES (NULL, 'Aarav');  -- REJECTED: PK cannot be NULL
  INSERT INTO student VALUES (101, 'Aarav');   -- Accepted
  INSERT INTO student VALUES (101, 'Bipana');  -- REJECTED: duplicate PK

3. REFERENTIAL INTEGRITY CONSTRAINTS:

Referential integrity ensures that relationships between tables remain consistent.
A foreign key in one relation must either match an existing primary key value in
the referenced relation OR be NULL (if the FK allows NULLs).

  Example:
  CREATE TABLE enrollment (
      roll_no    INT REFERENCES student(roll_no),
      course_id  VARCHAR(10) REFERENCES course(course_id),
      grade      CHAR(2),
      PRIMARY KEY (roll_no, course_id)
  );

  INSERT INTO enrollment VALUES (999, 'CS301', 'A');
  -- REJECTED if roll_no 999 does not exist in student

Referential integrity also governs what happens when the referenced tuple is
deleted or updated. The options (specified as ON DELETE / ON UPDATE actions) are:
  • CASCADE: automatically propagate the change.
  • SET NULL: set the foreign key to NULL.
  • SET DEFAULT: set the foreign key to a default value.
  • RESTRICT / NO ACTION: reject the delete/update.


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Functional dependencies and Armstrong's axioms.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A FUNCTIONAL DEPENDENCY (FD) is a constraint between two sets of attributes in a
relation. Given a relation R, we say that attribute set Y is functionally
dependent on attribute set X (written X → Y) if, for every valid instance of R,
whenever two tuples agree on the values of X, they must also agree on the values
of Y. In other words, X uniquely determines Y.

Example: In student(roll_no, name, dept), roll_no → name means that each roll_no
is associated with exactly one name. Two different tuples with the same roll_no
MUST have the same name.

ARMSTRONG'S AXIOMS:

Armstrong's axioms are a set of inference rules that are SOUND (they produce only
valid FDs) and COMPLETE (they can derive ALL valid FDs that follow from a given set
F). They form the basis for computing the closure of FDs (F⁺).

1. REFLEXIVITY (Trivial Dependency):
   If Y ⊆ X, then X → Y.

   Example: {roll_no, name} → roll_no (a set of attributes always determines
   any of its subsets — this is trivially true).

   {A, B, C} → {A, B} — always holds.

2. AUGMENTATION:
   If X → Y, then XZ → YZ for any set of attributes Z.

   Example: If roll_no → name, then (roll_no, dept) → (name, dept).
   Adding the same attributes to both sides preserves the dependency.

3. TRANSITIVITY:
   If X → Y and Y → Z, then X → Z.

   Example: If roll_no → dept and dept → dept_head, then roll_no → dept_head.
   The dependency chains through Y.

From these three BASIC axioms, several additional useful rules can be derived:

4. UNION: If X → Y and X → Z, then X → YZ.
   Example: If roll_no → name and roll_no → dept, then roll_no → (name, dept).

5. DECOMPOSITION: If X → YZ, then X → Y and X → Z.
   Example: If roll_no → (name, dept), then roll_no → name and roll_no → dept.

6. PSEUDO-TRANSITIVITY: If X → Y and WY → Z, then WX → Z.

These axioms are used to compute the CLOSURE OF A SET OF ATTRIBUTES (X⁺), which
is the set of all attributes that can be determined by X given the FDs. Attribute
closure is fundamental to identifying candidate keys and testing normalization.


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Assertions and triggers in SQL.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Both assertions and triggers are mechanisms for enforcing complex integrity
constraints that cannot be expressed through simple column-level or table-level
constraints (like CHECK, NOT NULL, or FOREIGN KEY).

ASSERTIONS:

An assertion is a declarative constraint that specifies a condition that the
database must ALWAYS satisfy. Unlike CHECK constraints (which apply to a single
table), assertions can involve multiple tables and complex subqueries. If any
modification to the database would cause the assertion condition to become false,
the modification is rejected.

Syntax:
  CREATE ASSERTION assertion_name CHECK (condition);

Example: "The total budget of all departments must not exceed 10,000,000."
  CREATE ASSERTION budget_limit CHECK (
      (SELECT SUM(budget) FROM department) <= 10000000
  );

Example: "Every student must be enrolled in at least one course."
  CREATE ASSERTION must_enroll CHECK (
      NOT EXISTS (
          SELECT s.roll_no FROM student s
          WHERE NOT EXISTS (
              SELECT 1 FROM enrollment e WHERE e.roll_no = s.roll_no
          )
      )
  );

Note: Most commercial DBMS do NOT fully support assertions due to performance
concerns (they must be checked after every modification). Triggers are typically
used instead.

TRIGGERS:

A trigger is a procedural mechanism that automatically executes a block of code
(the trigger body) in response to a specific event (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE) on
a specific table. Triggers are more flexible than assertions because they can
execute arbitrary SQL statements, not just check conditions.

Components of a trigger:
  • EVENT: What operation activates the trigger (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE).
  • TIMING: When the trigger fires (BEFORE or AFTER the event).
  • GRANULARITY: Per ROW (fires once for each affected row) or per STATEMENT.
  • CONDITION: Optional WHEN clause to restrict trigger firing.
  • ACTION: The SQL statements to execute.

Example: Log every salary change to an audit table.

  CREATE TRIGGER salary_audit
  AFTER UPDATE OF salary ON employee
  FOR EACH ROW
  WHEN (OLD.salary <> NEW.salary)
  BEGIN
      INSERT INTO salary_log (emp_id, old_salary, new_salary, change_date)
      VALUES (NEW.emp_id, OLD.salary, NEW.salary, CURRENT_TIMESTAMP);
  END;

In this trigger, OLD refers to the tuple values before the update, and NEW refers
to the values after. The trigger fires for each row where the salary actually
changed and records the change in a log table.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Denormalization — when, why, and trade-offs.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

DENORMALIZATION is the deliberate process of introducing controlled redundancy
into a normalized database schema to improve read performance. It is the
strategic reversal of normalization — adding redundant data or merging tables
to reduce the number of joins needed for common queries.

While normalization eliminates redundancy and ensures data integrity, it often
results in many small tables that must be joined frequently for common queries.
In read-heavy applications (such as reporting dashboards, analytics, or high-
traffic web applications), these joins can become performance bottlenecks.

WHEN TO USE DENORMALIZATION:
  • When read performance is critical and queries involve many joins.
  • When the application is read-heavy (many more SELECTs than INSERTs/UPDATEs).
  • When query response time is more important than storage efficiency.
  • After profiling queries and identifying join-related bottlenecks.

WHY — PRACTICAL EXAMPLE:

Consider a normalized e-commerce database:
  customers(cust_id, name, city)
  orders(order_id, cust_id, order_date)
  order_items(order_id, prod_id, quantity)
  products(prod_id, prod_name, price)

A common query — "show customer name, order date, product name, and total" —
requires joining ALL FOUR tables. On a dashboard that runs this query thousands
of times per second, this is expensive.

Denormalized: Add cust_name to orders and prod_name + price to order_items:
  order_summary(order_id, cust_id, cust_name, order_date, prod_name,
                quantity, unit_price, line_total)

Now the query needs no joins at all.

TRADE-OFFS:
  ┌──────────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Normalization          │ Denormalization          │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Redundancy       │ Minimal                │ Controlled/Intentional   │
  │ Read Performance │ Slower (many joins)    │ Faster (fewer joins)     │
  │ Write Performance│ Faster (one update)    │ Slower (multiple updates)│
  │ Storage          │ Less                   │ More                     │
  │ Update Anomalies │ None                   │ Possible                 │
  │ Data Integrity   │ High                   │ Requires careful control │
  │ Schema Complexity│ More tables, simpler   │ Fewer tables, wider      │
  │ Best for         │ OLTP (transactions)    │ OLAP (analytics/reports) │
  └──────────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Features of good relational designs and problems of bad design.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A good relational database design minimizes redundancy, avoids anomalies, and
ensures that each relation represents a single, coherent concept. The quality of
a database design is measured by how well it avoids the following problems.

PROBLEMS OF BAD DATABASE DESIGN:

Consider a poorly designed (unnormalized) relation:

  student_course(roll_no, name, dept, course_id, course_name, instructor, grade)

  ┌─────────┬────────┬──────┬───────────┬──────────┬────────────┬───────┐
  │ roll_no │ name   │ dept │ course_id │ course   │ instructor │ grade │
  ├─────────┼────────┼──────┼───────────┼──────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ 101     │ Aarav  │ CS   │ CS301     │ DBMS     │ Dr. Sharma │ A     │
  │ 101     │ Aarav  │ CS   │ CS302     │ Networks │ Dr. Poudel │ B     │
  │ 102     │ Bipana │ IT   │ CS301     │ DBMS     │ Dr. Sharma │ A     │
  └─────────┴────────┴──────┴───────────┴──────────┴────────────┴───────┘

1. REDUNDANCY: Aarav's name and dept are stored twice (once for each course).
   Dr. Sharma's name and the course name "DBMS" are repeated for every student
   enrolled. This wastes storage and creates maintenance nightmares.

2. UPDATE ANOMALY: If Dr. Sharma changes her name (e.g., marriage), we must
   update EVERY row where she appears. Missing even one row creates inconsistency.

3. INSERTION ANOMALY: We cannot add a new course (e.g., "AI" by Dr. Thapa")
   unless at least one student is enrolled in it, because roll_no is part of the
   key and cannot be NULL.

4. DELETION ANOMALY: If Bipana (the only student in CS301 from IT) drops the
   course, we lose not only her enrollment but also the information that CS301
   is taught by Dr. Sharma.

FEATURES OF GOOD RELATIONAL DESIGN:

1. Each relation represents a SINGLE entity or relationship (no mixing).
2. Attributes depend on the key, the WHOLE key, and NOTHING BUT THE KEY (3NF).
3. Minimal redundancy — data is stored only once.
4. No update, insertion, or deletion anomalies.
5. Appropriate normalization (at least 3NF/BCNF for OLTP).
6. Clear primary keys and foreign keys.
7. Meaningful attribute names and consistent naming conventions.
8. Proper use of constraints for data integrity.

The solution for the above bad design is normalization into:
  student(roll_no PK, name, dept)
  course(course_id PK, course_name, instructor)
  enrollment(roll_no FK, course_id FK, grade)  PK = (roll_no, course_id)


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Closure of attribute sets with R(A,B,C,D,E,F).
         F = {AB → C, C → D, D → E, E → F, B → D}
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Finding {A, B}⁺:

Start with {A, B}⁺ = {A, B}

Apply AB → C:  A, B ∈ {A,B}⁺  →  add C  →  {A, B, C}
Apply C → D:   C ∈ {A,B,C}    →  add D  →  {A, B, C, D}
Apply D → E:   D ∈ result      →  add E  →  {A, B, C, D, E}
Apply E → F:   E ∈ result      →  add F  →  {A, B, C, D, E, F}
Apply B → D:   D already in result.

{A, B}⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E, F} = ALL attributes of R

Since {A,B}⁺ contains all attributes, AB is a SUPER KEY.

ii. Finding candidate keys:

  Can A alone determine all? A⁺: Start {A}. No FD has only A on left.
  A⁺ = {A}. Not a super key.

  Can B alone? B⁺: Start {B}. Apply B → D → {B,D}. Apply D → E → {B,D,E}.
  Apply E → F → {B,D,E,F}. No more. B⁺ = {B,D,E,F}. Not all attributes.

  AB is a super key (from part i). Is it minimal?
  A alone: not a super key. B alone: not a super key.
  So AB is a CANDIDATE KEY (no proper subset is a super key).

  Are there other candidate keys? Check other pairs:
  AC⁺: {A,C} → C→D → {A,C,D} → D→E → {A,C,D,E} → E→F → {A,C,D,E,F}. Missing B.
  Not a super key.

  AB is the ONLY candidate key.

iii. Highest normal form:

Check 3NF: For each non-trivial FD X → Y, either X is a superkey or Y is part of
a candidate key.

  AB → C: AB is a superkey ✓
  C → D:  C is not a superkey, D is not part of any candidate key. VIOLATES 3NF.
  D → E:  D is not a superkey, E is not part of any candidate key. VIOLATES 3NF.
  E → F:  Same reasoning. VIOLATES 3NF.
  B → D:  B is not a superkey, D is not part of any candidate key. VIOLATES 3NF.

The relation is NOT in 3NF. Since C, D, E are non-key attributes determining
other non-key attributes, the relation is only in 1NF (assuming all values are
atomic).

Actually, checking more carefully: AB is the only candidate key. C → D is a
transitive dependency (AB → C → D). So the relation is in 1NF but has partial/
transitive dependencies. It is in 1NF.


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Multivalued dependencies (MVDs) and 4NF.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A MULTIVALUED DEPENDENCY (MVD) is a constraint where the value of one attribute
determines a SET of values of another attribute, independent of other attributes.
An MVD X →→ Y in a relation R(X, Y, Z) means that the set of Y values associated
with a given X value is independent of the set of Z values associated with that
same X value.

FUNCTIONAL DEPENDENCY vs MULTIVALUED DEPENDENCY:

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ FD: X → Y            │ MVD: X →→ Y                                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Each X determines    │ Each X determines a SET of Y values             │
  │ exactly ONE Y value  │ independent of Z values                         │
  │ If t1[X]=t2[X],      │ If t1[X]=t2[X], swapping Y values between      │
  │ then t1[Y]=t2[Y]     │ t1 and t2 must also produce valid tuples       │
  │ Produces redundancy  │ Produces a DIFFERENT kind of redundancy         │
  │ solved by 3NF/BCNF   │ Solved by 4NF                                   │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Example: Consider a relation representing students, their courses, and hobbies:

  student_info(student_id, course, hobby)

  ┌────────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
  │ student_id │ course   │ hobby    │
  ├────────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ S1         │ DBMS     │ Cricket  │
  │ S1         │ DBMS     │ Music    │
  │ S1         │ Networks │ Cricket  │
  │ S1         │ Networks │ Music    │
  └────────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

Here, student_id →→ course and student_id →→ hobby. The courses a student takes
are independent of their hobbies. But to represent all combinations, we get
redundancy (S1's DBMS appears twice — once for each hobby).

This relation is in BCNF (no FD violations) but still has redundancy due to MVDs.

FOURTH NORMAL FORM (4NF):

A relation is in 4NF if, for every non-trivial MVD X →→ Y, X is a superkey.

The above relation violates 4NF because student_id →→ course, but student_id is
NOT a superkey (the key is {student_id, course, hobby}).

DECOMPOSITION TO 4NF:
  student_course(student_id, course)
  student_hobby(student_id, hobby)

This eliminates the redundancy entirely. 4NF ensures that independent multi-
valued facts about an entity are stored in separate relations.


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Referential integrity: CASCADE, SET NULL, SET DEFAULT, RESTRICT.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Referential integrity ensures that a foreign key value in one table always
corresponds to an existing primary key value in the referenced table. A violation
occurs when:
  • An INSERT or UPDATE in the referencing table sets a FK value that does not
    exist in the referenced table.
  • A DELETE or UPDATE in the referenced table removes/changes a PK value that
    is still referenced by FK values in other tables.

The second case is handled by specifying an ACTION to take when a referenced
row is deleted or updated:

1. CASCADE: The operation propagates to all referencing rows.
   • ON DELETE CASCADE: When a row in the parent table is deleted, all rows in
     the child table that reference it are ALSO deleted.
   • ON UPDATE CASCADE: When the PK in the parent is updated, the FK values
     in all child rows are automatically updated to match.

   Example: Deleting a department cascades to delete all students in that dept.
   This is dangerous but useful for true existence-dependent relationships.

2. SET NULL: The FK values in the referencing rows are set to NULL.
   • ON DELETE SET NULL: Deleting a department sets dept_id to NULL for all
     students who were in that department.
   • Requires the FK column to allow NULLs.

3. SET DEFAULT: The FK values are set to a pre-defined default value.
   • ON DELETE SET DEFAULT: Deleting a department sets dept_id to a default
     value (e.g., 'UNASSIGNED') for affected students.
   • Requires a DEFAULT value to be defined and that value must exist in the
     referenced table.

4. RESTRICT (NO ACTION): The operation is REJECTED if any referencing rows exist.
   • ON DELETE RESTRICT: Cannot delete a department if any students reference it.
   • This is the safest option and is the DEFAULT behavior in most DBMS.

  Example DDL:
  CREATE TABLE student (
      roll_no   INT PRIMARY KEY,
      name      VARCHAR(50),
      dept_id   INT REFERENCES department(dept_id)
                ON DELETE SET NULL
                ON UPDATE CASCADE
  );

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Action           │ Effect when referenced row is deleted            │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CASCADE          │ Delete all referencing rows too                  │
  │ SET NULL         │ Set FK to NULL in referencing rows               │
  │ SET DEFAULT      │ Set FK to default value in referencing rows      │
  │ RESTRICT         │ Reject the delete operation entirely             │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Database normalization and how it eliminates anomalies.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Database normalization is a systematic process of organizing a relational database
to reduce data redundancy and eliminate undesirable characteristics known as
anomalies (insertion, update, and deletion anomalies). It involves decomposing
relations into smaller, well-structured relations using the theory of functional
dependencies.

ANOMALIES IN AN UNNORMALIZED RELATION:

Consider: emp_project(emp_id, emp_name, dept, proj_id, proj_name, hours)

  ┌────────┬──────────┬──────┬─────────┬───────────┬───────┐
  │ emp_id │ emp_name │ dept │ proj_id │ proj_name │ hours │
  ├────────┼──────────┼──────┼─────────┼───────────┼───────┤
  │ E1     │ Ramesh   │ IT   │ P101    │ Website   │ 40    │
  │ E1     │ Ramesh   │ IT   │ P102    │ App       │ 20    │
  │ E2     │ Sita     │ IT   │ P101    │ Website   │ 30    │
  └────────┴──────────┴──────┴─────────┴───────────┴───────┘

1. INSERTION ANOMALY: We cannot record a new project (e.g., P103 "Analytics")
   unless at least one employee is assigned to it, because emp_id is part of the
   composite primary key.

2. UPDATE ANOMALY: If the project P101 is renamed from "Website" to "Portal,"
   we must update EVERY row that mentions P101. Missing even one creates
   inconsistency.

3. DELETION ANOMALY: If Sita (the only employee other than Ramesh on P101) leaves,
   deleting her row might lose the information that P101 is called "Website" —
   though Ramesh's row still has it. But if E2 was the ONLY employee on a unique
   project, deleting that row would lose the project information entirely.

HOW NORMALIZATION ELIMINATES ANOMALIES:

Normalization decomposes the relation based on FDs:
  FDs: emp_id → (emp_name, dept), proj_id → proj_name

Decomposed into:
  employee(emp_id PK, emp_name, dept)
  project(proj_id PK, proj_name)
  assignment(emp_id FK, proj_id FK, hours)  PK = (emp_id, proj_id)

Now:
  • Insertion anomaly gone: We can add project P103 to the project table
    without assigning any employee.
  • Update anomaly gone: Renaming P101 requires updating only ONE row in
    the project table.
  • Deletion anomaly gone: Deleting Sita's assignment does not affect the
    project or employee information.


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (a) Domain constraints and check constraints with SQL examples.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

DOMAIN CONSTRAINTS specify the set of valid values for an attribute by restricting
its data type. Every attribute in a relation must be drawn from a specific domain.
The DBMS automatically enforces these constraints by rejecting any value that does
not conform to the defined data type.

Examples in SQL:
  emp_id    INT             -- Only integers
  name      VARCHAR(100)    -- Strings up to 100 characters
  salary    DECIMAL(10,2)   -- Decimal with 10 digits, 2 after point
  join_date DATE            -- Valid dates only
  email     VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL  -- Cannot be NULL

In PostgreSQL, you can define custom domains:
  CREATE DOMAIN positive_int AS INT CHECK (VALUE > 0);
  CREATE DOMAIN email_type AS VARCHAR(100) CHECK (VALUE LIKE '%@%.%');

CHECK CONSTRAINTS are more specific restrictions on the values of one or more
columns. They allow you to define arbitrary Boolean conditions that must be
satisfied for every row.

Column-level CHECK:
  CREATE TABLE student (
      roll_no   INT PRIMARY KEY,
      name      VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,
      age       INT CHECK (age >= 16 AND age <= 60),
      gpa       DECIMAL(3,2) CHECK (gpa BETWEEN 0.00 AND 4.00),
      gender    CHAR(1) CHECK (gender IN ('M', 'F', 'O'))
  );

Table-level CHECK (can reference multiple columns):
  CREATE TABLE reservation (
      res_id     INT PRIMARY KEY,
      check_in   DATE NOT NULL,
      check_out  DATE NOT NULL,
      CHECK (check_out > check_in)  -- Multi-column constraint
  );

CHECK constraints are evaluated on INSERT and UPDATE. If the condition evaluates
to FALSE, the operation is rejected. If it evaluates to UNKNOWN (due to NULLs),
it is treated as satisfied (because NULL comparisons are UNKNOWN, not FALSE).



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 4 (b) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Normalize the Student-Course relation into 3NF.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Given relation:
  R(Student_ID, Student_Name, Course_ID, Course_Name, Instructor, Dept)

Step 1: Identify functional dependencies:
  Student_ID → Student_Name
  Course_ID → Course_Name, Instructor, Dept
  (Student_ID, Course_ID) is the composite primary key.

Step 2: Check 1NF — All values are atomic. ✓ Already in 1NF.

Step 3: Check 2NF — No partial dependencies allowed.
  Student_ID → Student_Name is a PARTIAL dependency (Student_Name depends on
  only PART of the composite PK). VIOLATES 2NF.
  Course_ID → Course_Name, Instructor, Dept is also partial. VIOLATES 2NF.

  Decompose to remove partial dependencies:
    Student(Student_ID PK, Student_Name)
    Course(Course_ID PK, Course_Name, Instructor, Dept)
    Enrollment(Student_ID FK, Course_ID FK)  PK = (Student_ID, Course_ID)

Step 4: Check 3NF — No transitive dependencies.
  In Student: Student_ID → Student_Name. No transitive dependency. ✓
  In Course: Course_ID → Instructor, Course_ID → Dept.
  Is there Instructor → Dept? If we assume each instructor belongs to one dept,
  then YES: Instructor → Dept is a transitive dependency
  (Course_ID → Instructor → Dept). VIOLATES 3NF.

  Decompose Course further:
    Course(Course_ID PK, Course_Name, Instructor FK)
    Instructor_Info(Instructor PK, Dept)

FINAL 3NF SCHEMA:
  Student(Student_ID PK, Student_Name)
  Instructor_Info(Instructor PK, Dept)
  Course(Course_ID PK, Course_Name, Instructor FK → Instructor_Info)
  Enrollment(Student_ID FK, Course_ID FK)  PK = (Student_ID, Course_ID)

Note: If Instructor does NOT determine Dept (i.e., an instructor can be in
multiple depts), then Course was already in 3NF after Step 3. The final answer
depends on the real-world semantics.


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Normalize Emp-Project to BCNF.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Given relation:
  R(Emp_ID, Emp_Name, Project_ID, Project_Name, Hours, Dept_Name, Dept_Head)

Step 1: Identify FDs:
  Emp_ID → Emp_Name, Dept_Name, Dept_Head
  Project_ID → Project_Name
  Dept_Name → Dept_Head
  (Emp_ID, Project_ID) → Hours

  Composite PK = (Emp_ID, Project_ID)

Step 2: 1NF — All values are atomic. ✓

Step 3: 2NF — Check for partial dependencies:
  Emp_ID → Emp_Name, Dept_Name, Dept_Head (partial — depends on part of PK)
  Project_ID → Project_Name (partial — depends on part of PK)

  Decompose:
    Employee(Emp_ID PK, Emp_Name, Dept_Name, Dept_Head)
    Project(Project_ID PK, Project_Name)
    Assignment(Emp_ID FK, Project_ID FK, Hours)  PK = (Emp_ID, Project_ID)

Step 4: 3NF — Check for transitive dependencies:
  In Employee: Emp_ID → Dept_Name → Dept_Head (transitive!) VIOLATES 3NF.

  Decompose:
    Employee(Emp_ID PK, Emp_Name, Dept_Name FK)
    Department(Dept_Name PK, Dept_Head)

Step 5: BCNF — For every non-trivial FD X → Y, X must be a superkey.
  Employee: Emp_ID → (Emp_Name, Dept_Name). Emp_ID is PK (superkey). ✓
  Department: Dept_Name → Dept_Head. Dept_Name is PK (superkey). ✓
  Project: Project_ID → Project_Name. Project_ID is PK (superkey). ✓
  Assignment: (Emp_ID, Project_ID) → Hours. Composite PK (superkey). ✓

FINAL BCNF SCHEMA:
  Employee(Emp_ID PK, Emp_Name, Dept_Name FK → Department)
  Department(Dept_Name PK, Dept_Head)
  Project(Project_ID PK, Project_Name)
  Assignment(Emp_ID FK, Project_ID FK, Hours)  PK = (Emp_ID, Project_ID)


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Closure of FDs for R(A,B,C,D,E), F = {A→BC, B→D, CD→E, A→D}.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. A⁺ (Closure of A):
  Start: A⁺ = {A}
  Apply A → BC: add B, C → {A, B, C}
  Apply B → D:  add D → {A, B, C, D}
  Apply CD → E: C,D ∈ result → add E → {A, B, C, D, E}
  Apply A → D:  D already present.

  A⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E} = ALL attributes

ii. Candidate keys:
  Since A⁺ = all attributes, A is a superkey.
  A is a single attribute, so no proper subset exists.
  Therefore, A is a CANDIDATE KEY.

  Are there others? We need to check if any other single attribute or
  minimal combination determines all attributes.
  B⁺ = {B, D} (B→D). Not all attributes.
  C⁺ = {C}. Not all.
  D⁺ = {D}. Not all.
  E⁺ = {E}. Not all.

  A is the ONLY candidate key.

iii. Normal form analysis:
  Check each FD against BCNF (which implies 3NF):
  • A → BC: A is a superkey ✓
  • B → D: B is NOT a superkey (B⁺ = {B,D} ≠ all attributes).
    Also, D is not part of any candidate key. VIOLATES both BCNF and 3NF.
  • CD → E: {C,D}⁺ = {C,D,E}. Not all attributes. CD is not a superkey.
    E is not part of any candidate key. VIOLATES both BCNF and 3NF.
  • A → D: A is a superkey ✓

  The relation is NOT in 3NF (and therefore not in BCNF).
  Since all values are atomic, it is in 1NF.
  Since the only candidate key is A (single attribute), there can be no
  partial dependencies on the PK. So it is in 2NF.
  But B → D is a transitive dependency (A → B → D), violating 3NF.

  The relation is in 2NF but NOT in 3NF.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) SQL trigger to update stock on new order.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A trigger is a stored procedure that automatically executes when a specific event
occurs on a table. Here, we want to reduce the stock_qty of a product whenever a
new order is placed.

Assuming:
  products(prod_id PK, prod_name, price, stock_qty)
  orders(order_id PK, prod_id FK, quantity, order_date)

Trigger:

  CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION update_stock()
  RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
  BEGIN
      -- Reduce stock by the ordered quantity
      UPDATE products
      SET stock_qty = stock_qty - NEW.quantity
      WHERE prod_id = NEW.prod_id;

      -- Optionally check if stock goes negative
      IF (SELECT stock_qty FROM products WHERE prod_id = NEW.prod_id) < 0 THEN
          RAISE EXCEPTION 'Insufficient stock for product %', NEW.prod_id;
      END IF;

      RETURN NEW;
  END;
  $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

  CREATE TRIGGER trg_update_stock
  AFTER INSERT ON orders
  FOR EACH ROW
  EXECUTE FUNCTION update_stock();

How it works:
  1. When a new row is inserted into the orders table, the trigger fires.
  2. NEW refers to the newly inserted row — NEW.prod_id and NEW.quantity give
     the product and quantity ordered.
  3. The trigger updates the products table, reducing stock_qty by the ordered
     quantity.
  4. If stock goes negative, an exception is raised and the transaction is
     rolled back (both the order and the stock update are undone).


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Normalize the Invoice relation into 3NF.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Given relation:
  R(Invoice_No, Date, Cust_ID, Cust_Name, Item_Code, Item_Name, Qty,
    Unit_Price, Total)

Step 1: Identify FDs:
  Invoice_No → Date, Cust_ID, Cust_Name
  Cust_ID → Cust_Name
  Item_Code → Item_Name, Unit_Price
  (Invoice_No, Item_Code) → Qty, Total
  Total is derived: Total = Qty × Unit_Price

  Composite PK = (Invoice_No, Item_Code)

Step 2: 1NF — All values atomic ✓

Step 3: 2NF — Remove partial dependencies:
  Invoice_No → Date, Cust_ID, Cust_Name (partial — depends on part of PK)
  Item_Code → Item_Name, Unit_Price (partial — depends on part of PK)

  Decompose:
    Invoice(Invoice_No PK, Date, Cust_ID FK, Cust_Name)
    Item(Item_Code PK, Item_Name, Unit_Price)
    Invoice_Item(Invoice_No FK, Item_Code FK, Qty, Total)
      PK = (Invoice_No, Item_Code)

Step 4: 3NF — Remove transitive dependencies:
  In Invoice: Invoice_No → Cust_ID → Cust_Name (transitive!) VIOLATES 3NF.

  Decompose:
    Invoice(Invoice_No PK, Date, Cust_ID FK)
    Customer(Cust_ID PK, Cust_Name)

  Note: Total = Qty × Unit_Price is a derived attribute. It can be kept as a
  stored computed column or removed (calculated at query time).

FINAL 3NF SCHEMA:
  Customer(Cust_ID PK, Cust_Name)
  Invoice(Invoice_No PK, Date, Cust_ID FK → Customer)
  Item(Item_Code PK, Item_Name, Unit_Price)
  Invoice_Item(Invoice_No FK, Item_Code FK, Qty, Total)
    PK = (Invoice_No, Item_Code)


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Assertions vs triggers + audit log trigger.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

ASSERTIONS vs TRIGGERS:

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Assertion               │ Trigger                 │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Nature           │ Declarative             │ Procedural              │
  │ Scope            │ Entire database         │ Single table            │
  │ When checked     │ After every modification│ On specific events      │
  │                  │ that could violate it   │ (INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE)  │
  │ Action on        │ Rejects the violating   │ Can execute arbitrary   │
  │ violation        │ operation               │ SQL (log, update, etc.) │
  │ DBMS support     │ Very limited            │ Widely supported        │
  │ Performance      │ Expensive (global check)│ Efficient (targeted)    │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

Assertions define WHAT must be true; triggers define WHAT TO DO when something
happens.

AUDIT LOG TRIGGER:

Assuming:
  employee(emp_id, name, salary, dept)
  audit_log(log_id SERIAL, emp_id, emp_name, deleted_at TIMESTAMP)

  CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION log_employee_deletion()
  RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
  BEGIN
      INSERT INTO audit_log (emp_id, emp_name, deleted_at)
      VALUES (OLD.emp_id, OLD.name, CURRENT_TIMESTAMP);
      RETURN OLD;
  END;
  $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

  CREATE TRIGGER trg_audit_delete
  BEFORE DELETE ON employee
  FOR EACH ROW
  EXECUTE FUNCTION log_employee_deletion();

When any row is deleted from employee, OLD.emp_id and OLD.name are captured and
logged with the current timestamp before the deletion proceeds.


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Normalize Order-Product relation into 3NF.
         PK = {Order_ID, Product_ID}
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Given FDs:
  Order_ID → Order_Date, Cust_Name, Cust_City
  Cust_Name → Cust_City (assuming each customer lives in one city)
  Product_ID → Product_Name, Price
  (Order_ID, Product_ID) → Qty

Step 1: 1NF ✓ (all values atomic)

Step 2: 2NF — Partial dependencies:
  Order_ID → Order_Date, Cust_Name, Cust_City (partial)
  Product_ID → Product_Name, Price (partial)

  Decompose:
    Order(Order_ID PK, Order_Date, Cust_Name, Cust_City)
    Product(Product_ID PK, Product_Name, Price)
    Order_Product(Order_ID FK, Product_ID FK, Qty)
      PK = (Order_ID, Product_ID)

Step 3: 3NF — Transitive dependencies:
  In Order: Order_ID → Cust_Name → Cust_City (transitive!)

  Decompose:
    Order(Order_ID PK, Order_Date, Cust_Name FK)
    Customer(Cust_Name PK, Cust_City)

  (Using Cust_Name as PK assumes unique customer names. In practice, a
   Cust_ID would be introduced.)

FINAL 3NF SCHEMA:
  Customer(Cust_Name PK, Cust_City)
  Order(Order_ID PK, Order_Date, Cust_Name FK → Customer)
  Product(Product_ID PK, Product_Name, Price)
  Order_Product(Order_ID FK, Product_ID FK, Qty)  PK = (Order_ID, Product_ID)


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) BCNF vs 3NF. Example in 3NF but not BCNF. Decompose.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

3NF: For every non-trivial FD X → Y, EITHER X is a superkey OR Y is a prime
attribute (part of some candidate key).

BCNF: For every non-trivial FD X → Y, X MUST be a superkey. (No exception for
prime attributes.)

BCNF is strictly stronger than 3NF. Every BCNF relation is in 3NF, but not
every 3NF relation is in BCNF.

EXAMPLE — In 3NF but NOT in BCNF:

  R(Student, Course, Instructor)
  FDs:
    (Student, Course) → Instructor  (each student-course pair has one instructor)
    Instructor → Course             (each instructor teaches only one course)

  Candidate keys:
    (Student, Course)⁺ = {Student, Course, Instructor} ✓ superkey
    (Student, Instructor)⁺: Student,Instructor → Course (via Instructor → Course)
      = {Student, Instructor, Course} ✓ also a superkey

  Both are candidate keys. All attributes are prime (part of some CK).

  Check 3NF for Instructor → Course:
    Instructor is NOT a superkey (Instructor⁺ = {Instructor, Course} ≠ all).
    BUT Course IS a prime attribute (part of CK {Student, Course}).
    So 3NF condition satisfied ✓.

  Check BCNF for Instructor → Course:
    Instructor is NOT a superkey. VIOLATES BCNF ✗.

  So R is in 3NF but NOT in BCNF.

DECOMPOSITION INTO BCNF:
  Decompose on the violating FD Instructor → Course:

  R₁(Instructor PK, Course)         — from the FD Instructor → Course
  R₂(Student, Instructor FK)        — remaining attributes with Student
    PK = (Student, Instructor)

  Both R₁ and R₂ are in BCNF:
    R₁: Instructor → Course, Instructor is PK ✓
    R₂: No non-trivial FDs (or (Student, Instructor) is PK) ✓

  Note: This decomposition is lossless (the join of R₁ and R₂ gives back R)
  but may NOT be dependency-preserving (the FD (Student, Course) → Instructor
  cannot be checked on a single table).


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) R(A,B,C,D,E,F), F = {A→B, A→C, BC→D, D→E, CF→A}.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

i. Closures:

  A⁺: Start {A}. A→B: {A,B}. A→C: {A,B,C}. BC→D: {A,B,C,D}.
      D→E: {A,B,C,D,E}. CF→A: A already present.
  A⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E}  (missing F — NOT all attributes)

  (CF)⁺: Start {C,F}. CF→A: {A,C,F}. A→B: {A,B,C,F}. A→C: C already.
         BC→D: {A,B,C,D,F}. D→E: {A,B,C,D,E,F}.
  (CF)⁺ = {A, B, C, D, E, F} = ALL attributes

  (BC)⁺: Start {B,C}. BC→D: {B,C,D}. D→E: {B,C,D,E}. No more applicable.
  (BC)⁺ = {B, C, D, E}  (missing A, F — NOT all)

ii. Candidate keys:
  A⁺ misses F → A is not a superkey.
  (CF)⁺ = all → CF is a superkey.
  Is CF minimal? C⁺ = {C}, F⁺ = {F}. Neither alone is a superkey.
  So CF is a CANDIDATE KEY.

  (AF)⁺: {A,F} → A→B: {A,B,F} → A→C: {A,B,C,F} → BC→D: {A,B,C,D,F}
         → D→E: {A,B,C,D,E,F} = ALL. AF is a superkey.
  Is AF minimal? A⁺ = {A,B,C,D,E} (missing F). F⁺ = {F}. Neither alone works.
  AF is also a CANDIDATE KEY.

  Candidate Keys: CF, AF

iii. Highest normal form:
  Check BCNF for each FD:
    A → B: A⁺ = {A,B,C,D,E} ≠ all. A is NOT a superkey. VIOLATES BCNF.
    But B is a prime attribute? B is not part of CF or AF. So B is NOT prime.
    VIOLATES 3NF too.

  Wait — let me recheck. Prime attributes = those appearing in ANY candidate key.
  CK₁ = {C, F}, CK₂ = {A, F}. Prime attributes = {A, C, F}.
  Non-prime = {B, D, E}.

    A → B: A is not a superkey, B is not prime. VIOLATES 3NF.
    A → C: A is not a superkey, C IS prime. 3NF satisfied ✓ but BCNF violated ✗.
    BC → D: (BC)⁺ = {B,C,D,E} ≠ all. Not superkey. D not prime. VIOLATES 3NF.
    D → E: D not superkey, E not prime. VIOLATES 3NF.
    CF → A: CF is a superkey ✓

  The relation is NOT in 3NF. Since all values are atomic, it is in 1NF.

iv. BCNF Decomposition:

  Start with R(A, B, C, D, E, F), violating FD: A → B.
  Decompose:
    R₁(A, B)      — PK = A
    R₂(A, C, D, E, F) — remaining

  In R₂, project FDs: A→C, BC→D becomes A,C→D (since B removed but A→B so
  effectively A→C→... check): A→C, apply: AD→? Actually need to recompute.

  FDs on R₂: A→C, A→D (from A→BC, BC→D, so A→D transitively), D→E, CF→A.
  So: A→C, A→D, D→E, CF→A.

  R₂: A→C, A is not superkey in R₂? (AF)⁺ projected = {A,C,D,E,F} = all of R₂.
  (CF)⁺ projected: CF→A, A→C, A→D, D→E = {A,C,D,E,F} = all. Both AF and CF
  are keys of R₂.

  A→C: A not superkey (A⁺ in R₂ = {A,C,D,E} missing F). Violates BCNF.

  Decompose R₂ on A→C:
    R₃(A, C) PK = A
    R₄(A, D, E, F) — FDs: A→D, D→E, and for CF→A we lost C... check.

  In R₄: A→D, D→E.
  (AF)⁺ = {A,D,E,F} = all of R₄. AF is key.
  A→D: A not superkey. Violates BCNF.

  Decompose R₄ on A→D:
    R₅(A, D) PK = A
    R₆(A, E, F) — FD: check — A→E (from A→D→E transitively)? In R₆ we have
    D→E but D not in R₆. So no FD applies. PK = (A, F) since AF was a CK.

  Wait, actually in R₆(A, E, F): From original FDs on these attributes only,
  do we have any? A→E (A→D→E, but D projected out — FD may not project).
  We need to check: A⁺ in original = {A,B,C,D,E}. Projected onto {A,E,F}: {A,E}.
  So A → E holds on R₆. A is not superkey (missing F). BCNF violated.

  Decompose R₆ on A→E:
    R₇(A, E) PK = A
    R₈(A, F) PK = (A, F) — no non-trivial FDs. ✓ BCNF.

  FINAL BCNF DECOMPOSITION:
    R₁(A, B)    PK = A
    R₃(A, C)    PK = A
    R₅(A, D)    PK = A
    R₇(A, E)    PK = A
    R₈(A, F)    PK = (A, F)

  This is lossless but note that R₁, R₃, R₅, R₇ can be combined since they
  all have PK = A:

  SIMPLIFIED BCNF:
    R_main(A PK, B, C, D, E)    — combines R₁, R₃, R₅, R₇
    R_AF(A, F)                   — PK = (A, F)

  Verify: In R_main, A→B, A→C, A→D, A→E — A is PK, all satisfied ✓
  In R_AF: no non-trivial FDs. ✓

  Note: The FD CF→A is lost in this decomposition (not dependency-preserving),
  which is a known trade-off of BCNF decomposition.


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 4 (b) Trigger to validate grade and log changes.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Assuming:
  enrollment(roll_no, course_id, grade)
  grade_change_log(log_id SERIAL, roll_no, course_id, old_grade, new_grade,
                   changed_at TIMESTAMP)

  CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION validate_and_log_grade()
  RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
  BEGIN
      -- Validate the new grade
      IF NEW.grade < 0 OR NEW.grade > 100 THEN
          RAISE EXCEPTION 'Invalid grade: %. Must be between 0 and 100.',
                          NEW.grade;
      END IF;

      -- Log the change
      INSERT INTO grade_change_log (roll_no, course_id, old_grade,
                                     new_grade, changed_at)
      VALUES (OLD.roll_no, OLD.course_id, OLD.grade,
              NEW.grade, CURRENT_TIMESTAMP);

      RETURN NEW;
  END;
  $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

  CREATE TRIGGER trg_grade_update
  BEFORE UPDATE OF grade ON enrollment
  FOR EACH ROW
  EXECUTE FUNCTION validate_and_log_grade();

This trigger:
  1. Fires BEFORE an UPDATE on the grade column of enrollment.
  2. Checks if the new grade is between 0 and 100; if not, raises an exception
     which rolls back the update.
  3. If valid, logs the old and new grade values with a timestamp.
  4. Returns NEW to allow the update to proceed.



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 5 (a) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Need for database security. Security violations vs integrity
         violations.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

DATABASE SECURITY refers to the collection of policies, mechanisms, and controls
that protect a database against unauthorized access, misuse, modification, or
destruction. In today's world, databases store critical information — financial
records, medical histories, government data, personal information — and any
breach can have devastating consequences including financial loss, legal
liability, loss of reputation, and violation of privacy laws.

Why database security is needed:
  • Protecting sensitive data (PII, financial records, medical records).
  • Compliance with regulations (GDPR, HIPAA, PCI-DSS, Nepal's Privacy Act).
  • Preventing unauthorized access, both external (hackers) and internal
    (disgruntled employees).
  • Maintaining data integrity — ensuring data is not tampered with.
  • Ensuring availability — preventing denial-of-service attacks.

SECURITY VIOLATIONS vs INTEGRITY VIOLATIONS:

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect               │ Security Violation       │ Integrity Violation      │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Definition           │ Unauthorized access to   │ Data becomes incorrect   │
  │                      │ or manipulation of data  │ or inconsistent          │
  │ Nature               │ About WHO can do WHAT    │ About WHAT data is valid │
  │ Cause                │ Malicious intent or      │ Software bugs, human     │
  │                      │ access control failure   │ error, constraint gaps   │
  │ Example              │ A student accessing the  │ A student's age stored   │
  │                      │ grades table to change   │ as -5 due to missing     │
  │                      │ their own grades         │ CHECK constraint         │
  │ Another example      │ SQL injection to extract │ Deleting a department    │
  │                      │ all user passwords       │ without updating FK refs │
  │ Prevention           │ Authentication,          │ Constraints (domain,     │
  │                      │ authorization, RBAC,     │ entity, referential),    │
  │                      │ encryption, auditing     │ triggers, assertions     │
  │ Detected by          │ Audit logs, IDS/IPS      │ DBMS constraint checks   │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

Types of security violations:
  1. Unauthorized reading (confidentiality breach) — e.g., reading salary data.
  2. Unauthorized modification — e.g., changing account balances.
  3. Unauthorized destruction — e.g., deleting critical records.
  4. Unauthorized disclosure — e.g., leaking customer data.

Types of integrity violations:
  1. Domain violation — storing "abc" in an INT column.
  2. Entity integrity violation — NULL in a primary key.
  3. Referential integrity violation — FK pointing to non-existent PK.
  4. Semantic integrity violation — logically incorrect data (salary < 0).


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Access control: MAC vs DAC.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Access control is a security mechanism that determines WHO can access WHAT
resources and WHAT operations they can perform. It is the cornerstone of database
security, ensuring that only authorized users can read, modify, or delete data.

DISCRETIONARY ACCESS CONTROL (DAC):

In DAC, the owner of an object (table, view, etc.) has full control over who
can access it. The owner can GRANT or REVOKE privileges to/from other users at
their discretion. This is the model used by most commercial RDBMS (PostgreSQL,
MySQL, Oracle).

Characteristics of DAC:
  • Owner-controlled: The creator of a table decides who can access it.
  • Flexible: Privileges can be granted with or without GRANT OPTION.
  • SQL-based: Uses GRANT and REVOKE statements.
  • Risk: A user who receives access can potentially pass it on (if given
    WITH GRANT OPTION), creating privilege chains that are hard to track.

Example:
  GRANT SELECT, INSERT ON employee TO user_hr;
  GRANT SELECT ON employee TO user_manager WITH GRANT OPTION;
  -- user_manager can now grant SELECT on employee to others

MANDATORY ACCESS CONTROL (MAC):

In MAC, the system (not individual users) enforces access policies based on
SECURITY LABELS (classifications) assigned to both data objects and users. Users
cannot override or modify these labels — the policy is mandatory.

The Bell-LaPadula Model is the classic MAC model:
  • Security levels (in ascending order): Unclassified < Confidential <
    Secret < Top Secret.
  • Each data object has a CLASSIFICATION (e.g., "Secret").
  • Each user has a CLEARANCE (e.g., "Confidential").
  • Rules:
    - NO READ UP: A user can read only objects at or below their clearance.
      (A Confidential user cannot read Secret data.)
    - NO WRITE DOWN: A user can write only to objects at or above their level.
      (Prevents leaking classified information to lower levels.)

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ DAC                      │ MAC                      │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Policy set by    │ Data owner               │ System administrator     │
  │ Flexibility      │ High                     │ Low (rigid)              │
  │ Security level   │ Lower                    │ Higher                   │
  │ Implementation   │ GRANT/REVOKE             │ Security labels          │
  │ Override         │ Owner can change          │ Users CANNOT change      │
  │ Use case         │ Commercial applications  │ Military, government     │
  │ SQL support      │ Widely supported          │ Oracle Label Security,   │
  │                  │                          │ SELinux (limited)        │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Authorization in database security. Privilege types and
         GRANT/REVOKE.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Authorization is the process of determining WHAT operations a given user is
permitted to perform on WHICH database objects. After a user is authenticated
(identity verified), the DBMS checks authorization before executing any
operation.

TYPES OF AUTHORIZATION PRIVILEGES:

1. READ (SELECT): Permission to read data from a table or view.
   A user with SELECT privilege can query the table but cannot modify it.

2. INSERT: Permission to add new tuples to a table.
   Can be restricted to specific columns:
   GRANT INSERT (name, email) ON student TO user_clerk;

3. UPDATE: Permission to modify existing data.
   Can also be column-specific:
   GRANT UPDATE (salary) ON employee TO user_hr;

4. DELETE: Permission to remove tuples from a table.

5. REFERENCES: Permission to create foreign keys that reference a table.
   GRANT REFERENCES (dept_id) ON department TO user_admin;

6. INDEX: Permission to create indices on a table (some RDBMS).

7. ALL PRIVILEGES: Grants all available privileges at once.

GRANTING PRIVILEGES:

  GRANT privilege_list ON object TO user_list [WITH GRANT OPTION];

  Examples:
  GRANT SELECT ON employee TO user_hr;
  GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON student TO user_registrar;
  GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON course TO user_admin;
  GRANT SELECT ON employee TO user_manager WITH GRANT OPTION;
    -- user_manager can now grant SELECT to others

REVOKING PRIVILEGES:

  REVOKE privilege_list ON object FROM user_list [CASCADE | RESTRICT];

  Examples:
  REVOKE INSERT ON student FROM user_clerk;
  REVOKE SELECT ON employee FROM user_manager CASCADE;
    -- Also revokes from anyone user_manager had granted to
  REVOKE ALL PRIVILEGES ON course FROM user_admin;

Authorization graph: When privileges are granted with GRANT OPTION, a directed
graph forms where nodes are users and edges represent granted privileges. REVOKE
CASCADE traverses this graph, revoking from all downstream users.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Views as security mechanisms.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A VIEW is a virtual table derived from one or more base tables through a query.
Views do not store data themselves but provide a window into the underlying data.
One of the most important uses of views is as a SECURITY MECHANISM — they allow
database administrators to restrict what data users can see and modify, without
changing the underlying tables.

How views provide security:

1. COLUMN-LEVEL RESTRICTION: A view can expose only certain columns, hiding
   sensitive data.

   Example: An employee table has sensitive salary and SSN data.

   CREATE VIEW employee_public AS
   SELECT emp_id, name, dept, position
   FROM employee;
   -- Hides: salary, ssn, address, phone

   GRANT SELECT ON employee_public TO role_general_staff;
   -- General staff can see employee info but NOT salaries or SSNs.

2. ROW-LEVEL RESTRICTION: A view can filter rows using a WHERE clause.

   Example: A department manager should see only employees in their department.

   CREATE VIEW dept_cs_employees AS
   SELECT emp_id, name, salary, position
   FROM employee
   WHERE dept = 'CS';

   GRANT SELECT ON dept_cs_employees TO user_cs_head;
   -- The CS head can only see CS department employees.

3. AGGREGATION HIDING: A view can show only summaries, not individual records.

   CREATE VIEW dept_salary_summary AS
   SELECT dept, COUNT(*) AS emp_count, AVG(salary) AS avg_salary
   FROM employee
   GROUP BY dept;

   -- Users see department-level statistics but cannot see individual salaries.

4. UPDATABLE VIEWS with CHECK OPTION: Ensure that any data modification through
   the view still satisfies the view's WHERE clause.

   CREATE VIEW active_students AS
   SELECT * FROM student WHERE status = 'Active'
   WITH CHECK OPTION;
   -- Users cannot insert/update a student with status ≠ 'Active' through this view.

Practical example — Hospital database:

  Base table: patient(patient_id, name, dob, blood_type, diagnosis,
                      treatment, billing_amount, insurance_id)

  View for doctors:
  CREATE VIEW doctor_view AS
  SELECT patient_id, name, dob, blood_type, diagnosis, treatment
  FROM patient;
  -- Doctors see medical info but NOT billing/insurance.

  View for billing staff:
  CREATE VIEW billing_view AS
  SELECT patient_id, name, billing_amount, insurance_id
  FROM patient;
  -- Billing staff see financial info but NOT diagnosis/treatment.


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) GRANT and REVOKE commands with examples.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

The GRANT and REVOKE commands are SQL statements used to manage authorization
privileges in a relational database. They implement Discretionary Access Control
(DAC), where the owner of a database object controls who can access it.

GRANT COMMAND:

Syntax:
  GRANT privilege_list ON object TO user_or_role [WITH GRANT OPTION];

  • privilege_list: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, REFERENCES, ALL PRIVILEGES
  • object: table name, view name, or schema
  • WITH GRANT OPTION: allows the grantee to further grant the same privilege

Examples:

  -- Grant read access on student table to user 'registrar'
  GRANT SELECT ON student TO registrar;

  -- Grant insert and update on specific columns
  GRANT INSERT, UPDATE (grade) ON enrollment TO faculty;

  -- Grant all privileges to admin
  GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON student TO admin;

  -- Grant with the ability to pass on the privilege
  GRANT SELECT ON student TO dept_head WITH GRANT OPTION;

  -- Grant to a role (group of users)
  CREATE ROLE read_only;
  GRANT SELECT ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO read_only;
  GRANT read_only TO user_intern;

REVOKE COMMAND:

Syntax:
  REVOKE privilege_list ON object FROM user_or_role [CASCADE | RESTRICT];

  • CASCADE: Also revokes from all users who received the privilege transitively.
  • RESTRICT: Fails if any dependent grants exist (safer option).

Examples:

  -- Revoke insert privilege
  REVOKE INSERT ON student FROM registrar;

  -- Revoke all privileges
  REVOKE ALL PRIVILEGES ON student FROM admin;

  -- Cascade revoke: if dept_head granted SELECT to others, those are also revoked
  REVOKE SELECT ON student FROM dept_head CASCADE;

  -- Restrict: fails if dept_head has granted to others
  REVOKE SELECT ON student FROM dept_head RESTRICT;

  -- Revoke role from user
  REVOKE read_only FROM user_intern;

Authorization graph example:

  DBA → grants SELECT to A (WITH GRANT OPTION)
  A → grants SELECT to B (WITH GRANT OPTION)
  B → grants SELECT to C

  REVOKE SELECT FROM A CASCADE;
  Result: A, B, and C all lose SELECT privilege.


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) with hospital example.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

ROLE-BASED ACCESS CONTROL (RBAC) is a security model that assigns permissions
to ROLES rather than to individual users. Users are then assigned to roles, and
they inherit the permissions associated with those roles. This simplifies
administration because instead of managing privileges for each of hundreds of
users individually, the DBA defines a handful of roles and assigns users to them.

Key concepts:
  • ROLE: A named collection of privileges (e.g., "doctor", "nurse", "admin").
  • USER-ROLE ASSIGNMENT: Mapping users to roles.
  • PERMISSION-ROLE ASSIGNMENT: Mapping privileges to roles.
  • ROLE HIERARCHY: Roles can inherit from other roles.

Hospital Database Example:

Tables:
  patient(patient_id, name, dob, blood_type, diagnosis, treatment,
          billing_amount, insurance_id, assigned_doctor)
  prescription(rx_id, patient_id, doctor_id, medicine, dosage, date)
  billing(bill_id, patient_id, amount, status, payment_date)
  staff(staff_id, name, role, department)

Step 1: Create roles

  CREATE ROLE doctor;
  CREATE ROLE nurse;
  CREATE ROLE billing_clerk;
  CREATE ROLE hospital_admin;

Step 2: Assign privileges to roles

  -- Doctors: full access to patient medical data, prescriptions
  GRANT SELECT, UPDATE ON patient TO doctor;
  GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON prescription TO doctor;
  GRANT SELECT (patient_id, name, billing_amount) ON billing TO doctor;

  -- Nurses: read medical data, update treatment/vitals only
  GRANT SELECT ON patient TO nurse;
  GRANT UPDATE (treatment) ON patient TO nurse;
  GRANT SELECT ON prescription TO nurse;
  -- Nurses CANNOT see billing or insurance info

  -- Billing clerks: access only billing and basic patient info
  GRANT SELECT (patient_id, name) ON patient TO billing_clerk;
  GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON billing TO billing_clerk;
  -- Billing clerks CANNOT see diagnosis, treatment, or prescriptions

  -- Admin: everything
  GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO hospital_admin;

Step 3: Assign users to roles

  GRANT doctor TO user_dr_sharma, user_dr_poudel;
  GRANT nurse TO user_nurse_maya, user_nurse_rita;
  GRANT billing_clerk TO user_clerk_hari;
  GRANT hospital_admin TO user_admin_ram;

Step 4: Role hierarchy (optional)

  GRANT nurse TO doctor;  -- Doctors inherit all nurse privileges
  GRANT doctor TO hospital_admin;  -- Admin inherits all doctor privileges

  ┌───────────────────┐
  │  hospital_admin   │ ← has ALL privileges
  └────────┬──────────┘
           │ inherits
  ┌────────┴──────────┐
  │     doctor        │ ← medical + limited billing + nurse privileges
  └────────┬──────────┘
           │ inherits
  ┌────────┴──────────┐
  │      nurse        │ ← read patient, update treatment
  └───────────────────┘

  ┌───────────────────┐
  │  billing_clerk    │ ← billing + basic patient info (separate branch)
  └───────────────────┘


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Encryption mechanisms: symmetric and asymmetric key.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Encryption is the process of converting plaintext data into ciphertext using a
mathematical algorithm and a KEY, so that the data cannot be read by unauthorized
parties. Only those with the correct decryption key can convert the ciphertext
back to plaintext. Encryption provides CONFIDENTIALITY — even if an attacker
gains access to the encrypted data, they cannot read it without the key.

1. SYMMETRIC KEY ENCRYPTION (Secret Key Encryption):

  The SAME key is used for both encryption and decryption. Both the sender and
  receiver must share the secret key securely.

  Plaintext → [Encrypt with Key K] → Ciphertext → [Decrypt with Key K] → Plaintext

  Algorithms: AES (Advanced Encryption Standard), DES, 3DES, Blowfish.

  Advantages:
  • Very fast (suitable for encrypting large volumes of data).
  • Simple implementation.
  • Low computational overhead.

  Disadvantages:
  • KEY DISTRIBUTION PROBLEM: How do you securely share the key?
  • KEY MANAGEMENT: With N users, you need N(N-1)/2 keys for pairwise
    communication.
  • If the key is compromised, all encrypted data is exposed.

  Use in databases:
  • Transparent Data Encryption (TDE) — encrypts entire database files at rest
    using AES. Used by Oracle, SQL Server, PostgreSQL.
  • Column-level encryption — encrypting specific sensitive columns (SSN, credit
    card numbers) using AES.

2. ASYMMETRIC KEY ENCRYPTION (Public Key Encryption):

  Uses a PAIR of mathematically related keys:
  • PUBLIC KEY: Shared openly, used to ENCRYPT data.
  • PRIVATE KEY: Kept secret, used to DECRYPT data.

  Anyone can encrypt with the public key, but ONLY the private key holder can
  decrypt.

  Plaintext → [Encrypt with Public Key] → Ciphertext
  Ciphertext → [Decrypt with Private Key] → Plaintext

  Algorithms: RSA, ECC (Elliptic Curve Cryptography), DSA.

  Advantages:
  • No key distribution problem — public key can be shared freely.
  • Supports DIGITAL SIGNATURES (sign with private key, verify with public key).
  • Better scalability: N users need only N key pairs (not N²).

  Disadvantages:
  • Much SLOWER than symmetric encryption (100-1000× slower).
  • Computationally expensive for large data volumes.

  Use in databases:
  • Encrypting the symmetric key itself (hybrid approach): data is encrypted
    with a fast symmetric key (AES), and the symmetric key is encrypted with
    RSA. This combines the speed of symmetric encryption with the key management
    benefits of asymmetric.
  • SSL/TLS connections between client and database server use asymmetric
    encryption for key exchange, then switch to symmetric for data transfer.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Symmetric                │ Asymmetric               │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Keys             │ One shared key            │ Public + Private pair    │
  │ Speed            │ Fast                      │ Slow                     │
  │ Key distribution │ Problematic               │ Easy (public key shared) │
  │ Use case         │ Data-at-rest encryption   │ Key exchange, signatures │
  │ Algorithms       │ AES, DES, 3DES            │ RSA, ECC, DSA            │
  │ Scalability      │ Poor (N² keys)            │ Good (N key pairs)       │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Security threats and SQL injection.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

TYPES OF SECURITY THREATS:

1. UNAUTHORIZED DISCLOSURE (Confidentiality Threat):
   An attacker reads data they should not have access to.
   Example: Accessing salary records through a misconfigured view.

2. UNAUTHORIZED MODIFICATION (Integrity Threat):
   An attacker alters data without permission.
   Example: Changing a student's grade in the enrollment table.

3. DENIAL OF SERVICE (Availability Threat):
   An attacker makes the database unavailable to legitimate users.
   Example: Flooding the DB server with millions of complex queries.

4. SQL INJECTION (Combined Threat):
   An attacker injects malicious SQL code through application inputs.
   This can lead to unauthorized reading, modification, or deletion.

SQL INJECTION — How it works:

Consider a login form where the application constructs the SQL query by
concatenating user input:

  Application code (Python — VULNERABLE):
  query = "SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = '" + user_input + "'
           AND password = '" + pass_input + "'"

  Normal usage:
  user_input = "aarav"
  pass_input = "secret123"
  Query becomes:
  SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = 'aarav' AND password = 'secret123'

  ATTACK — attacker enters:
  user_input = "' OR '1'='1' --"
  pass_input = "anything"
  Query becomes:
  SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = '' OR '1'='1' --' AND password = 'anything'

  The -- comments out the rest. '1'='1' is always TRUE, so ALL rows are returned.
  The attacker is logged in without a valid password.

  More dangerous attack:
  user_input = "'; DROP TABLE users; --"
  Query becomes:
  SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = ''; DROP TABLE users; --' AND ...

  This DELETES the entire users table!

PREVENTION OF SQL INJECTION:

1. PARAMETERIZED QUERIES (Prepared Statements) — THE BEST DEFENSE:
   cursor.execute("SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = %s AND password = %s",
                  (user_input, pass_input))
   The database driver handles escaping — user input is NEVER interpreted as SQL.

2. INPUT VALIDATION: Reject inputs containing SQL keywords or special characters.

3. STORED PROCEDURES: Encapsulate queries in the database, reducing exposure.

4. LEAST PRIVILEGE: The application database user should have only the minimum
   necessary privileges (no DROP, no GRANT).

5. WEB APPLICATION FIREWALLS (WAF): Detect and block SQL injection patterns.

6. ORM USAGE: Frameworks like Django ORM, SQLAlchemy automatically use
   parameterized queries.


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Components of a database security system. Views as security.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A database security system consists of multiple layers working together:

1. AUTHENTICATION: Verifying the identity of a user.
   Methods: username/password, Kerberos, LDAP, certificates, biometrics.
   "WHO are you?"

2. AUTHORIZATION: Determining what an authenticated user is allowed to do.
   Implemented through GRANT/REVOKE, roles, privileges.
   "WHAT can you do?"

3. ACCESS CONTROL: The mechanism that enforces authorization policies.
   Types: DAC (owner-controlled), MAC (system-controlled), RBAC (role-based).
   "HOW are permissions enforced?"

4. ENCRYPTION: Protecting data confidentiality at rest and in transit.
   At rest: TDE, column-level encryption.
   In transit: SSL/TLS connections.

5. AUDITING: Recording who did what, when, and from where.
   Audit logs, audit trails, triggers for logging.

6. INTEGRITY CONTROLS: Ensuring data correctness.
   Constraints, triggers, assertions.

7. BACKUP AND RECOVERY: Protecting against data loss.
   Regular backups, point-in-time recovery, transaction logs.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                 Database Security System             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Layer 1: Authentication (identity verification)    │
  │  Layer 2: Authorization (privilege checking)        │
  │  Layer 3: Access Control (DAC/MAC/RBAC enforcement) │
  │  Layer 4: Encryption (data protection)              │
  │  Layer 5: Auditing (activity logging)               │
  │  Layer 6: Integrity Controls (data validation)      │
  │  Layer 7: Backup & Recovery (data preservation)     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

VIEWS AS SECURITY MECHANISM:

Views act as a security layer by exposing only a subset of the data:

Example:
  Base table: employee(emp_id, name, dept, salary, ssn, address, performance)

  -- View for managers: see their department only, no SSN
  CREATE VIEW dept_view AS
  SELECT emp_id, name, salary, performance
  FROM employee
  WHERE dept = CURRENT_USER;  -- PostgreSQL: restricts to logged-in dept

  -- View for public directory: name and department only
  CREATE VIEW public_directory AS
  SELECT emp_id, name, dept FROM employee;

  -- Revoke all access to base table, grant only view access
  REVOKE ALL ON employee FROM PUBLIC;
  GRANT SELECT ON dept_view TO role_manager;
  GRANT SELECT ON public_directory TO PUBLIC;


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (a) Authentication vs Authorization. Multi-factor authentication.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

AUTHENTICATION is the process of verifying the IDENTITY of a user — confirming
that the user is who they claim to be. AUTHORIZATION is the process of
determining what an authenticated user is PERMITTED to do — what data they can
access and what operations they can perform.

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect               │ Authentication           │ Authorization            │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Question answered    │ "Who are you?"           │ "What can you do?"       │
  │ When it occurs       │ Before authorization     │ After authentication     │
  │ Mechanism            │ Password, biometrics,    │ GRANT/REVOKE, roles,     │
  │                      │ tokens, certificates     │ access control lists     │
  │ Failure result       │ Login denied             │ Permission denied        │
  │ Controlled by        │ System/network admin     │ DBA / data owner         │
  │ SQL example          │ CREATE USER WITH PASSWORD│ GRANT SELECT ON table    │
  │ Can change?          │ User can change password │ Only DBA/owner can       │
  │                      │                          │ change privileges        │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

MULTI-FACTOR AUTHENTICATION (MFA):

MFA requires users to provide TWO OR MORE independent authentication factors
from different categories before access is granted. The three categories are:

  1. SOMETHING YOU KNOW: Password, PIN, security question.
  2. SOMETHING YOU HAVE: Phone (OTP), hardware token, smart card.
  3. SOMETHING YOU ARE: Fingerprint, face recognition, iris scan.

How MFA applies to database access:

In enterprise environments, direct database access typically uses:
  • Factor 1 (Know): Database username and password.
  • Factor 2 (Have): OTP from an authenticator app (Google Authenticator, Duo)
    or a hardware security key (YubiKey).
  • Factor 3 (Are): Biometric verification (less common for DB access, more
    common for OS-level or VPN access).

Example workflow:
  1. DBA connects to PostgreSQL using psql.
  2. Enters username + password (Factor 1).
  3. System sends OTP to registered phone (Factor 2).
  4. DBA enters OTP.
  5. Connection established only if both factors are correct.

Benefits:
  • Even if a password is stolen, the attacker cannot access the DB without
    the second factor.
