  • Greatly reduces risk of brute-force attacks.
  • Required by many compliance frameworks (PCI-DSS, HIPAA).



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 5 (b) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Query processing — basic steps with diagram.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Query processing refers to the entire sequence of activities involved in
extracting data from a database in response to an SQL query. The DBMS must
translate a high-level declarative SQL statement into a low-level sequence of
operations that can be efficiently executed on the stored data.

BASIC STEPS:

  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │   SQL Query          │
  │   (User Input)       │
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │
             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  Step 1: PARSING &   │
  │  TRANSLATION         │
  │  • Syntax checking   │
  │  • Semantic checking  │
  │  • Convert SQL to    │
  │    internal RA form  │
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │ (Relational Algebra Expression)
             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  Step 2: OPTIMIZATION│
  │  • Generate multiple │
  │    equivalent plans  │
  │  • Estimate cost of  │
  │    each plan         │
  │  • Select cheapest   │
  │    plan              │
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │ (Optimized Execution Plan)
             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  Step 3: EVALUATION  │
  │  • Execute the plan  │
  │  • Access storage    │
  │  • Apply operators   │
  │  • Return results    │
  └──────────┬───────────┘
             │
             ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │   Query Result       │
  └──────────────────────┘

DETAILED EXPLANATION:

Step 1 — PARSING AND TRANSLATION:
  • The PARSER checks the SQL query for syntactic correctness (valid SQL grammar).
  • The SEMANTIC ANALYZER checks that referenced tables and columns exist, data
    types are compatible, and the user has authorization.
  • The translator converts the SQL into an INTERNAL REPRESENTATION, typically a
    relational algebra expression or a query tree.
  • Example: SELECT name FROM student WHERE dept = 'CS'
    becomes: π_(name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student))

Step 2 — OPTIMIZATION:
  • A single SQL query can be executed in many different ways (different join
    orders, different algorithms, different index usage).
  • The QUERY OPTIMIZER evaluates multiple EQUIVALENT expressions and EVALUATION
    PLANS, estimates the COST of each (in terms of disk I/O, CPU, memory), and
    selects the plan with the lowest estimated cost.
  • Techniques: pushing selections down, reordering joins, choosing between
    nested-loop join, hash join, and merge join.

Step 3 — EVALUATION:
  • The QUERY EXECUTION ENGINE executes the chosen plan.
  • It accesses the appropriate files, applies the relational operators, and
    produces the result.
  • Results are returned to the user or application.


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Query optimization: cost-based vs heuristic-based.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Query optimization is the process of selecting the most efficient execution plan
for a given query from among multiple equivalent alternatives. The goal is to
minimize the total COST of query execution (primarily disk I/O).

COST-BASED OPTIMIZATION:

Cost-based optimization evaluates multiple execution plans and estimates the COST
of each using statistical information about the data. It selects the plan with
the lowest estimated cost.

Components:
  • STATISTICS: The optimizer maintains metadata about tables:
    - nᵣ: number of tuples in relation r
    - bᵣ: number of blocks occupied by r
    - fᵣ: blocking factor (tuples per block)
    - V(A, r): number of distinct values of attribute A in r
    - Height of B⁺ tree indices
  • COST MODEL: Formulas that estimate the cost of each operation:
    - Linear scan: cost = bᵣ
    - Binary search: cost = ⌈log₂(bᵣ)⌉
    - B⁺ tree index lookup: cost = height + 1
    - Nested-loop join: cost = bᵣ × bₛ (worst case)
  • PLAN ENUMERATION: The optimizer generates many equivalent plans and
    evaluates the cost of each.

Advantage: Produces better plans (closer to truly optimal).
Disadvantage: Expensive itself — evaluating all plans takes time.

HEURISTIC-BASED OPTIMIZATION:

Heuristic-based optimization applies a set of RULES (heuristics) that are known
to generally improve query performance, WITHOUT estimating actual costs. These
rules transform the query tree into a more efficient form.

Common heuristic rules:
  1. PUSH SELECTIONS DOWN: Apply σ as early as possible to reduce tuple count.
  2. PUSH PROJECTIONS DOWN: Apply π early to reduce column count.
  3. COMBINE SELECTIONS: σ_(A ∧ B) is better than σ_A(σ_B(...)).
  4. PERFORM MOST RESTRICTIVE SELECTIONS FIRST: The selection that eliminates
     the most tuples should be applied first.
  5. AVOID CARTESIAN PRODUCTS: Replace × followed by σ with ⋈.
  6. REORDER JOINS: Join smaller intermediate results first.

Advantage: Fast to apply — no need for statistics or cost estimation.
Disadvantage: May not find the truly optimal plan.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Cost-Based               │ Heuristic-Based          │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Approach         │ Estimate cost of plans   │ Apply transformation     │
  │                  │                          │ rules                    │
  │ Uses statistics  │ Yes                      │ No                       │
  │ Quality of plan  │ Near-optimal             │ Generally good           │
  │ Optimization time│ Higher                   │ Lower                    │
  │ Complex queries  │ Better                   │ May miss good plans      │
  │ Used by          │ Modern RDBMS (PostgreSQL,│ Simpler systems,         │
  │                  │ Oracle, SQL Server)      │ first-pass optimization  │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

Most modern RDBMS use a COMBINATION: heuristic rules first (to prune the search
space), then cost-based evaluation of the remaining plans.


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Equivalence of expressions. Transformation rules.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Two relational algebra expressions are EQUIVALENT if they produce the SAME result
on every valid database instance. The query optimizer exploits equivalences to
transform a query into a form that is more efficient to execute, without changing
the result.

TRANSFORMATION RULES (Equivalence Rules):

1. SELECTION CASCADE:
   σ_(θ₁ ∧ θ₂)(E) ≡ σ_(θ₁)(σ_(θ₂)(E))

   A conjunctive selection can be broken into a sequence of individual selections.
   Example: σ_(dept='CS' ∧ age>20)(student)
          ≡ σ_(dept='CS')(σ_(age>20)(student))

2. SELECTION COMMUTATIVITY:
   σ_(θ₁)(σ_(θ₂)(E)) ≡ σ_(θ₂)(σ_(θ₁)(E))

   The order of selections does not matter.

3. PROJECTION CASCADE:
   π_(L₁)(π_(L₂)(…(π_(Lₙ)(E))…)) ≡ π_(L₁)(E)

   Only the outermost projection matters (if L₁ ⊆ L₂ ⊆ … ⊆ Lₙ).

4. SELECTION-CARTESIAN PRODUCT TO JOIN:
   σ_(θ)(E₁ × E₂) ≡ E₁ ⋈_(θ) E₂

   A selection on a Cartesian product is equivalent to a theta join.

5. JOIN COMMUTATIVITY:
   E₁ ⋈ E₂ ≡ E₂ ⋈ E₁

6. JOIN ASSOCIATIVITY:
   (E₁ ⋈ E₂) ⋈ E₃ ≡ E₁ ⋈ (E₂ ⋈ E₃)

7. SELECTION DISTRIBUTION OVER JOIN:
   If θ involves only attributes of E₁:
   σ_(θ)(E₁ ⋈ E₂) ≡ σ_(θ)(E₁) ⋈ E₂

   Push the selection down to the relevant operand.

8. PROJECTION DISTRIBUTION OVER JOIN:
   π_(L₁ ∪ L₂)(E₁ ⋈ E₂) ≡ π_(L₁)(E₁) ⋈ π_(L₂)(E₂)
   (Only if join attributes are included in L₁ and L₂.)

EXAMPLE:
  Original: π_(name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student ⋈ enrollment ⋈ course))

  Transformation 1 — Push selection down (Rule 7):
    π_(name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student) ⋈ enrollment ⋈ course)
    (dept is an attribute of student, so push σ down to student)

  Transformation 2 — Push projection down:
    π_(name)(π_(roll_no, name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student)) ⋈
             π_(roll_no, course_id)(enrollment) ⋈
             course)

  These transformations reduce the size of intermediate results, making the
  query much faster to execute.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Query cost estimation.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Query cost estimation is the process of predicting how expensive a particular
evaluation plan will be BEFORE actually executing it. The query optimizer uses
these estimates to compare alternative plans and choose the cheapest one.

COST COMPONENTS:

1. DISK I/O COST (Dominant factor):
   The number of disk block reads (and writes) required. Since disk access is
   ~100,000× slower than memory access, this is the PRIMARY cost metric.
   • Sequential I/O: Reading consecutive blocks — faster due to disk head
     locality.
   • Random I/O: Reading scattered blocks — much slower.

2. CPU COST:
   Time spent on in-memory operations: comparisons, hash computations, sorting.
   Usually much smaller than disk I/O cost and sometimes ignored in simple models.

3. COMMUNICATION COST:
   In distributed databases, the cost of transferring data between nodes.
   Proportional to the amount of data transferred over the network.

COST ESTIMATION FOR COMMON OPERATIONS:

Given:
  nᵣ = number of tuples in relation r
  bᵣ = number of blocks (bᵣ = ⌈nᵣ / fᵣ⌉, where fᵣ = blocking factor)
  V(A, r) = number of distinct values of attribute A

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Operation              │ Estimated Cost (in block transfers)        │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Linear scan (full)     │ bᵣ                                        │
  │ Linear scan (key)      │ bᵣ / 2 (on average, for unique key)       │
  │ Binary search          │ ⌈log₂(bᵣ)⌉                               │
  │ B⁺ tree index (equal)  │ hᵢ + 1 (hᵢ = height of index)            │
  │ B⁺ tree range query    │ hᵢ + number of leaf blocks in range       │
  │ Hash index (equal)     │ 1 (best case) to 1 + overflow blocks      │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Nested-Loop Join       │ nᵣ × bₛ + bᵣ  (tuple-level)              │
  │ Block Nested-Loop Join │ bᵣ × bₛ + bᵣ  (much better)              │
  │ Indexed Nested-Loop    │ bᵣ + nᵣ × c   (c = index lookup cost)    │
  │ Sort-Merge Join        │ bᵣ log bᵣ + bₛ log bₛ + bᵣ + bₛ          │
  │ Hash Join              │ 3(bᵣ + bₛ) + 4 × nₕ (nₕ = partitions)   │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

SELECTIVITY ESTIMATION:

For σ_(A=v)(r): estimated tuples = nᵣ / V(A, r)
For σ_(A>v)(r): estimated tuples = nᵣ × (max(A) - v) / (max(A) - min(A))

The optimizer uses these estimates to calculate intermediate result sizes,
which feed into join cost estimates.


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Airline schema — RA expression, initial and optimized query tree.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Query: "Find the names of all passengers who have booked a flight operated by
'Nepal Airlines'."

Relational Algebra Expression:
  π_(name)(passenger ⋈ booking ⋈ σ_(airline='Nepal Airlines')(flight))

INITIAL (UNOPTIMIZED) QUERY TREE:

  The naive approach: first compute the Cartesian product / natural join of all
  three tables, then apply the selection, then project.

                    π_(name)
                       │
                  σ_(airline='Nepal Airlines')
                       │
                      ⋈ (natural join)
                     / \
                   ⋈     flight
                  / \
          passenger  booking

  Cost problem: The bottom join (passenger ⋈ booking) produces a LARGE
  intermediate result containing all bookings with passenger info, before
  the selection on airline filters anything.

OPTIMIZATION — Apply Heuristic Transformation Rules:

Rule 1: PUSH SELECTION DOWN
  The selection σ_(airline='Nepal Airlines') involves only the attribute 'airline'
  from the flight table. Push it down to apply directly on flight BEFORE the join.

Rule 2: PUSH PROJECTION DOWN
  We only need 'name' from passenger, 'pass_id' and 'flight_id' from booking,
  and 'flight_id' from flight (after selection). Push projections down to reduce
  column width of intermediate results.

OPTIMIZED QUERY TREE:

                    π_(name)
                       │
                      ⋈ (on pass_id)
                     / \
    π_(pass_id, name)   π_(pass_id)
          │                  │
      passenger             ⋈ (on flight_id)
                           / \
              π_(flight_id)   π_(flight_id)
                    │              │
                 booking    σ_(airline='Nepal Airlines')
                                   │
                                flight

  Why this is better:
  1. σ is applied to flight FIRST, producing only Nepal Airlines flights
     (much smaller than all flights).
  2. This small result is joined with booking (producing only relevant bookings).
  3. Then joined with passenger (only relevant passengers).
  4. Projections remove unnecessary columns at each step.

  The optimized plan dramatically reduces the size of intermediate results.


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Query evaluation plans. Selecting the best plan.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A QUERY EVALUATION PLAN (also called an execution plan) is a specific sequence
of operations, along with the ALGORITHMS chosen for each operation, that the DBMS
will use to execute a query. It specifies not just WHAT operations to perform
(the relational algebra) but HOW to perform each one.

A single relational algebra expression can have multiple evaluation plans because:
  • Different JOIN ALGORITHMS: nested-loop, block nested-loop, hash join,
    sort-merge join.
  • Different ACCESS METHODS: sequential scan, index scan, bitmap scan.
  • Different JOIN ORDERS: (A ⋈ B) ⋈ C vs A ⋈ (B ⋈ C).
  • Whether to use MATERIALIZATION or PIPELINING.

Example: For the query π_(name)(student ⋈ enrollment ⋈ course)

  Plan 1: sequential scan on student, nested-loop join with enrollment,
           nested-loop join with course, then project.
  Plan 2: index scan on enrollment.course_id, sort-merge join with course,
           hash join with student, then project.
  Plan 3: hash join student-enrollment, then hash join with course.

HOW THE OPTIMIZER SELECTS THE BEST PLAN:

  Step 1: Generate all logically equivalent expressions (using transformation
          rules).
  Step 2: For each expression, enumerate possible evaluation plans (different
          algorithms).
  Step 3: Estimate the cost of each plan using statistics.
  Step 4: Select the plan with the LOWEST ESTIMATED COST.

In practice, enumerating ALL plans is exponential (for N tables, there are
N! join orders). The optimizer uses DYNAMIC PROGRAMMING to find the best plan
efficiently:
  • Build optimal plans for single tables.
  • Combine to find best 2-table plans.
  • Extend to 3-table plans, and so on.

MATERIALIZATION vs PIPELINING:
  • Materialization: Each operator produces its ENTIRE result before the next
    operator begins. Results are stored in temporary relations.
  • Pipelining: Tuples flow from one operator to the next WITHOUT being fully
    materialized. Much more memory-efficient.


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Query execution plan. Query tree optimization with heuristics.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A QUERY EXECUTION PLAN is the concrete, optimized plan that the DBMS executes.
It can be viewed in most RDBMS using the EXPLAIN command:

  EXPLAIN SELECT s.name FROM student s
  JOIN enrollment e ON s.roll_no = e.roll_no
  JOIN course c ON e.course_id = c.course_id
  WHERE c.credits > 3;

The output shows: scan types, join algorithms, estimated rows, cost.

QUERY TREE REPRESENTATION:

A QUERY TREE (or operator tree) represents a relational algebra expression as a
tree where:
  • Leaf nodes are base relations.
  • Internal nodes are relational algebra operators (σ, π, ⋈, ×, ∪, etc.).
  • The root produces the final result.
  • Execution flows bottom-up (leaves → root).

EXAMPLE:

Query: "Find names of students enrolled in courses with credits > 3."
  π_(name)(student ⋈ enrollment ⋈ σ_(credits>3)(course))

INITIAL QUERY TREE (naive — no optimization):

              π_(name)
                 │
            σ_(credits > 3)
                 │
                ⋈
               / \
              ⋈   course
             / \
       student  enrollment

HEURISTIC OPTIMIZATION STEPS:

  Step 1: Push σ_(credits > 3) down to course (it involves only course attributes):

              π_(name)
                 │
                ⋈
               / \
              ⋈   σ_(credits > 3)(course)
             / \
       student  enrollment

  Step 2: Reorder joins — join enrollment with the filtered course first
  (smaller intermediate result):

              π_(name)
                 │
                ⋈ (on roll_no)
               / \
         student   ⋈ (on course_id)
                  / \
          enrollment  σ_(credits > 3)(course)

  Step 3: Push projections down:

              π_(name)
                 │
                ⋈ (on roll_no)
               / \
   π_(roll_no, name)  π_(roll_no)
          │                │
      student         ⋈ (on course_id)
                     / \
         π_(roll_no, course_id)  π_(course_id)
                │                     │
           enrollment          σ_(credits > 3)
                                      │
                                   course

This final tree is the OPTIMIZED QUERY TREE. Each optimization step reduces the
size of intermediate results.


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Steps in query processing: parsing, optimization, evaluation.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

1. PARSING (Syntax Analysis + Semantic Analysis):

The parser receives the SQL query as a string of characters and performs:

  a. LEXICAL ANALYSIS: Breaks the query into tokens (keywords, identifiers,
     operators, constants).
     "SELECT name FROM student WHERE age > 20"
     → [SELECT] [name] [FROM] [student] [WHERE] [age] [>] [20]

  b. SYNTAX ANALYSIS: Checks that the token sequence conforms to the SQL grammar.
     Uses a parse tree (or abstract syntax tree) to represent the structure.
     Detects errors like: "SELCT name FRM student" → syntax error.

  c. SEMANTIC ANALYSIS: Checks that the query makes logical sense:
     • Do the referenced tables exist in the schema?
     • Do the referenced columns exist in those tables?
     • Are data types compatible (e.g., comparing INT with INT, not INT with
       VARCHAR)?
     • Does the user have authorization to access those tables?
     • Are aggregate functions used correctly with GROUP BY?

  If any check fails, an error message is returned to the user.

  Output: An internal representation (usually a relational algebra expression
  or annotated parse tree).

2. OPTIMIZATION:

  The optimizer takes the relational algebra expression and finds the best
  execution plan.

  a. LOGICAL OPTIMIZATION (heuristic):
     • Push selections and projections down.
     • Eliminate redundant operations.
     • Convert Cartesian products with selections into joins.

  b. PHYSICAL OPTIMIZATION (cost-based):
     • Choose algorithms for each operation (hash join vs merge join, etc.).
     • Choose access methods (sequential scan vs index scan).
     • Evaluate join orders using dynamic programming.
     • Estimate costs using catalog statistics.

  Output: An optimized execution plan with specific algorithms.

3. EVALUATION (Execution):

  The query execution engine executes the plan:
  • Opens scans on the required tables/indices.
  • Executes operators in the order specified by the plan.
  • Uses pipelining where possible to avoid materializing intermediates.
  • Returns the result set to the client.

  The execution engine interacts with the STORAGE MANAGER to read/write disk
  blocks and with the BUFFER MANAGER to cache frequently accessed blocks.


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Heuristic rules for query optimization — at least four rules.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Heuristic transformation rules are algebraic rules that, when applied to a query
tree, generally produce a more efficient execution plan. They work by reducing
the size of intermediate results and avoiding unnecessary computation.

RULE 1: PERFORM SELECTION AS EARLY AS POSSIBLE (Push Selection Down)

  This is the MOST IMPORTANT heuristic. Moving σ operators down the tree reduces
  the number of tuples that subsequent operators must process.

  Before: π_(name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student ⋈ enrollment))
  After:  π_(name)(σ_(dept='CS')(student) ⋈ enrollment)

  If student has 10,000 rows and only 500 are in CS, the join processes 500
  rows instead of 10,000. The cost reduction can be dramatic.

RULE 2: PERFORM PROJECTION AS EARLY AS POSSIBLE (Push Projection Down)

  Eliminating unnecessary columns early reduces the width of tuples, which means
  fewer bytes per block, more tuples per block, and therefore fewer disk I/O
  operations.

  Before: student(roll_no, name, dept, address, phone, email, dob, photo)
          ⋈ enrollment(roll_no, course_id, grade)
  After:  π_(roll_no, name)(student) ⋈ π_(roll_no)(enrollment)

  The projected student relation may fit in many fewer blocks.

RULE 3: REPLACE CARTESIAN PRODUCT FOLLOWED BY SELECTION WITH JOIN

  σ_(θ)(R × S) ≡ R ⋈_(θ) S

  A Cartesian product produces |R| × |S| tuples, which can be enormous. If a
  selection on a join condition follows, it is far more efficient to combine them
  into a theta join, which only produces matching tuples.

  Before: σ_(student.roll_no = enrollment.roll_no)(student × enrollment)
  After:  student ⋈_(roll_no) enrollment

RULE 4: COMBINE CASCADED SELECTIONS INTO A SINGLE SELECTION

  σ_(θ₁)(σ_(θ₂)(R)) ≡ σ_(θ₁ ∧ θ₂)(R)

  Applying a single pass with a combined predicate is more efficient than two
  separate passes.

RULE 5: REORDER JOINS TO REDUCE INTERMEDIATE SIZE

  Join the tables that produce the SMALLEST intermediate result first. If
  σ_(dept='CS')(student) has 500 rows and course has 50 rows, join these first
  rather than joining student (10,000 rows) with enrollment (50,000 rows).

RULE 6: DECOMPOSE CONJUNCTIVE SELECTIONS FOR DISTRIBUTION

  σ_(θ₁ ∧ θ₂)(R ⋈ S) ≡ σ_(θ₁)(R) ⋈ σ_(θ₂)(S)
  (When θ₁ involves only R's attributes and θ₂ involves only S's attributes.)

  This allows each selection to be pushed to its respective relation.


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 5 (b) Optimize the given RA expression with query trees.
         π_(artist_name)(σ_(genre='Rock' ∧ play_count > 1000000)(
             artists ⋈ albums ⋈ songs))
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

Given expression:
  π_(artist_name)(σ_(genre='Rock' ∧ play_count > 1000000)(artists ⋈ albums ⋈ songs))

INITIAL (UNOPTIMIZED) QUERY TREE:

                  π_(artist_name)
                       │
             σ_(genre='Rock' ∧ play_count > 1000000)
                       │
                      ⋈ (albums.album_id = songs.album_id)
                     / \
                   ⋈     songs
                  / \    (song_id, song_name, album_id, duration, play_count)
           artists  albums
    (artist_id,    (album_id, album_name,
     artist_name,   artist_id, release_year)
     genre, country)

  Problem: The bottom join produces ALL artist-album combinations. Then the
  second join adds ALL songs. The selection filters only at the very end.

STEP 1: Break conjunctive selection (Rule: Selection Cascade):

  σ_(genre='Rock' ∧ play_count > 1000000) ≡ σ_(genre='Rock')(σ_(play_count > 1000000)(...))

STEP 2: Push selections down (Rule: Push Selection):

  • genre is an attribute of artists → push σ_(genre='Rock') to artists.
  • play_count is an attribute of songs → push σ_(play_count > 1000000) to songs.

STEP 3: Push projection down (Rule: Push Projection):

  • We need only artist_name from artists (plus artist_id for join).
  • We need only album_id and artist_id from albums (for joins).
  • We need only album_id from songs (for join, after selection).

OPTIMIZED QUERY TREE:

                  π_(artist_name)
                       │
                      ⋈ (artist_id)
                     / \
   π_(artist_id,        π_(artist_id)
    artist_name)              │
        │                    ⋈ (album_id)
  σ_(genre='Rock')          / \
        │       π_(artist_id,   π_(album_id)
     artists     album_id)          │
                    │        σ_(play_count > 1000000)
                  albums            │
                                 songs

  Benefits:
  1. σ_(genre='Rock') on artists first — if 5 genres, reduces to ~20% of rows.
  2. σ_(play_count > 1000000) on songs first — likely very selective.
  3. Projections strip unnecessary columns (country, album_name, release_year,
     song_name, duration, play_count are dropped early).
  4. The joins operate on much smaller intermediate results.

  If artists has 10,000 rows, albums has 50,000, and songs has 500,000:
  • Unoptimized: joins produce 10,000 × 50,000 × 500,000 combinations before
    filtering (impractical).
  • Optimized: ~2,000 Rock artists join with ~50,000 albums → ~10,000 rows,
    then join with ~5,000 popular songs → ~500 rows. Dramatic improvement.



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 6 (a) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) File organization: heap and sequential.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

FILE ORGANIZATION refers to how records are physically arranged and stored in
secondary storage (disk). The choice of file organization affects the speed of
different operations: insertion, deletion, searching, and sequential access.

1. HEAP FILE ORGANIZATION (Unordered File):

Records are placed in the file in the order they are inserted — there is NO
particular ordering. New records are always added at the END of the file (or in
the first available space after a deletion).

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Block 1: [Rec5] [Rec1] [Rec8]             │
  │ Block 2: [Rec3] [Rec7] [Rec2]             │
  │ Block 3: [Rec9] [Rec4] [---]  ← free space│
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Advantages:
  • INSERTION is very fast: O(1) — just append to the end.
  • No reorganization needed after insertion.
  • Works well for bulk loading data.

Disadvantages:
  • SEARCH requires linear scan: O(bᵣ) — must check every block.
  • DELETION creates wasted space (holes); periodic compaction needed.
  • Range queries are very expensive.

When to use: When the table is small, when most queries are full table scans,
or when insertion speed is critical and searches are rare.

2. SEQUENTIAL FILE ORGANIZATION (Ordered File):

Records are stored in sorted order based on a SEARCH KEY (one or more attributes).
This ordering makes range queries and sorted retrieval efficient.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Block 1: [Rec1] [Rec2] [Rec3]             │
  │ Block 2: [Rec4] [Rec5] [Rec6]             │
  │ Block 3: [Rec7] [Rec8] [Rec9]             │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  (Records sorted by search key)

Advantages:
  • BINARY SEARCH possible: O(⌈log₂(bᵣ)⌉) for equality queries on search key.
  • Range queries are efficient: find the start point, then read sequentially.
  • Ordered output without sorting.

Disadvantages:
  • INSERTION is expensive: inserting a record in sorted order may require
    shifting all subsequent records. Typically handled with an OVERFLOW BLOCK
    (chain of unsorted overflow records that is periodically merged).
  • DELETION requires reorganization or marking records as deleted.
  • Searches on non-search-key attributes still require linear scan.
  • Periodic reorganization (merge overflow) needed.

  ┌───────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect            │ Heap File            │ Sequential File      │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Order             │ None (insertion order)│ Sorted by search key │
  │ Insertion         │ O(1) — very fast     │ O(bᵣ) — may shift    │
  │ Search (key)      │ O(bᵣ) — linear scan │ O(log₂ bᵣ) — binary │
  │ Range query       │ O(bᵣ) — full scan   │ Efficient            │
  │ Deletion          │ Mark + compact later │ Mark + reorganize    │
  │ Maintenance       │ Low                  │ High (reorganization)│
  └───────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) B⁺ tree of order 4. Insert: 5, 8, 12, 15, 20, 25, 30, 35, 40.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A B⁺ tree of order 4 (also called order n=4) means:
  • Each internal node has at most 4 pointers and 3 keys.
  • Each internal node (except root) has at least ⌈4/2⌉ = 2 pointers.
  • Each leaf node can hold at most 3 key-pointer pairs (n-1 = 3).
  • Each leaf (except root) has at least ⌈(4-1)/2⌉ = 2 key-pointer pairs.
  • Leaf nodes are linked for sequential access.

Step-by-step insertions:

Insert 5:  [5]  (single leaf, also root)

Insert 8:  [5, 8]

Insert 12: [5, 8, 12]  (leaf full with 3 keys)

Insert 15: Leaf [5, 8, 12] is full. Split:
  Left leaf: [5, 8]    Right leaf: [12, 15]
  Middle key (12) goes up to new root.

              [12]
             /    \
        [5, 8] → [12, 15]

Insert 20: Goes to right leaf → [12, 15, 20] (fits)

              [12]
             /    \
        [5, 8] → [12, 15, 20]

Insert 25: Right leaf [12, 15, 20] full. Split:
  Left: [12, 15]   Right: [20, 25]
  Middle key (20) goes up to root.

              [12, 20]
             /    |    \
      [5, 8] → [12, 15] → [20, 25]

Insert 30: Goes to rightmost leaf → [20, 25, 30] (fits)

              [12, 20]
             /    |    \
      [5, 8] → [12, 15] → [20, 25, 30]

Insert 35: Rightmost leaf [20, 25, 30] full. Split:
  Left: [20, 25]   Right: [30, 35]
  Middle key (30) goes up to root.

              [12, 20, 30]
             /    |    |    \
      [5, 8] → [12, 15] → [20, 25] → [30, 35]

Insert 40: Goes to rightmost leaf → [30, 35, 40] (fits)

FINAL B⁺ TREE:

              [12, 20, 30]
             /    |    |    \
      [5, 8] → [12, 15] → [20, 25] → [30, 35, 40]

  Properties of the final tree:
  • Root has 3 keys and 4 pointers ✓
  • All leaves are at the same level (level 1) ✓
  • Leaf nodes are linked (→) for sequential access ✓
  • All search key values appear in leaves ✓
  • Height = 1 (root is at level 0, leaves at level 1)


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) Hash indices. How hashing works. Advantages and disadvantages.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

HASHING is a file organization technique where a HASH FUNCTION h(K) is applied
to the search key value K of a record to compute the address of the BUCKET
(disk block or group of blocks) where the record should be stored.

How it works:
  1. Choose a hash function h that maps search key values to bucket numbers
     (0, 1, 2, …, B-1 where B is the number of buckets).
  2. To INSERT a record with key K: compute h(K) → bucket number b. Store the
     record in bucket b.
  3. To SEARCH for key K: compute h(K) → bucket b. Search only bucket b.
  4. To DELETE key K: compute h(K) → bucket b. Find and remove from bucket b.

Example:
  Hash function: h(K) = K mod 5 (5 buckets: 0, 1, 2, 3, 4)

  Insert keys: 12, 17, 5, 23, 8, 35, 10

  h(12) = 12 mod 5 = 2  → bucket 2
  h(17) = 17 mod 5 = 2  → bucket 2 (same as 12 — collision!)
  h(5)  =  5 mod 5 = 0  → bucket 0
  h(23) = 23 mod 5 = 3  → bucket 3
  h(8)  =  8 mod 5 = 3  → bucket 3 (collision with 23)
  h(35) = 35 mod 5 = 0  → bucket 0 (collision with 5)
  h(10) = 10 mod 5 = 0  → bucket 0

  Bucket 0: [5, 35, 10]
  Bucket 1: [ ]
  Bucket 2: [12, 17]
  Bucket 3: [23, 8]
  Bucket 4: [ ]

If a bucket overflows, an OVERFLOW BUCKET is chained to it (overflow chaining).

ADVANTAGES:
  • Equality searches are very fast: O(1) average case — just compute hash and
    access one bucket.
  • Insertion and deletion are also O(1) average.
  • Simple to implement.

DISADVANTAGES:
  • RANGE QUERIES are not supported efficiently. h(K) does not preserve order,
    so finding all records where K > 20 requires scanning all buckets.
  • HASH COLLISIONS: Multiple keys may map to the same bucket, causing overflow
    chains that degrade performance.
  • BUCKET OVERFLOW: If the data distribution is skewed, some buckets become
    much fuller than others.
  • Fixed number of buckets: If the database grows significantly, the hash
    function may need to be changed and ALL records REHASHED (expensive).
  • Wasted space if data shrinks.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) Indexed sequential file organization (ISAM).
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

INDEXED SEQUENTIAL ACCESS METHOD (ISAM) combines the benefits of both sequential
file organization and indexing. Records are stored in sorted order on disk (like
sequential files), and an INDEX is maintained on top to speed up lookups.

Structure:

The index is typically organized in multiple levels:

  LEVEL 3: Master Index       (very small — fits in memory)
    ↓
  LEVEL 2: Cylinder Index     (one entry per cylinder)
    ↓
  LEVEL 1: Track Index        (one entry per track within a cylinder)
    ↓
  DATA BLOCKS: Sorted records  (sequential order)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        MASTER INDEX                                │
  │  [ 100 | 200 | 300 | 400 ]                                       │
  │    ↓      ↓      ↓      ↓                                        │
  │ CYLINDER/TRACK INDEX                                               │
  │  [10|20|30|40|50] [110|120|130|140] [210|220|...] [310|320|...]    │
  │    ↓  ↓  ↓  ↓  ↓                                                 │
  │ DATA BLOCKS (sorted records)                                       │
  │  [1,2,3..10] [11,12..20] [21,22..30] ...                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

How access works:
  1. To find record with key 125:
     - Check master index: 125 > 100, 125 < 200 → go to cylinder for 100-200.
     - Check cylinder/track index: 125 > 120, 125 < 130 → go to track for 120.
     - Sequential scan within that track to find 125.

  2. Range query (keys 50–80):
     - Use index to find key 50.
     - Read sequentially from that point until key > 80.

Advantages:
  • Fast equality lookups: O(log levels + 1 block read).
  • Efficient range queries (sequential data + index).
  • Both sequential and random access supported.

Disadvantages:
  • Overflow blocks for insertions degrade performance over time.
  • Periodic reorganization required (merge overflow blocks back into main file).
  • Index must be rebuilt during reorganization.
  • Deletion leaves gaps; compaction needed.

Comparison:
  ISAM → static index (does not change shape with insertions)
  B⁺ tree → dynamic index (automatically rebalances)

Modern systems prefer B⁺ trees over ISAM because B⁺ trees handle insertions
and deletions dynamically without needing periodic reorganization.


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) Fixed-length vs variable-length records.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

FIXED-LENGTH RECORDS:

Every record in the file occupies exactly the same number of bytes, regardless
of the actual data stored. The size is determined by the schema.

Example: student(roll_no INT(4), name CHAR(20), dept CHAR(10), age INT(4))
  Record size = 4 + 20 + 10 + 4 = 38 bytes (always)

  ┌──────┬────────────────────┬──────────┬────┐
  │ 4B   │ 20B                │ 10B      │ 4B │  = 38 bytes per record
  │roll_no│ name              │ dept     │ age│
  └──────┴────────────────────┴──────────┴────┘

Storage: Records are stored sequentially in blocks. Finding the i-th record
is simple: offset = (i-1) × record_size.

Deletion: Two approaches:
  a. Shift subsequent records to fill the gap (expensive).
  b. Use a FREE LIST: mark deleted record and add to a linked list of free
     slots. Next insertion reuses the slot.

  Block with free list:
  ┌─────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐
  │Hdr→3│ │ Rec1 │ │ Rec2 │ │ FREE→5 │ │ Rec4 │ │ FREE→∅│
  └─────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘

VARIABLE-LENGTH RECORDS:

Records can have different sizes. This occurs when:
  • A relation has VARCHAR or TEXT fields.
  • A relation stores multivalued attributes.
  • A file stores records of multiple relation types.

Example: student(roll_no INT, name VARCHAR(50), bio TEXT)
  Record sizes: "Aarav" with short bio = 40 bytes
                "Bipana" with long bio = 250 bytes

Handling variable-length records:

Method 1: SLOTTED PAGE STRUCTURE (most common)

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ BLOCK HEADER                                          │
  │ [#entries] [end_of_free_space] [slot1: offset,len]    │
  │ [slot2: offset,len] [slot3: offset,len]               │
  │ ─── free space ───────────────────────────────────     │
  │                                  [Record 3] [Rec 2]   │
  │                                              [Rec 1]  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ← header grows right          records grow left →

  • The header contains a SLOT ARRAY with one entry per record (offset + length).
  • Records are stored from the END of the block, growing inward.
  • Free space is between the slot array and the records.
  • To find a record: look up its slot → get offset and length → read.
  • Deletion: remove slot entry, compact records if needed.

Method 2: Separator characters (e.g., ⊥ delimiter between fields).

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Fixed-Length             │ Variable-Length       │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Record size      │ Same for all records    │ Varies per record     │
  │ Storage          │ Simpler, direct offset  │ Complex (slotted page)│
  │ Space efficiency │ May waste (padding)     │ Better (no padding)   │
  │ Access speed     │ Faster (direct calc)    │ Slower (indirection)  │
  │ Implementation   │ Simple                  │ Complex               │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) B⁺ tree index file. Node structure. Advantages over other methods.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A B⁺ TREE is a balanced, multi-level index structure that maintains data in
sorted order and allows efficient search, insertion, deletion, and range queries.
It is the MOST WIDELY USED index structure in modern RDBMS.

NODE STRUCTURE:

1. INTERNAL (NON-LEAF) NODE:
  ┌────┬────┬────┬────┬────┬────┬────┐
  │ P₁ │ K₁ │ P₂ │ K₂ │ P₃ │ K₃ │ P₄ │    (order n=4: 3 keys, 4 pointers)
  └────┴────┴────┴────┴────┴────┴────┘
  • Pᵢ are pointers to child nodes.
  • Kᵢ are search key values (in sorted order: K₁ < K₂ < K₃).
  • For a search value V:
    - If V < K₁: follow P₁
    - If K₁ ≤ V < K₂: follow P₂
    - If K₂ ≤ V < K₃: follow P₃
    - If V ≥ K₃: follow P₄

2. LEAF NODE:
  ┌────┬────┬────┬────┬────┬────┬────┐
  │ K₁ │ Ptr│ K₂ │ Ptr│ K₃ │ Ptr│ Pₙ │    Pₙ → next leaf node
  └────┴────┴────┴────┴────┴────┴────┘
  • Kᵢ are the actual search key values.
  • Each Ptr points to the ACTUAL DATA RECORD (or block) on disk.
  • Pₙ is a SIBLING POINTER that links to the next leaf node,
    forming a LINKED LIST of all leaves.
  • ALL search key values appear in leaf nodes (unlike B trees where some
    values appear only in internal nodes).

KEY PROPERTIES:
  • BALANCED: All leaf nodes are at the SAME depth. This guarantees worst-case
    O(log n) search.
  • Self-balancing: Insertions and deletions maintain balance through splits
    and merges.
  • Height h = ⌈log_(⌈n/2⌉)(K)⌉ where K is the number of search key values.
    For n=100 and K=1,000,000: h = ⌈log₅₀(1000000)⌉ = 4.

ADVANTAGES OVER OTHER INDEXING METHODS:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ vs Linear Index       │ B⁺ tree search: O(log n) vs O(n) linear   │
  │                       │ B⁺ tree handles dynamic data (inserts/     │
  │                       │ deletes) without full reorganization       │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vs Hash Index         │ B⁺ tree supports RANGE QUERIES efficiently │
  │                       │ (hash does not). B⁺ tree supports ORDER BY.│
  │                       │ Hash is slightly faster for point queries. │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vs Binary Search Tree │ B⁺ tree has much lower HEIGHT because each │
  │                       │ node holds many keys (high branching       │
  │                       │ factor). BST: height = O(log₂ n).         │
  │                       │ B⁺ tree: height = O(log_(n/2) N) — much   │
  │                       │ fewer disk accesses.                       │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vs B-tree (non-plus)  │ B⁺ tree stores all data in leaves →       │
  │                       │ simpler sequential scan. Leaf-level linked │
  │                       │ list enables efficient range queries.      │
  │                       │ B-tree stores some data in internal nodes  │
  │                       │ → variable traversal depth.                │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vs ISAM               │ B⁺ tree is DYNAMIC — handles inserts and  │
  │                       │ deletes gracefully. ISAM is static — needs │
  │                       │ overflow chains and periodic reorganization.│
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) Static hashing vs dynamic (extendable) hashing.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

STATIC HASHING:

In static hashing, the number of buckets B is FIXED when the hash file is created.
The hash function maps keys to one of these B buckets.

  h(K) = K mod B

  ┌──────────┐
  │ Bucket 0 │ → [records with h(K)=0]
  │ Bucket 1 │ → [records with h(K)=1]
  │ Bucket 2 │ → [records with h(K)=2] → [overflow bucket]
  │ Bucket 3 │ → [records with h(K)=3]
  └──────────┘

Problems with static hashing:
  1. If too few buckets: frequent overflows → long overflow chains → degraded
     search performance.
  2. If too many buckets: wasted space (empty or near-empty buckets).
  3. Cannot adapt to data growth without REHASHING (changing B and redistributing
     ALL records — very expensive).
  4. Skewed distribution: some buckets overflow while others are empty.

DYNAMIC (EXTENDABLE) HASHING:

Extendable hashing solves the problems of static hashing by allowing the number
of buckets to grow DYNAMICALLY as needed, without reorganizing the entire file.

Key concept: Uses the first i BITS of the hash value (called the GLOBAL DEPTH)
to select a bucket. A DIRECTORY of 2ⁱ entries maps hash prefixes to buckets.

Structure:
  Global depth: i = 2 (using first 2 bits)
  Directory (2² = 4 entries):

  ┌────────┬─────────────┐
  │ Hash   │ Bucket Ptr   │
  │ Prefix │              │
  ├────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 00     │ → Bucket A  │     Bucket A (local depth 2): [5, 12]
  │ 01     │ → Bucket B  │     Bucket B (local depth 2): [9, 17]
  │ 10     │ → Bucket C  │     Bucket C (local depth 1): [6, 14, 22]
  │ 11     │ → Bucket D  │     Bucket D (local depth 2): [7, 15]
  └────────┴─────────────┘

  LOCAL DEPTH: Each bucket tracks how many bits it actually uses. When a bucket
  overflows:
    - If local depth = global depth: DOUBLE the directory (increase global depth
      by 1), then split the overflowing bucket.
    - If local depth < global depth: JUST SPLIT the bucket (no directory change).

  This means only the overflowing bucket is reorganized — NOT the entire file.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Static Hashing           │ Extendable Hashing       │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Buckets          │ Fixed number             │ Grows dynamically        │
  │ Overflow         │ Overflow chains          │ Splits bucket + doubles  │
  │                  │                          │ directory if needed      │
  │ Reorganization   │ Full rehash if B changes │ Only the split bucket    │
  │ Space efficiency │ Poor for growing data    │ Better adaptation        │
  │ Directory        │ Not needed               │ Required (2ⁱ entries)    │
  │ Lookup cost      │ O(1) + overflow chain    │ O(1) — 2 disk accesses   │
  │                  │                          │ (directory + bucket)     │
  │ Complexity       │ Simple                   │ More complex             │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) Heap file organization — details, advantages, disadvantages, when.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

In a heap file, records are stored in NO PARTICULAR ORDER. As new records are
inserted, they are placed at the end of the file or in the first available free
space. There is no sorting, no indexing, and no hashing — just a simple
collection of records in disk blocks.

Physical organization:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Block 0: [Rec_A] [Rec_F] [Rec_C]                   │
  │ Block 1: [Rec_B] [Rec_G] [FREE]                    │
  │ Block 2: [Rec_D] [Rec_E] [Rec_H]                   │
  │ Block 3: [Rec_I] [FREE]  [FREE]   ← end of file    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Records are scattered — no logical order.

Operations:

  INSERT: Efficient — O(1).
    Find the last block (or first block with free space using a free-space map).
    Write the record there. No need to search for correct position.

  SEARCH (for a specific key value): Inefficient — O(bᵣ) worst case.
    Must perform a LINEAR SCAN, reading every block until the record is found
    or the entire file is scanned. On average, bᵣ/2 blocks are read.

  DELETE: Find the record (linear scan), then either:
    a. Shift subsequent records (expensive).
    b. Mark the slot as free (fast, but creates fragmentation).

  RANGE QUERY: Inefficient — O(bᵣ) — full scan required since data is unsorted.

Advantages:
  • Simplest file organization to implement.
  • Fastest insertions — no ordering to maintain.
  • Works well for bulk data loading.
  • Good for small tables where full scan is acceptable.
  • No overhead for maintaining order or indices.

Disadvantages:
  • Very slow searches (linear scan).
  • Fragmentation after many deletions.
  • Range queries and sorted access are expensive.
  • Not suitable for large tables with frequent lookups.

When heap file is appropriate:
  • Table is very small (fits in a few blocks).
  • Application needs to INSERT many records quickly (logging, audit trails).
  • Most queries are full table scans (batch processing, analytics on entire
    dataset).
  • The table is rarely searched by specific key values.
  • Temporary tables used during query processing.


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) B⁺ tree of order 3. Insert: 10, 20, 5, 15, 25, 30, 35, 40, 3, 8.
         Show tree after last three insertions (3, 8, 40).
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

B⁺ tree of order 3 means:
  • Internal nodes: at most 3 pointers, 2 keys. At least ⌈3/2⌉ = 2 pointers.
  • Leaf nodes: at most 2 key-pointer pairs. At least ⌈(3-1)/2⌉ = 1 key.

Build tree up to insertion of 35 and 40 (showing key moments):

Insert 10: [10]
Insert 20: [10, 20]  (leaf full)
Insert 5:  [5, 10, 20] — overflow! Split:
           Left: [5]   Right: [10, 20]   Push 10 up.
              [10]
             /    \
           [5]  → [10, 20]

Insert 15: → right leaf becomes [10, 15, 20] — overflow! Split:
           [10, 15] and [20]. Push 15 up.
              [10, 15]
             /    |    \
           [5] → [10] → [15, 20]

Wait — let me recalculate with order 3 (max 2 keys per leaf):

Insert 10: [10]
Insert 20: [10, 20] (fits — max 2 keys per leaf)
Insert 5: [5, 10, 20] — overflow (3 keys, max is 2). Split:
  Left: [5]  Right: [10, 20]  Push up 10.
            [10]
           /    \
         [5] → [10, 20]

Insert 15: Goes to right leaf [10, 20] → [10, 15, 20] — overflow. Split:
  Left: [10]  Right: [15, 20]  Push up 15.
            [10, 15]
           /    |    \
         [5] → [10] → [15, 20]

Insert 25: Goes to rightmost leaf → [15, 20, 25] — overflow. Split:
  Left: [15]  Right: [20, 25]  Push up 20.
  Root [10, 15] already has 2 keys → add 20 → [10, 15, 20] — root overflow!
  Split root: Left internal: [10], Right internal: [20]. Push 15 up as new root.

                  [15]
                 /    \
              [10]    [20]
             /   \    /   \
           [5]→[10]→[15]→[20, 25]

Insert 30: → rightmost leaf [20, 25] → [20, 25, 30] — overflow. Split:
  Left: [20]  Right: [25, 30]  Push 25 up to parent [20].
  [20] gets 25 → [20, 25]. Fits.

                  [15]
                 /    \
              [10]    [20, 25]
             /   \    /  |   \
           [5]→[10]→[15]→[20]→[25, 30]

Insert 35: → [25, 30] → [25, 30, 35] — overflow. Split:
  Left: [25]  Right: [30, 35]  Push 30 up.
  Parent [20, 25] + 30 → [20, 25, 30] — overflow! Split:
  Left internal: [20], Right internal: [30]. Push 25 up.
  Root [15] + 25 → [15, 25]. Fits.

                    [15, 25]
                   /    |    \
                [10]  [20]   [30]
               /  \    |     /  \
            [5]→[10]→[15]→[20]→[25]→[30, 35]

TREE BEFORE LAST 3 INSERTIONS (after 35):

                    [15, 25]
                   /    |    \
                [10]  [20]   [30]
               /  \    |     /  \
            [5]→[10]→[15]→[20]→[25]→[30, 35]

AFTER INSERT 40:
  → goes to [30, 35] → [30, 35, 40] — overflow. Split:
  Left: [30]  Right: [35, 40]. Push 35 up.
  Parent [30] + 35 → [30, 35]. Fits.

                    [15, 25]
                   /    |    \
                [10]  [20]   [30, 35]
               /  \    |     /  |   \
            [5]→[10]→[15]→[20]→[25]→[30]→[35, 40]

AFTER INSERT 3:
  → goes to [5] (leftmost leaf). [3, 5] — fits!

                    [15, 25]
                   /    |    \
                [10]  [20]   [30, 35]
               /  \    |     /  |   \
         [3,5]→[10]→[15]→[20]→[25]→[30]→[35, 40]

AFTER INSERT 8:
  → h(8) → goes to leftmost leaf [3, 5]. Wait — 8 > 5 and 8 < 10.
  → goes to [3, 5] (since 8 < 10, follow P₁ of [10]). [3, 5, 8] — overflow!
  Split: Left: [3]  Right: [5, 8]. Push 5 up.
  Parent [10] + 5 → [5, 10]. Fits.

FINAL TREE (after all insertions):

                    [15, 25]
                   /    |    \
              [5, 10]  [20]   [30, 35]
             /  |   \   |     /  |   \
           [3]→[5,8]→[10]→[15]→[20]→[25]→[30]→[35, 40]


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (a) B⁺ tree indexing vs hash indexing — comparison.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect               │ B⁺ Tree Index            │ Hash Index               │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Data structure       │ Balanced tree with linked │ Hash table with buckets  │
  │                      │ leaf nodes                │                          │
  │ Equality search      │ O(log n) — good          │ O(1) — excellent         │
  │ Range query          │ Excellent (leaf linked    │ NOT supported (must scan │
  │                      │ list traversal)           │ all buckets)             │
  │ ORDER BY             │ Supported (sorted leaves)│ NOT supported            │
  │ Insertion            │ O(log n) — may split     │ O(1) average — may need  │
  │                      │ nodes                     │ overflow/rehash          │
  │ Deletion             │ O(log n) — may merge     │ O(1) average             │
  │ Space utilization    │ ≥ 50% full (guaranteed)  │ Varies (may waste space) │
  │ Dynamic growth       │ Handles well (splits/    │ Static: poor. Extendable:│
  │                      │ merges automatically)     │ handled via directory    │
  │ Worst case search    │ O(log n) — guaranteed    │ O(n) — if all hash to    │
  │                      │                          │ same bucket              │
  │ Skewed data          │ Handles well (balanced)  │ Poor (uneven buckets)    │
  │ Index maintenance    │ Self-balancing            │ May need rehashing       │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

When B⁺ TREE is preferred:
  • Queries involve range conditions: WHERE price BETWEEN 100 AND 500
  • Queries need sorted output: ORDER BY name
  • Queries use comparisons: WHERE age > 25
  • The workload is mixed (equality + range + sorting)
  • General-purpose indexing (DEFAULT in most RDBMS)

  Example: "Find all students with GPA between 3.0 and 4.0"
  B⁺ tree: find leaf for 3.0, then follow linked list to 4.0 → efficient.
  Hash: must scan ALL buckets checking each record → O(n) → terrible.

When HASH INDEX is preferred:
  • ONLY equality queries: WHERE emp_id = 12345
  • High-volume point lookups with no range queries
  • In-memory hash tables for join operations (hash join)
  • When O(1) lookup is critical (e.g., caching, session stores)

  Example: "Find the employee with emp_id = 12345"
  Hash: compute h(12345) → access bucket → O(1). Fastest possible.
  B⁺ tree: traverse 3-4 levels → O(log n). Slightly slower.

Most RDBMS default to B⁺ tree because it handles ALL query types well.
PostgreSQL supports both: CREATE INDEX ... USING btree (default) or USING hash.



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 6 (b) — ANSWERS
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Transaction states with state transition diagram.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A TRANSACTION is a logical unit of work that consists of one or more database
operations (reads and writes) that must be treated as an ATOMIC, indivisible
unit. Either ALL operations in a transaction are completed successfully (COMMIT),
or NONE of them take effect (ABORT/ROLLBACK).

Example: Bank transfer — debit account A by Rs. 1000 and credit account B by
Rs. 1000. Both operations must succeed or both must fail.

TRANSACTION STATES:

A transaction passes through the following states during its lifecycle:

  1. ACTIVE: The transaction is currently executing. This is the initial state.
     All read and write operations happen in this state.

  2. PARTIALLY COMMITTED: The transaction has executed its final statement
     (the last operation) but has NOT yet been committed. The changes exist
     in memory but may not have been written to disk.

  3. COMMITTED: The transaction has completed successfully. All changes have
     been permanently recorded in the database. Once committed, the transaction
     CANNOT be undone.

  4. FAILED: The transaction has encountered an error (hardware failure,
     constraint violation, deadlock) and can no longer proceed normally.

  5. ABORTED: The transaction has been rolled back — all changes made during
     execution have been UNDONE, and the database is restored to its state
     before the transaction began.

     After abort, the system can either:
     a. RESTART the transaction (if the failure was due to hardware/software
        error, not logic error).
     b. KILL the transaction (if it was due to internal logic error).

STATE TRANSITION DIAGRAM:

  ┌──────────┐                     ┌───────────────────┐
  │  ACTIVE  │ ─── last statement ──→ PARTIALLY COMMITTED │
  └────┬─────┘      executed       └──────────┬──────────┘
       │                                      │
       │ error/                               │ output written
       │ failure                              │ to disk
       │                                      │
       ▼                                      ▼
  ┌──────────┐                     ┌───────────────────┐
  │  FAILED  │                     │    COMMITTED      │
  └────┬─────┘                     └───────────────────┘
       │
       │ rollback
       │ complete
       │
       ▼
  ┌──────────┐
  │  ABORTED │ → (restart or kill)
  └──────────┘

  Also: PARTIALLY COMMITTED → FAILED (if output cannot be written to disk,
  e.g., disk failure during commit).


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) ACID properties.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

The ACID properties are four fundamental guarantees that every transaction
processing system must provide to ensure reliability and correctness.

1. ATOMICITY ("All or Nothing"):

  A transaction is an indivisible unit. Either ALL operations in the transaction
  are executed and their effects are reflected in the database, or NONE of them
  are. There is no partial execution.

  Why important: Consider a bank transfer: debit A, credit B. If the system
  crashes after debiting A but before crediting B, Rs. 1000 disappears. Atomicity
  ensures that if the credit fails, the debit is also rolled back.

  Ensured by: Transaction management component (UNDO log / rollback mechanism).

2. CONSISTENCY:

  A transaction takes the database from one CONSISTENT STATE to another consistent
  state. All integrity constraints (domain, entity, referential, semantic) must be
  satisfied both before and after the transaction.

  Why important: If the sum of all account balances must always be Rs. 1,000,000,
  a transfer transaction must not change this total. If it does, the database
  enters an inconsistent state.

  Ensured by: Integrity constraints, application logic, and the DBMS constraint
  checker.

3. ISOLATION:

  Even though multiple transactions may execute CONCURRENTLY, each transaction
  must behave as if it is the ONLY transaction running. The intermediate results
  of one transaction must not be visible to other concurrent transactions.

  Why important: If transaction T₁ is transferring money and T₂ reads the account
  balance between T₁'s debit and credit, T₂ sees an incorrect (inconsistent)
  balance. Isolation prevents this.

  Ensured by: Concurrency control mechanisms (locking, MVCC, timestamps).

4. DURABILITY:

  Once a transaction is COMMITTED, its changes are PERMANENT and survive any
  subsequent system failures (power outage, crash, disk failure). The committed
  data must be saved to non-volatile storage.

  Why important: If a customer transfers money and the system confirms the
  transaction, the transfer must survive even if the system crashes one second
  later.

  Ensured by: Write-ahead logging (WAL), recovery mechanisms, database backups.

  ┌───────────────┬────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │ Property      │ Guarantees                     │ Ensured by           │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Atomicity     │ All or nothing                 │ Undo/Redo logs       │
  │ Consistency   │ Valid state → Valid state       │ Constraints          │
  │ Isolation     │ No interference between txns   │ Concurrency control  │
  │ Durability    │ Committed = permanent          │ WAL, recovery        │
  └───────────────┴────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Concurrency control. Lock-based protocol. Shared vs exclusive lock.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

CONCURRENCY CONTROL is the mechanism by which the DBMS manages simultaneous
execution of multiple transactions while ensuring the ACID properties, particularly
ISOLATION and CONSISTENCY.

Why needed: If transactions execute without control, several problems arise:
  • LOST UPDATE: T₁ and T₂ both read and update the same item. One update
    overwrites the other.
  • DIRTY READ: T₁ reads data written by T₂ that has NOT yet committed.
    If T₂ aborts, T₁ has read invalid data.
  • UNREPEATABLE READ: T₁ reads an item, T₂ modifies it, T₁ reads it again
    and gets a different value.

LOCK-BASED PROTOCOL:

The most common concurrency control mechanism. Before a transaction can access a
data item, it must acquire a LOCK on that item. The lock prevents other
transactions from conflicting access.

Two types of locks:

1. SHARED LOCK (S-lock / Read Lock):
   • Acquired when a transaction wants to READ a data item.
   • Multiple transactions can hold S-locks on the same item simultaneously
     (because concurrent reads do not conflict).
   • Denoted: lock-S(Q) — transaction requests shared lock on item Q.

2. EXCLUSIVE LOCK (X-lock / Write Lock):
   • Acquired when a transaction wants to WRITE (modify) a data item.
   • Only ONE transaction can hold an X-lock on an item at a time.
   • No other transaction can hold ANY lock (S or X) on the item while an
     X-lock is held.
   • Denoted: lock-X(Q) — transaction requests exclusive lock on item Q.

LOCK COMPATIBILITY MATRIX:

  ┌───────────────────┬───────────┬───────────┐
  │ Request \ Held    │  S-lock   │  X-lock   │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┤
  │ S-lock (read)     │ GRANTED ✓ │ WAIT ✗    │
  │ X-lock (write)    │ WAIT ✗    │ WAIT ✗    │
  └───────────────────┴───────────┴───────────┘

  • S-S: Compatible (multiple readers allowed).
  • S-X: Incompatible (reader must wait for writer, or vice versa).
  • X-X: Incompatible (only one writer at a time).

Example:
  T₁: lock-S(A); read(A); unlock(A);
  T₂: lock-X(A); write(A); unlock(A);

  If T₁ holds S(A) and T₂ requests X(A), T₂ must WAIT until T₁ releases S(A).
  If T₂ holds X(A) and T₁ requests S(A), T₁ must WAIT until T₂ releases X(A).

Simple locking alone does NOT guarantee serializability — the TWO-PHASE LOCKING
protocol (2PL) is needed for that guarantee.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Serializability. Conflict vs view serializability. Precedence graph.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

SERIALIZABILITY is the correctness criterion for concurrent transaction execution.
A schedule (interleaved execution of multiple transactions) is SERIALIZABLE if its
effect is equivalent to SOME serial execution of the same transactions.

Serial schedule: Transactions execute one after another with NO interleaving.
Always correct but slow (no concurrency).

Non-serial schedule: Transactions are interleaved. May or may not be correct.
A non-serial schedule is correct if it is serializable.

CONFLICT SERIALIZABILITY:

Two operations CONFLICT if they:
  1. Belong to DIFFERENT transactions,
  2. Access the SAME data item, AND
  3. At least one is a WRITE operation.

Conflict types:
  • read-write conflict (WR or RW)
  • write-write conflict (WW)
  (read-read does NOT conflict)

A schedule is CONFLICT SERIALIZABLE if it can be transformed into a serial
schedule by swapping NON-CONFLICTING adjacent operations.

VIEW SERIALIZABILITY:

A schedule S is VIEW SERIALIZABLE if it is VIEW EQUIVALENT to some serial
schedule. View equivalence requires:
  1. For each data item Q, if Tᵢ reads the initial value of Q in S, then Tᵢ
     must also read the initial value of Q in the serial schedule.
  2. If Tᵢ reads a value of Q written by Tⱼ in S, then the same must hold in
     the serial schedule.
  3. For each data item Q, the transaction that performs the FINAL write of Q
     in S must also perform the final write in the serial schedule.

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect               │ Conflict Serializable    │ View Serializable        │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Based on             │ Conflicting operations   │ Read-from relationships  │
  │ Testing              │ Precedence graph (poly.) │ NP-complete              │
  │ Relationship         │ Subset of view serial.   │ Superset of conflict ser.│
  │ Every conflict-      │ —                        │ Also view-serializable   │
  │ serializable schedule│                          │                          │
  │ Practical use        │ Widely used              │ Rarely used (expensive)  │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

TESTING CONFLICT SERIALIZABILITY — PRECEDENCE GRAPH:

1. Create a node for each transaction.
2. Add a directed edge Tᵢ → Tⱼ if there is a conflicting pair of operations
   where Tᵢ's operation comes BEFORE Tⱼ's in the schedule.
3. If the graph has NO CYCLE → the schedule is CONFLICT SERIALIZABLE.
   If the graph HAS a CYCLE → the schedule is NOT conflict serializable.

Example:
  Schedule S: r₁(A); r₂(A); w₁(A); w₂(A); r₁(B); r₂(B); w₂(B); w₁(B);

  Conflicts:
  r₂(A) before w₁(A): T₂ → T₁ (RW conflict on A)
  w₁(A) before w₂(A): T₁ → T₂ (WW conflict on A)
  r₁(B) before w₂(B): T₁ → T₂ (RW conflict on B)
  w₂(B) before w₁(B): T₂ → T₁ (WW conflict on B)

  Precedence graph:
  T₁ → T₂ (from A: WW, and B: RW)
  T₂ → T₁ (from A: RW, and B: WW)

  CYCLE exists: T₁ → T₂ → T₁. NOT conflict serializable.


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Deadlocks. Prevention and detection. Example.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A DEADLOCK occurs when two or more transactions are waiting for each other to
release locks, creating a circular dependency where none can proceed.

Example:
  T₁: lock-X(A);           -- T₁ holds X-lock on A
  T₂:             lock-X(B);  -- T₂ holds X-lock on B
  T₁: lock-X(B);           -- T₁ WAITS for B (held by T₂)
  T₂:             lock-X(A);  -- T₂ WAITS for A (held by T₁)

  T₁ waits for T₂ (to release B). T₂ waits for T₁ (to release A).
  Neither can proceed → DEADLOCK.

  Wait-for graph:  T₁ → T₂ → T₁  (cycle = deadlock)

DEADLOCK PREVENTION:

Prevention ensures that deadlocks NEVER occur by imposing restrictions on how
transactions request locks.

1. LOCK ORDERING: All transactions must request locks in a predefined global
   order (e.g., alphabetical by data item name). If all transactions lock A
   before B, the circular wait cannot form.

2. WAIT-DIE SCHEME (timestamp-based):
   • If Tᵢ is OLDER than Tⱼ (lower timestamp) and Tᵢ requests a lock held by Tⱼ:
     Tᵢ WAITS (old waits for young).
   • If Tᵢ is YOUNGER and requests a lock held by older Tⱼ:
     Tᵢ DIES (aborts and restarts with same timestamp).
   • Non-preemptive: only the requesting transaction may abort.

3. WOUND-WAIT SCHEME (timestamp-based):
   • If Tᵢ is OLDER and requests a lock held by younger Tⱼ:
     Tᵢ WOUNDS Tⱼ (forces Tⱼ to abort — preemptive).
   • If Tᵢ is YOUNGER and requests a lock held by older Tⱼ:
     Tᵢ WAITS (young waits for old).
   • Preemptive: the lock holder may be forced to abort.

4. TIMEOUT: If a transaction waits for a lock longer than a threshold, it is
   assumed to be in a deadlock and is aborted.

DEADLOCK DETECTION:

The system allows deadlocks to occur but detects and resolves them.

1. WAIT-FOR GRAPH:
   • Nodes = transactions. Edge Tᵢ → Tⱼ means Tᵢ is waiting for Tⱼ.
   • Periodically check for CYCLES. A cycle = deadlock.

2. VICTIM SELECTION:
   When a deadlock is detected, one transaction (the "victim") must be ABORTED
   to break the cycle. The victim is chosen based on:
   • How much work it has done (prefer aborting the one with least work).
   • How many locks it holds.
   • How many other transactions would be affected.
   • Avoid STARVATION: don't always choose the same transaction as victim.

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect           │ Prevention               │ Detection                │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Approach         │ Prevent deadlock from     │ Allow deadlock, then     │
  │                  │ ever occurring            │ detect and resolve       │
  │ Mechanism        │ Ordering, timestamps      │ Wait-for graph           │
  │ Overhead         │ Lower (no detection cost) │ Graph maintenance +      │
  │                  │                          │ periodic cycle check     │
  │ Aborts           │ May abort unnecessarily   │ Only aborts actual       │
  │                  │ (conservative)            │ deadlocked transactions  │
  │ Better when      │ Deadlocks are frequent    │ Deadlocks are rare       │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Serial vs non-serial schedules. Testing serializability.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

A SCHEDULE is a chronological ordering of the operations (reads and writes) of
multiple transactions as they are executed by the DBMS.

SERIAL SCHEDULE:
  Transactions execute one at a time, with NO interleaving. Each transaction
  completes entirely before the next one begins.

  Example (T₁ then T₂):
  r₁(A); w₁(A); r₁(B); w₁(B); r₂(A); w₂(A); r₂(B); w₂(B);
  |←──────── T₁ ────────→|  |←──────── T₂ ────────→|

  Guaranteed correct (preserves consistency), but poor performance because
  there is NO concurrency — CPU and I/O cannot overlap.

  For N transactions, there are N! possible serial schedules, all correct.

NON-SERIAL SCHEDULE:
  Operations of multiple transactions are INTERLEAVED. Allows concurrency
  (better throughput) but may produce incorrect results if not properly
  controlled.

  Example:
  r₁(A); r₂(A); w₁(A); w₂(A); r₁(B); r₂(B); w₂(B); w₁(B);
  |← T₁ and T₂ interleaved →|

  This may or may not be equivalent to some serial schedule.

TESTING WHETHER A NON-SERIAL SCHEDULE IS SERIALIZABLE:

Method: PRECEDENCE GRAPH (for conflict serializability)

  1. Create a node for each transaction Tᵢ.
  2. For each pair of conflicting operations (same data item, at least one write,
     different transactions), add an edge from the transaction whose operation
     comes FIRST to the transaction whose operation comes SECOND.
  3. Check for cycles:
     • NO CYCLE → Conflict serializable ✓
     • CYCLE → NOT conflict serializable ✗

  If the graph is acyclic, a TOPOLOGICAL SORT of the graph gives the equivalent
  serial order.

Example:
  S: r₁(A); w₂(A); r₂(B); w₁(B);

  Conflicts:
  r₁(A) before w₂(A): T₁ → T₂  (RW on A)
  r₂(B) before w₁(B): T₂ → T₁  (RW on B)

  Graph: T₁ → T₂ → T₁ — CYCLE! NOT serializable.

Another example:
  S: r₁(A); w₁(A); r₂(A); w₂(A); r₁(B); w₁(B); r₂(B); w₂(B);

  Conflicts:
  w₁(A) before r₂(A): T₁ → T₂  (WR on A)
  w₁(A) before w₂(A): T₁ → T₂  (WW on A)
  w₁(B) before r₂(B): T₁ → T₂  (WR on B)
  w₁(B) before w₂(B): T₁ → T₂  (WW on B)

  Graph: T₁ → T₂ only. NO CYCLE ✓ Serializable. Equivalent to serial: T₁, T₂.


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) SQL standard isolation levels. Dirty/non-repeatable/phantom reads.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

SQL defines four ISOLATION LEVELS that control the degree of isolation between
concurrent transactions. Higher isolation provides more correctness but less
concurrency (lower throughput).

PROBLEMS (phenomena) that can occur without proper isolation:

1. DIRTY READ: Transaction T₁ reads data WRITTEN by T₂ that has NOT yet
   committed. If T₂ aborts, T₁ has read data that never existed.
   Example: T₂ updates salary to 50000 (not committed). T₁ reads 50000.
   T₂ aborts. T₁ used a "phantom" value.

2. NON-REPEATABLE READ (Fuzzy Read): T₁ reads a data item, T₂ modifies and
   commits it, T₁ reads it again and gets a DIFFERENT value.
   Example: T₁ reads balance = 1000. T₂ updates balance to 500 and commits.
   T₁ reads again → balance = 500. Two reads in same transaction, different results.

3. PHANTOM READ: T₁ reads a set of rows matching a condition. T₂ inserts a new
   row that matches the condition and commits. T₁ re-executes the same query
   and gets an ADDITIONAL row (a "phantom").
   Example: T₁: SELECT * FROM student WHERE dept = 'CS' → 50 students.
   T₂: INSERT INTO student VALUES (..., 'CS') and commits.
   T₁ re-queries → 51 students. The new student is a phantom.

ISOLATION LEVELS:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ Isolation Level    │ Dirty Read │ Non-Repeatable   │ Phantom Read │
  │                    │            │ Read             │              │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ READ UNCOMMITTED   │ Possible   │ Possible         │ Possible     │
  │ READ COMMITTED     │ Prevented  │ Possible         │ Possible     │
  │ REPEATABLE READ    │ Prevented  │ Prevented        │ Possible     │
  │ SERIALIZABLE       │ Prevented  │ Prevented        │ Prevented    │
  └────────────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────┘

  READ UNCOMMITTED (Lowest isolation):
    • No locks on reads. Can read uncommitted data from other transactions.
    • Use case: Approximate counts, reporting where accuracy is not critical.
    SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ UNCOMMITTED;

  READ COMMITTED (Default in PostgreSQL, Oracle):
    • Reads only COMMITTED data. Writes hold locks until commit.
    • But the same query may return different results if another transaction
      commits between the two reads (non-repeatable read).
    SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED;

  REPEATABLE READ (Default in MySQL InnoDB):
    • All data read by the transaction is locked until commit. No other
      transaction can modify the read data.
    • But new rows can be INSERTED by other transactions (phantom reads).
    SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;

  SERIALIZABLE (Highest isolation):
    • Full isolation — equivalent to serial execution.
    • Typically implemented with range locks or MVCC (multi-version concurrency
      control) to prevent phantoms.
    • Lowest concurrency, highest correctness.
    SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Two-Phase Locking (2PL): basic, strict, rigorous.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

The TWO-PHASE LOCKING (2PL) protocol is a concurrency control protocol that
guarantees CONFLICT SERIALIZABILITY. It divides each transaction's execution
into two phases regarding lock acquisition and release.

BASIC TWO-PHASE LOCKING:

Phase 1 — GROWING PHASE: The transaction may acquire locks but may NOT release
any lock.

Phase 2 — SHRINKING PHASE: The transaction may release locks but may NOT acquire
any new lock.

  The LOCK POINT is the moment when the transaction has acquired all its locks
  (the transition from growing to shrinking phase).

  Timeline:
  ────────────────────────────────────────────→ time
  |←── Growing Phase ──→|←── Shrinking Phase ──→|
  lock-S(A)  lock-X(B)  lock-S(C)  unlock(A)  unlock(B)  unlock(C)
                            ↑
                        Lock Point

  Guarantee: If all transactions follow 2PL, the resulting schedule is
  conflict serializable. The serial order is determined by the order of lock
  points.

  Problem: Basic 2PL does NOT prevent CASCADING ROLLBACK. If T₁ releases a lock
  (shrinking phase) and T₂ reads the value, then T₁ aborts, T₂ must also abort
  (and any transaction that read T₂'s data, etc.).

STRICT TWO-PHASE LOCKING (Strict 2PL):

  • All EXCLUSIVE (X) locks held by a transaction are released only AFTER the
    transaction COMMITS or ABORTS.
  • Shared locks may be released during the shrinking phase.

  Timeline:
  ──────────────────────────────────────────→ time
  |←── Growing Phase ──→|←── Shrinking ──→|
  lock-S(A) lock-X(B) lock-S(C) unlock(A) unlock(C)  COMMIT  unlock(B)
                                                        ↑
                                            X-locks released after COMMIT

  Advantage: Prevents cascading rollback because uncommitted writes are never
  visible to other transactions (X-lock is held until commit).

RIGOROUS TWO-PHASE LOCKING (Rigorous 2PL):

  • ALL locks (both S and X) are held until the transaction COMMITS or ABORTS.
  • No locks are released during execution — ALL released at once at the end.

  Timeline:
  ──────────────────────────────────────────→ time
  |←── Growing Phase (all of execution) ──→|
  lock-S(A) lock-X(B) lock-S(C)  ... operations ...  COMMIT
                                                       ↓
                                              unlock(A), unlock(B), unlock(C)

  Advantage: Simplest to implement. Guarantees serializability AND prevents
  cascading rollback AND ensures that the serial order matches the commit order.

  ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Protocol         │ Key difference                                       │
  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
