  │ Basic 2PL        │ Locks released in shrinking phase. Cascading abort    │
  │                  │ possible.                                            │
  │ Strict 2PL       │ X-locks held till commit. No cascading abort.        │
  │                  │ S-locks can be released earlier.                      │
  │ Rigorous 2PL     │ ALL locks held till commit. Simplest. Serial order   │
  │                  │ = commit order.                                       │
  └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  All three guarantee conflict serializability. Rigorous 2PL is used by most
  commercial RDBMS (PostgreSQL, MySQL InnoDB, SQL Server).


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Recovery and atomicity. Log-based recovery. Redo vs Undo.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

RECOVERY is the process of restoring the database to a correct, consistent state
after a failure. ATOMICITY requires that if a transaction is interrupted by a
failure, its partial effects must be UNDONE. If a committed transaction's effects
are lost due to a crash, they must be REDONE.

LOG-BASED RECOVERY:

The system maintains a WRITE-AHEAD LOG (WAL) — a sequential file that records
every modification made to the database BEFORE the modification is applied to
the database itself.

Log records contain:
  • <Tᵢ, start>: Transaction Tᵢ has started.
  • <Tᵢ, X, Vold, Vnew>: Transaction Tᵢ changed data item X from old value
    Vold to new value Vnew.
  • <Tᵢ, commit>: Transaction Tᵢ has committed.
  • <Tᵢ, abort>: Transaction Tᵢ has aborted.

WRITE-AHEAD LOGGING RULE (WAL Rule):
  The log record for a modification MUST be written to stable storage (disk)
  BEFORE the modified data is written to disk. This ensures that the log always
  has enough information to undo or redo any operation.

UNDO OPERATION:

  Used for transactions that were ACTIVE (not committed) at the time of crash.
  Since these transactions did not complete, their changes must be REVERSED.

  Process: Scan the log BACKWARDS. For each log record <Tᵢ, X, Vold, Vnew>
  where Tᵢ has no <Tᵢ, commit> record, restore X to Vold.

  Example:
  Log: <T₁, start> <T₁, A, 100, 200> <T₁, B, 50, 75>  [CRASH]
  T₁ did not commit → UNDO: restore A = 100, B = 50.

REDO OPERATION:

  Used for transactions that COMMITTED before the crash but whose changes may
  not have been written to the disk (they were in the buffer when the crash
  occurred).

  Process: Scan the log FORWARD. For each log record <Tᵢ, X, Vold, Vnew>
  where Tᵢ has a <Tᵢ, commit> record, set X to Vnew.

  Example:
  Log: <T₂, start> <T₂, C, 300, 400> <T₂, commit>  [CRASH]
  T₂ committed but C may not have been flushed to disk → REDO: set C = 400.

RECOVERY PROCESS AFTER CRASH:

  1. Scan log to identify:
     • REDO LIST: committed transactions (have <commit> in log).
     • UNDO LIST: active transactions (have <start> but no <commit>).
  2. REDO all committed transactions (forward scan).
  3. UNDO all active transactions (backward scan).


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Q. 6 (b) Crash recovery. Checkpoints. How they reduce recovery time.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

ANSWER:

CRASH RECOVERY is the process of restoring the database to a consistent state
after an unexpected failure (power outage, system crash, hardware failure).
Without recovery mechanisms, committed data could be lost and uncommitted data
could remain in the database.

The problem with simple log-based recovery is that the ENTIRE LOG must be scanned
from the beginning, which can be very expensive if the log is large (terabytes
in production systems).

CHECKPOINTS:

A CHECKPOINT is a recovery technique that periodically saves the database state
to reduce the amount of log that must be scanned during recovery.

Checkpoint procedure:
  1. Temporarily SUSPEND all transaction operations.
  2. FLUSH all modified buffers (dirty pages) from memory to disk.
  3. Write a <CHECKPOINT L> record to the log, where L is the list of all
     currently ACTIVE transactions.
  4. Flush the log to stable storage.
  5. RESUME normal transaction processing.

After a checkpoint, all committed transactions before the checkpoint are
GUARANTEED to have their changes on disk. During recovery, we only need to
examine the log from the LAST CHECKPOINT forward — NOT from the beginning.

TIMELINE DIAGRAM:

  Time ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────→

  T₁: |──────────────────────|commit|
  T₂: |───────────|commit|                                    ← committed before CP
  T₃:        |──────────────────────────────────|commit|
  T₄:                              |─────────────────────|    ← active at crash
  T₅:                                       |──|commit|

                    ↑                                    ↑
               CHECKPOINT                             CRASH

  At checkpoint: T₂ already committed, T₁ and T₃ are active.
  After checkpoint: T₃ commits, T₅ starts and commits, T₄ starts but never commits.

  RECOVERY ACTIONS:
  • T₂: Ignore (committed before checkpoint, changes already on disk).
  • T₁: REDO (committed after checkpoint — need to ensure changes are on disk).
  • T₃: REDO (started before checkpoint, committed after — need REDO).
  • T₅: REDO (started and committed after checkpoint).
  • T₄: UNDO (active at crash, never committed — roll back changes).

  Only the log from the checkpoint forward needs to be examined!

HOW CHECKPOINTS REDUCE RECOVERY TIME:

  Without checkpoints:
  • Must scan entire log from the beginning (could be millions of records).
  • Must identify ALL committed and active transactions since system start.
  • Recovery could take hours.

  With checkpoints:
  • Only scan log from last checkpoint (much shorter — minutes of log).
  • Only process transactions that were active at or after the checkpoint.
  • Recovery takes seconds to minutes instead of hours.

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Without Checkpoint: Scan entire log                          │
  │ [Start] ←─────────── ENTIRE LOG (terabytes) ──────────────→ [Crash] │
  │                                                                     │
  │         With Checkpoint: Scan only recent log                        │
  │ [Start] ... [CP] ←── only this portion ──→ [Crash]                  │
  │                     (much smaller)                                   │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘



════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                         QUESTION 7 — ANSWERS
                      (Short Notes — Any Two)
════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

NOTE: Each set provides THREE short note topics. In the exam, students choose
ANY TWO. All three are answered here for completeness. Each short note is
approximately 5-mark length (half of a full 10-mark answer).


SET 1 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. NoSQL Databases and Their Characteristics
b. Checkpoints in Crash Recovery
c. Conflict Serializability
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. NoSQL DATABASES AND THEIR CHARACTERISTICS:

NoSQL ("Not Only SQL") databases are non-relational database systems designed to
handle large volumes of unstructured, semi-structured, or rapidly changing data
that traditional RDBMS cannot efficiently manage. They emerged in the 2000s to
address the scalability and flexibility needs of web-scale applications like
social media, IoT, and real-time analytics.

Key Characteristics:
  1. SCHEMA-LESS: No fixed schema — fields can vary between records.
  2. HORIZONTAL SCALABILITY: Data is distributed across multiple servers
     (sharding). Adding more servers increases capacity.
  3. HIGH AVAILABILITY: Data is replicated across nodes. If one fails, others
     continue serving requests.
  4. EVENTUAL CONSISTENCY: Instead of strict ACID, many NoSQL systems use the
     BASE model (Basically Available, Soft state, Eventually consistent).
  5. FLEXIBLE DATA MODELS: Support various data models beyond tables.

Categories:
  • Document stores (MongoDB): Store data as JSON/BSON documents.
  • Key-Value stores (Redis, DynamoDB): Simple key→value pairs. Ultra-fast.
  • Column-family stores (Cassandra, HBase): Data stored in columns rather
    than rows. Optimized for queries on specific columns.
  • Graph databases (Neo4j): Nodes and edges represent entities and relationships.
    Ideal for social networks, recommendation engines.

When to use NoSQL: Big data, real-time analytics, content management, IoT data,
flexible schema requirements, horizontal scaling needs.

When NOT to use: Complex joins, ACID transactions required, highly structured
relational data, financial systems requiring strict consistency.


b. CHECKPOINTS IN CRASH RECOVERY:

A checkpoint is a mechanism that reduces the amount of work needed during database
recovery after a crash. Without checkpoints, recovery requires scanning the entire
transaction log from the beginning, which is impractical for long-running systems.

During a checkpoint:
  1. All modified (dirty) pages in the buffer are flushed to disk.
  2. A <CHECKPOINT L> record is written to the log (L = list of active
     transactions at checkpoint time).
  3. The log is flushed to stable storage.

After a crash, recovery starts from the LAST CHECKPOINT:
  • Transactions committed before the checkpoint: changes already on disk — skip.
  • Transactions committed after the checkpoint: REDO their operations.
  • Transactions active at crash (never committed): UNDO their operations.

This dramatically reduces recovery time from hours to seconds.

Fuzzy checkpoints allow transactions to continue during the checkpoint process,
avoiding the performance hit of stopping all activity.


c. CONFLICT SERIALIZABILITY:

A schedule is conflict serializable if it can be transformed into a serial
schedule by swapping adjacent non-conflicting operations. Two operations conflict
if they: (1) belong to different transactions, (2) access the same data item,
and (3) at least one is a write.

Testing: Build a PRECEDENCE GRAPH. Nodes = transactions. Edge Tᵢ → Tⱼ if Tᵢ has
a conflicting operation before Tⱼ's conflicting operation. If the graph is
ACYCLIC → conflict serializable. A topological sort gives the equivalent serial
order. If the graph has a CYCLE → NOT conflict serializable.

Every conflict-serializable schedule is also view-serializable, but the converse
is not necessarily true. Conflict serializability is the standard correctness
criterion used in practice because it can be efficiently tested (polynomial time).


SET 2 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Object Oriented Database and ORM
b. Denormalization for Performance
c. SQL Standard Isolation Levels
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. OBJECT ORIENTED DATABASE AND ORM:

An Object-Oriented Database (OODB) stores data as OBJECTS, similar to object-
oriented programming. Each object has attributes (data) and methods (behavior),
organized into classes with inheritance. OODBs directly store complex data types
(lists, sets, nested objects) without the impedance mismatch of mapping objects
to relational tables.

OODBs follow the ODMG (Object Data Management Group) standard. Examples include
db4o, ObjectDB, and Versant.

However, RDBMS remain dominant. The gap between object-oriented application code
and relational databases is bridged by ORM (Object-Relational Mapping).

ORM is a programming technique that maps database tables to programming language
classes. Each row becomes an object instance; each column becomes an attribute.
SQL queries are replaced by method calls.

Popular ORM frameworks:
  • Python: Django ORM, SQLAlchemy
  • Java: Hibernate, JPA
  • Node.js: Sequelize, Prisma
  • Ruby: ActiveRecord

Benefits of ORM: Reduces SQL coding, prevents SQL injection, provides database
portability, and lets developers work with familiar objects.

Drawbacks: Performance overhead, less control over generated SQL, complex queries
may be harder to express.


b. DENORMALIZATION FOR PERFORMANCE:

Denormalization is the deliberate introduction of redundancy into a normalized
database to improve READ performance. While normalization reduces redundancy and
prevents anomalies, it creates many tables that require expensive JOIN operations.

When to denormalize:
  • Read-heavy workloads (OLAP, reporting, dashboards).
  • Frequently executed queries involving many joins.
  • When query response time is critical.

Techniques:
  1. Adding derived/redundant columns (storing calculated values).
  2. Pre-joining tables (combining frequently joined tables).
  3. Adding summary tables (materialized aggregate data).

Trade-offs: Faster reads but slower writes (must update redundant data in
multiple places), increased storage, risk of data inconsistency if updates are
not properly managed. Best applied selectively, guided by query profiling.


c. SQL STANDARD ISOLATION LEVELS:

The SQL standard defines four isolation levels controlling concurrency:

  READ UNCOMMITTED: Lowest isolation. Allows dirty reads. Rarely used.
  READ COMMITTED: Default in PostgreSQL/Oracle. Prevents dirty reads but allows
    non-repeatable reads and phantoms.
  REPEATABLE READ: Default in MySQL. Prevents dirty and non-repeatable reads.
    Phantoms still possible.
  SERIALIZABLE: Highest isolation. Prevents all anomalies. Equivalent to serial
    execution. Lowest concurrency.

Each level prevents progressively more phenomena (dirty read → non-repeatable
read → phantom read). The DBA chooses the level based on the trade-off between
data correctness and system throughput.


SET 3 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Failure Classification in Crash Recovery
b. Distributed Ledger Technology and Blockchain
c. Features of Good Relational Designs
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. FAILURE CLASSIFICATION IN CRASH RECOVERY:

Database failures are classified into three categories based on severity:

1. TRANSACTION FAILURE:
   • Logical error: Transaction cannot complete due to internal conditions
     (division by zero, constraint violation, insufficient funds).
   • System error: DBMS terminates the transaction (deadlock victim).
   • Recovery: UNDO the transaction using log records. Relatively simple.

2. SYSTEM FAILURE (Soft Crash):
   • Hardware malfunction (power failure) or software bug (OS crash, DBMS crash).
   • Main memory (buffer) contents are LOST, but disk contents survive.
   • Recovery: Use the log to REDO committed transactions and UNDO active ones.
   • Checkpoints help limit the amount of log to process.

3. DISK FAILURE (Hard Crash):
   • Physical damage to disk — stored data is permanently lost.
   • Cannot be recovered from the log alone (the log may be on the same disk).
   • Recovery: Restore from BACKUP (periodically created full copy) and apply
     log records since the last backup to bring the database up to date.
   • Remote backup systems provide higher availability.


b. DISTRIBUTED LEDGER TECHNOLOGY AND BLOCKCHAIN:

A distributed ledger is a database that is shared, replicated, and synchronized
across multiple sites, countries, or institutions. Unlike centralized databases
with a single authority, distributed ledgers have NO central administrator.

Blockchain is the most well-known implementation of distributed ledger technology.
It organizes data into a chain of BLOCKS, where each block contains:
  • A set of transactions.
  • A cryptographic HASH of the previous block (creating the chain).
  • A timestamp and nonce.

Key properties:
  1. IMMUTABILITY: Once a block is added, it cannot be altered without changing
     all subsequent blocks (computationally infeasible).
  2. DECENTRALIZATION: No single point of control or failure.
  3. CONSENSUS: All nodes agree on the state of the ledger (using protocols like
     Proof of Work, Proof of Stake).
  4. TRANSPARENCY: All participants can verify transactions.

Applications in databases: Supply chain tracking, financial transactions
(cryptocurrency like Bitcoin, Ethereum), medical record management, digital
identity verification, smart contracts.


c. FEATURES OF GOOD RELATIONAL DESIGNS:

A good relational design minimizes redundancy and avoids anomalies:
  1. Each relation represents a single entity or relationship.
  2. Attributes depend on the key, the WHOLE key, and NOTHING but the key (3NF).
  3. Minimal redundancy — data stored only once.
  4. No insertion, update, or deletion anomalies.
  5. Proper normalization (at least 3NF for OLTP systems).
  6. Clear primary keys and well-defined foreign key relationships.
  7. Appropriate use of integrity constraints.
  8. Meaningful, consistent attribute naming conventions.


SET 4 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Recovery and Atomicity Using Log Records
b. Categories of NoSQL Databases
c. Graph-Based Protocols for Concurrency Control
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. RECOVERY AND ATOMICITY USING LOG RECORDS:

Atomicity requires that a transaction's effects are either ALL reflected in the
database or NONE at all. Log records ensure this by recording every change BEFORE
it is applied to the database (Write-Ahead Logging).

Log record format: <Tᵢ, X, Vold, Vnew>
  Tᵢ: transaction ID, X: data item, Vold: old value, Vnew: new value.

If a transaction commits: all its log records with new values can be used to
REDO its operations if they were not flushed to disk before a crash.

If a transaction aborts or the system crashes before commit: the log records
with old values are used to UNDO its operations, restoring the database to the
state before the transaction began.

The log itself must be written to STABLE STORAGE (non-volatile, reliable media)
before the data modifications are written — this is the WAL (Write-Ahead Logging)
protocol. This ensures that even if the system crashes, the log has enough
information for complete recovery.


b. CATEGORIES OF NoSQL DATABASES:

  1. KEY-VALUE STORES: Simplest model. Data stored as (key, value) pairs.
     O(1) lookup by key. Examples: Redis, Amazon DynamoDB, Riak.
     Use: Caching, session storage, user preferences.

  2. DOCUMENT STORES: Store semi-structured data as documents (JSON, BSON, XML).
     Each document can have different fields. Support queries on document content.
     Examples: MongoDB, CouchDB, Firebase Firestore.
     Use: Content management, catalogs, user profiles.

  3. COLUMN-FAMILY STORES: Data organized by columns rather than rows. Efficient
     for reading specific columns across many rows.
     Examples: Apache Cassandra, HBase, Google Bigtable.
     Use: Time-series data, IoT, analytics on large datasets.

  4. GRAPH DATABASES: Data modeled as nodes (entities) and edges (relationships).
     Optimized for traversing complex relationships.
     Examples: Neo4j, Amazon Neptune, ArangoDB.
     Use: Social networks, recommendation engines, fraud detection.


c. GRAPH-BASED PROTOCOLS FOR CONCURRENCY CONTROL:

Graph-based protocols restrict the ORDER in which transactions can lock data items,
using a partial ordering defined by a DIRECTED ACYCLIC GRAPH (DAG) on the data
items. The most common is the TREE PROTOCOL.

Tree Protocol rules:
  1. The first lock by a transaction may be on ANY data item.
  2. Subsequently, a data item Q can be locked ONLY IF the PARENT of Q (in the
     tree) is currently locked by the same transaction.
  3. Data items may be unlocked at any time (no two-phase requirement).
  4. A data item that has been locked and unlocked CANNOT be re-locked.

Advantages over 2PL:
  • Deadlock-free (no cycles possible in tree structure).
  • Can unlock items earlier than 2PL → higher concurrency.

Disadvantages:
  • May lock unnecessary items (a transaction must lock the parent before
    locking a child, even if it doesn't need the parent).
  • Not applicable to all database structures (requires a tree ordering).
  • Does not guarantee recoverability without additional protocol.


SET 5 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Redo and Undo Transactions Using Log
b. Distributed Databases and Their Advantages
c. Schema Diagrams in Relational Databases
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. REDO AND UNDO TRANSACTIONS USING LOG:

After a system crash, the recovery manager uses the log to restore consistency:

REDO (Roll Forward): For transactions that COMMITTED before the crash, their
changes may not have been written to disk (still in buffer). REDO re-applies
these changes by scanning the log FORWARD and writing the new values.

  If log has: <T₁, A, 100, 200> and <T₁, commit>
  Then: Set A = 200 on disk (even if it's already 200 — idempotent).

UNDO (Roll Back): For transactions that were ACTIVE (not committed) at crash
time, their partial changes must be reversed. UNDO scans the log BACKWARD and
restores old values.

  If log has: <T₂, B, 50, 75> but NO <T₂, commit>
  Then: Set B = 50 on disk (restore original value).

Recovery process: First REDO all committed transactions, then UNDO all
uncommitted ones. This ensures that committed work is preserved and uncommitted
work is erased — satisfying both durability and atomicity.


b. DISTRIBUTED DATABASES AND THEIR ADVANTAGES:

A distributed database is a collection of multiple, logically interrelated
databases distributed over a computer network. Each site has its own local DBMS
and can process local transactions independently.

Types:
  • Homogeneous: All sites use the same DBMS software.
  • Heterogeneous: Different sites may use different DBMS.

Advantages:
  1. IMPROVED RELIABILITY: If one site fails, others continue operating.
  2. DATA LOCALITY: Data stored close to where it's used → faster access.
  3. SCALABILITY: Adding new sites increases capacity.
  4. AUTONOMY: Each site can manage its own data independently.
  5. PARALLEL PROCESSING: Queries can be executed across multiple sites.

Challenges: Data replication consistency, distributed transactions (2-phase
commit protocol), network latency, security across sites, and the CAP theorem
(can only guarantee two of: Consistency, Availability, Partition tolerance).


c. SCHEMA DIAGRAMS IN RELATIONAL DATABASES:

A schema diagram is a graphical representation of the database schema showing
tables, their attributes, primary keys, and foreign key relationships. It
provides a bird's-eye view of the database structure.

Components:
  • Rectangles represent relations (tables).
  • Attributes are listed within each rectangle.
  • Primary keys are underlined or bolded.
  • Arrows from FK to PK show referential integrity constraints.

Schema diagrams differ from ER diagrams: schema diagrams show the actual
relational (table) structure after conversion from ER, while ER diagrams show
the conceptual design with entities, relationships, and cardinalities.

Tools for creating schema diagrams: pgAdmin, MySQL Workbench, dbdiagram.io,
draw.io.


SET 6 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Transaction Rollback and Recovery After System Crash
b. Cryptocurrency and Blockchain Properties
c. Two-Phase Locking Protocol
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. TRANSACTION ROLLBACK AND RECOVERY AFTER SYSTEM CRASH:

Rollback reverses the effects of an incomplete transaction. When a system crash
occurs, the recovery manager:
  1. Scans the log from the last checkpoint.
  2. Identifies committed transactions (have <commit> record) → REDO list.
  3. Identifies active transactions (have <start> but no <commit>) → UNDO list.
  4. REDO phase: scan forward, re-apply all changes from committed transactions.
  5. UNDO phase: scan backward, reverse all changes from active transactions
     by restoring old values.

After recovery, the database is in a consistent state: all committed work is
preserved, and all incomplete work is removed. The system can then resume normal
operation.


b. CRYPTOCURRENCY AND BLOCKCHAIN PROPERTIES:

Cryptocurrency is a digital currency that uses cryptographic techniques for
secure transactions. Built on blockchain technology, it operates without a
central authority (bank or government).

Key properties:
  1. DECENTRALIZATION: No central authority controls the currency.
  2. IMMUTABILITY: Transaction records cannot be altered once confirmed.
  3. TRANSPARENCY: All transactions are publicly visible on the blockchain.
  4. ANONYMITY/PSEUDONYMITY: Users identified by cryptographic addresses.
  5. LIMITED SUPPLY: Most cryptocurrencies have a maximum supply (Bitcoin: 21M).
  6. CONSENSUS: Transactions validated through consensus mechanisms (Proof of
     Work, Proof of Stake).

Examples: Bitcoin, Ethereum, Litecoin. Smart contracts (Ethereum) allow
programmable transactions that execute automatically when conditions are met.


c. TWO-PHASE LOCKING PROTOCOL:

2PL divides a transaction into two phases:
  • GROWING PHASE: Acquire locks, release none.
  • SHRINKING PHASE: Release locks, acquire none.

The lock point (when all locks are acquired) determines the serial order.
2PL guarantees conflict serializability.

Variants:
  • Basic 2PL: May cause cascading rollback.
  • Strict 2PL: X-locks held until commit → prevents cascading rollback.
  • Rigorous 2PL: ALL locks held until commit → simplest, strictest.

Limitation: 2PL can cause DEADLOCKS (two transactions waiting for each other's
locks). Deadlocks are resolved by detection (wait-for graph) or prevention
(wait-die, wound-wait schemes).


SET 7 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. High Availability Using Remote Backup System
b. Optimizing Commit Processing
c. Characteristics and Advantages of NoSQL Databases
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. HIGH AVAILABILITY USING REMOTE BACKUP SYSTEM:

A remote backup system (hot standby) ensures high availability by maintaining a
REPLICA of the database at a geographically separate site. The primary site
processes transactions normally, and log records are continuously shipped to the
remote backup site.

How it works:
  1. Primary site processes all transactions and writes log records.
  2. Log records are sent to the remote site over the network.
  3. Remote site applies log records (REDO) to keep its copy up to date.
  4. If the primary site fails, the remote site TAKES OVER as the new primary.

The time to switch (failover) depends on how up-to-date the backup is. If log
records are sent in real-time (synchronous replication), data loss is zero but
performance may suffer. If sent periodically (asynchronous), some recent
transactions may be lost.

This provides protection against catastrophic failures (fire, earthquake, etc.)
that could destroy the primary site.


b. OPTIMIZING COMMIT PROCESSING:

Commit processing can be optimized to reduce the overhead of writing to stable
storage:

1. GROUP COMMIT: Instead of flushing the log for each individual transaction
   commit, the system waits briefly and flushes the log for MULTIPLE transactions
   at once. This amortizes the I/O cost across many commits.

2. NON-VOLATILE WRITE BUFFERS (NV-RAM): Using battery-backed RAM or solid-state
   storage as a write buffer. Data written to NV-RAM is considered durable,
   eliminating the need for synchronous disk writes.

3. EARLY LOCK RELEASE: In certain protocols, locks can be released before the
   log is fully flushed, improving concurrency.

4. PARALLEL LOG WRITING: Using multiple disks or RAID configurations to write
   log records in parallel, reducing I/O bottleneck.


c. CHARACTERISTICS AND ADVANTAGES OF NoSQL DATABASES:

Characteristics: Schema-less design, horizontal scalability, high availability
through replication, support for unstructured/semi-structured data, eventual
consistency (BASE model), and diverse data models (document, key-value, column-
family, graph).

Advantages over RDBMS:
  1. Handles massive data volumes (petabytes) efficiently.
  2. Scales horizontally by adding commodity servers.
  3. Flexible schema adapts to changing requirements without migration.
  4. High throughput for read/write-heavy workloads.
  5. Built for distributed environments and cloud deployment.
  6. Better suited for real-time applications (gaming, IoT, social media).


SET 8 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Redo and Undo Operations in Recovery
b. View Serializability
c. Distributed Databases and Data Replication
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. REDO AND UNDO OPERATIONS IN RECOVERY:

After a crash, the recovery manager classifies transactions:
  • Committed transactions → REDO list (ensure changes are on disk).
  • Active (uncommitted) transactions → UNDO list (reverse changes).

REDO: Scan log forward from last checkpoint. For each <Tᵢ, X, Vold, Vnew> where
Tᵢ is in the redo list, set X = Vnew. This is idempotent — can be applied
multiple times without harm.

UNDO: Scan log backward. For each <Tᵢ, X, Vold, Vnew> where Tᵢ is in the undo
list, set X = Vold. Write a COMPENSATION LOG RECORD (CLR) for each undo operation
to prevent repeated undoing if there's another crash during recovery.

Order: REDO first (to reconstruct committed state), then UNDO (to remove
uncommitted changes). This is the ARIES algorithm approach.


b. VIEW SERIALIZABILITY:

A schedule S₁ is VIEW EQUIVALENT to schedule S₂ if:
  1. Same initial reads: If Tᵢ reads initial value of Q in S₁, it does in S₂.
  2. Same read-from: If Tᵢ reads Q written by Tⱼ in S₁, same in S₂.
  3. Same final write: The last write on Q in S₁ is by the same transaction in S₂.

A schedule is VIEW SERIALIZABLE if it is view equivalent to some serial schedule.

View serializability is a SUPERSET of conflict serializability — every conflict-
serializable schedule is view-serializable, but some view-serializable schedules
are NOT conflict-serializable (specifically, those involving blind writes).

Testing view serializability is NP-COMPLETE, making it impractical for real-time
use. Conflict serializability (polynomial-time testing via precedence graph) is
preferred in practice.


c. DISTRIBUTED DATABASES AND DATA REPLICATION:

Data replication maintains copies of the same data at multiple sites in a
distributed database. Types of replication:

1. FULL REPLICATION: Every site has a complete copy of the database.
   • Best read performance (all queries are local).
   • Worst write performance (every write must update ALL copies).

2. PARTIAL REPLICATION: Some frequently accessed data is replicated.
   • Balances read performance and write overhead.

3. NO REPLICATION (Fragmentation only): Each data item at exactly one site.
   • Best write performance, worst availability.

Replication consistency strategies:
  • Synchronous: All replicas updated in the same transaction (strong consistency
    but slow).
  • Asynchronous: Primary updated first, replicas updated later (faster but may
    have temporary inconsistencies).

Benefits: Improved availability, fault tolerance, reduced network latency.
Challenges: Keeping replicas consistent, conflict resolution, increased storage.


SET 9 ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Transaction Model and State Diagram
b. Object-Relational Model
c. Concurrency Control and Recovery
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. TRANSACTION MODEL AND STATE DIAGRAM:

A transaction is a logical unit of work consisting of one or more database
operations that must be executed atomically. The transaction model defines five
states: ACTIVE (executing), PARTIALLY COMMITTED (last statement executed but not
yet persisted), COMMITTED (changes permanent), FAILED (error encountered), and
ABORTED (changes rolled back).

  Active → Partially Committed → Committed
  Active → Failed → Aborted (→ restart or kill)
  Partially Committed → Failed (if flush fails)

Operations: BEGIN TRANSACTION, COMMIT (make permanent), ROLLBACK (undo all),
SAVEPOINT (partial rollback point).


b. OBJECT-RELATIONAL MODEL:

The Object-Relational Model extends the traditional relational model with
object-oriented features, combining the strengths of both:

  1. COMPLEX DATA TYPES: User-defined types (UDTs), arrays, multisets.
     CREATE TYPE Address AS (street VARCHAR, city VARCHAR, zip INT);

  2. INHERITANCE: Table inheritance where a child table inherits columns from
     a parent.
     CREATE TABLE graduate_student UNDER student (thesis_title VARCHAR);

  3. METHODS: Functions associated with types.

  4. REFERENCES: Object identifiers and references between objects.

  5. NESTED TABLES: Tables within tables (collections as column values).

PostgreSQL is the most prominent object-relational DBMS, supporting arrays,
JSON, composite types, inheritance, and user-defined types and functions.

The object-relational model bridges the gap between pure relational (SQL) and
pure object-oriented databases while maintaining SQL compatibility.


c. CONCURRENCY CONTROL AND RECOVERY:

Concurrency control and recovery are the two pillars of the transaction
management component of a DBMS.

Concurrency control ensures that multiple transactions executing simultaneously
do not interfere with each other. Mechanisms: lock-based protocols (2PL, S/X
locks), timestamp-based protocols, and multiversion concurrency control (MVCC).
The goal is to produce schedules that are serializable (equivalent to some serial
execution).

Recovery ensures that the database can be restored to a consistent state after
failures. Mechanisms: write-ahead logging (WAL), REDO/UNDO operations,
checkpoints, and remote backups. The goal is to maintain atomicity (undo
incomplete transactions) and durability (preserve committed transactions).

Together, they enforce the ACID properties: Atomicity (recovery), Consistency
(both), Isolation (concurrency control), Durability (recovery).


SET 10 ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
a. Graph-Based Protocols in Concurrency Control
b. Blockchain and Its Role in Distributed Databases
c. Lock-Based Protocols and Their Types
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

a. GRAPH-BASED PROTOCOLS IN CONCURRENCY CONTROL:

Graph-based protocols impose a PARTIAL ORDER on data items using a directed
acyclic graph (DAG). The most common is the Tree Protocol, where data items are
organized in a tree structure.

Rules of the Tree Protocol:
  1. First lock can be on any data item.
  2. Subsequent locks only on items whose PARENT is currently locked by the
     same transaction.
  3. Items can be unlocked at any time.
  4. Once unlocked, an item cannot be re-locked by the same transaction.

Advantage: DEADLOCK-FREE (no circular waits possible in a tree). Items can be
unlocked earlier than 2PL, allowing higher concurrency.

Disadvantage: Transactions may need to lock unnecessary parent items to reach
the desired child. Only works when data has a natural tree ordering (e.g., B⁺
tree index nodes).


b. BLOCKCHAIN AND ITS ROLE IN DISTRIBUTED DATABASES:

Blockchain is a distributed, immutable ledger technology where data is stored in
cryptographically linked blocks. Each block contains transactions, a hash of the
previous block, and a timestamp. This chain structure ensures that tampering with
any block invalidates all subsequent blocks.

Role in distributed databases:
  1. IMMUTABLE AUDIT TRAIL: Once recorded, data cannot be altered — ideal for
     regulatory compliance, financial records, and supply chain tracking.
  2. DECENTRALIZED TRUST: No need for a central authority — consensus protocols
     ensure agreement among all participants.
  3. SMART CONTRACTS: Self-executing code on the blockchain that automates
     business logic (e.g., automatic payment when goods are delivered).
  4. DATA INTEGRITY: Cryptographic hashing ensures data has not been tampered with.
  5. TRANSPARENCY: All participants can verify the state of the database.

Limitations: Low throughput compared to traditional databases, high storage
requirements (full copy at every node), energy consumption (Proof of Work),
and difficulty in updating/deleting data (by design).


c. LOCK-BASED PROTOCOLS AND THEIR TYPES:

Lock-based protocols control concurrent access by requiring transactions to
obtain LOCKS before accessing data items. A lock is a mechanism that restricts
access to a data item.

Types of locks:
  1. SHARED LOCK (S): For reading. Multiple transactions can hold S-locks on the
     same item simultaneously.
  2. EXCLUSIVE LOCK (X): For writing. Only one transaction can hold an X-lock,
     and no other lock (S or X) can coexist.

Compatibility: S-S: ✓, S-X: ✗, X-X: ✗.

Protocols:
  • SIMPLE LOCKING: Lock before access, unlock after. Does NOT guarantee
    serializability.
  • TWO-PHASE LOCKING (2PL): Growing phase (acquire locks) then shrinking phase
    (release locks). Guarantees serializability.
  • STRICT 2PL: X-locks held until commit. Prevents cascading rollback.
  • RIGOROUS 2PL: ALL locks held until commit. Most restrictive but simplest.

Lock granularity: Row-level (fine, high concurrency), page-level, table-level
(coarse, low overhead). Lock escalation: automatically upgrade from row to table
lock when too many row locks are held.
